Semiring Provenance for Lightweight Description Logics
Camille Bourgaux camille.bourgaux@ens.fr, Ana Ozaki ana.ozaki@uib.no, Rafael Peñaloza rafael.penaloza@unimib.it

Abstract
We investigate semiring provenance—a successful framework originally defined in the relational database setting—for description logics. In this context, the ontology axioms are annotated with elements of a commutative semiring and these annotations are propagated to the ontology consequences in a way that reflects how they are derived. We define a provenance semantics for a language that encompasses several lightweight description logics and show its relationships with semantics that have been defined for ontologies annotated with a specific kind of annotation (such as fuzzy degrees). We show that under some restrictions on the semiring, the semantics satisfies desirable properties (such as extending the semiring provenance defined for databases). We then focus on the well-known why-provenance, for which we study the complexity of problems related to the provenance of an assertion or a conjunctive query answer. Finally, we consider two more restricted cases which correspond to the so-called positive Boolean provenance and lineage in the database setting. For these cases, we present relationships with well-known notions related to explanations in description logics and complete our complexity analysis. As a side contribution, we provide conditions on an ℰ ​ ℒℋℐ ⊥ {\cal E\!LHI}_{\bot} ontology that guarantee tractable reasoning.



1
Introduction

Description logics (DLs) are a well-known family of formalisms, typically equivalent to fragments of first-order logic, in which conceptual knowledge about a particular domain
and facts about specific individuals are expressed in an ontology, using unary and binary predicates called concepts and
roles (?).
Important reasoning tasks performed over DL ontologies are axiom entailment, i.e. deciding whether a given DL axiom follows from the ontology; and query answering, focussing in particular on database-style conjunctive queries.
Since scalability of reasoning methods is crucial when using large ontologies, DLs with favorable computational properties have been investigated.
In particular, the DL-Lite and
{\cal E\!L}
families propose many dialects that allow for axiom entailment in polynomial time, and Boolean conjunctive query (BCQ) entailment in
NP
(?, ?, ?). Many real-world ontologies use languages from these families, which underlie the OWL 2 QL and OWL 2 EL profiles of the Semantic Web standard ontology language (?).

Several extensions of DLs go beyond simple axiom or query entailment and enrich the results with additional information. One can mention fuzzy DLs to express vagueness (?, ?), possibilistic DLs to handle uncertainty (?, ?, ?), or the bag semantics, which associates multiplicities to query results (?, ?).
Another kind of information that may be expected to accompany reasoning results is an
explanation
. It may indeed be crucial to know how a consequence—e.g. an axiom or a query answer—has been derived from the ontology.
In DLs, this problem has been studied mostly focusing on explaining axiom entailment, in particular concept subsumption, through
axiom pinpointing
, which consists in finding one or all minimal subsets of the ontology that entail the consequence, called
justifications
(?, ?, ?).
A similar approach was investigated for explaining ontology-mediated query answers, but focussing only on the minimal subsets of facts which together with the conceptual knowledge entail the query
(?, ?).
Alternative approaches that consider the whole ontology provide more involved proof-based explanations for BCQ entailment
(?, ?, ?). Investigation of proofs for axiom entailment that go beyond axiom pinpoiting is also an active line of research (?, ?).

Within the context of databases, the framework of
semiring provenance
(?, ?) generalizes the semantics of queries over databases annotated with different kinds of annotations, such as multiplicities, trust levels,
costs, clearance levels, etc.
Indeed, it defines the semantics of positive relational algebra queries over databases annotated by elements of an arbitrary commutative semiring, which is an algebraic structure with two binary operators called the
addition
and the
multiplication
.
Intuitively, joint use of the data corresponds to the semiring multiplication while alternative use of the data corresponds to the semiring addition.
The semantics is inductively defined on the structure of the query, in a similar way as the semantics of such queries over non-annotated databases.

The name “provenance” refers to the original idea of tracing the origin of a query answer.
Indeed, when applied to databases whose tuples are annotated with identifiers (called variables, or provenance tokens), the semiring provenance framework allows to capture many notions of provenance or explanation that have been considered in the database community for nearly 30 years, such as lineage or why-provenance (see, e.g. surveys and discussion papers by ? (?), ? (?), ? (?), and ? (?)). This is done by introducing so-called
provenance semirings

(?, ?). Provenance semiring elements are expressions built from variables associated with each tuple of the database. Depending on the provenance semiring used, these elements may be, for instance, polynomials with coefficients from
\mathbb{N}
, polynomials with Boolean coefficients, etc.
Such a provenance expression provides a representation of how tuples have been used to derive a query result.
An important feature of the semiring provenance framework is that a provenance expression can be used to compute the annotation that would be associated to the query result if the database was annotated by elements of any commutative semiring in which the considered provenance semiring can be homomorphically embedded (for example, if a query result has provenance
x\times y+z
, one can obtain the multiplicity of this result in the database in which the tuple with identifier
x
is annotated by
2
, the one with identifier
y
by
3
, and the one with identifier
z
by
4
, by replacing the variables by these values and evaluating the resulting expression in the semiring of the natural integers, i.e.,
2\times 3+4=10
).

Semiring provenance has also been studied for Datalog queries, for which it is defined based on the set of all derivation trees for the query (?, ?, ?),
and has drawn interest beyond relational databases,
notably in the context of the Semantic Web (?, ?, ?, ?, ?), but also
XML (?),
graph databases (?, ?), and
expressive logics (?, ?, ?).

In this work, we investigate semiring provenance for description logic ontologies.
An important feature that distinguishes DL
ontologies from the relational database setting is that not only facts but also axioms expressing
conceptual knowledge
can be annotated
and, thus,
taken into account in the computation of the provenance.
We also consider not only query answering—as
done for Datalog queries, which can be seen as ontologies—but also axiom entailment, such as concept subsumption, which is
a classical reasoning task for DL.
There have already been several proposals to use some kinds of semiring provenance with description logic ontologies (?, ?, ?, ?, ?), with different goals and semantics. We refer to Section
8
for a detailed comparison between these proposals and the one we introduce in this paper.

Our main goal is to define a semantics for ontologies annotated with elements of an arbitrary commutative semiring, which distinguishes us from ? (?), ? (?), and ? (?) who consider ontologies annotated with variables only.
Our semantics should satisfy some desirable properties (such as being consistent with the semiring provenance defined in the relational database context, or allowing to evaluate a provenance expression in another semiring and obtain the correct value), at least under some restrictions on the semiring (such as idempotency of the operations).
This distinguishes us from ? (?) and ? (?), whose notions of provenance do not coincide with the one defined for relational database, even if the semiring is fully idempotent. Indeed, ? (?) define the provenance values that an assertion can have in (finite) models but are not concerned with defining a single provenance value from all possible models, and if we extend the semantics defined by ? (?) (who focuses on concept inclusions) to assertions and consider a set of annotated assertions, all annotations will take part in the provenance value of a consequence, even if the assertions they annotate are irrelevant (cf. Section
8
).
Our semantics should also be defined independently from any specific reasoning algorithm, as the usual model-theoretic classical DL semantics (in the same way that the semantics of annotated databases is independent from how the (Datalog) query is evaluated).
We are also interested in the computational complexity of our framework, and conduct a preliminary complexity analysis focusing on some relevant cases (provenance semirings such that at least some of the desirable properties we consider are satisfied).
The rest of this work is organised as follows.

•

In Section
2
, we provide the useful background on description logics, semirings, and semiring provenance in the context of databases.
The DL language we consider in this paper is a syntactic restriction of
{\cal E\!LHI}_{\bot}
, denoted by
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
, which encompasses several dialects of the
DL-Lite
and
{\cal E\!L}
families.
We also introduce the
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
fragment of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
and state that in this language, satisfiability and axiom entailment are
PTime
-complete and BCQ entailment is
NP
-complete (Theorem
2.2
). This fragment is a side contribution of the paper, since it provides some insights on the complexity of reasoning with
{\cal E\!LHI}_{\bot}
ontologies.

•

In Section
3
, we define a semiring provenance semantics for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
(Section
3.1.1
) and explain our design choices and the restrictions of our setting (Sections
3.1.3
and
3.1.4
). Specifically, we defined the semantics of entailment of annotated axioms and BCQs from an annotated ontology, as well as the provenance of an axiom or a BCQ w.r.t. an annotated ontology. We show that satisfiability of an ontology does not depend on the annotations (Lemma
3.5
) and that computing the provenance of a rooted tree-shaped BCQ can be reduced to computing the provenance of a concept assertion (Theorem
3.11
). We then investigate whether the semantics captures some well-known semantics for ontologies annotated with specific kinds of information. In particular, we show that under some conditions, it captures the Zadeh semantics of fuzzy DLs as well as a possibilistic semantics (Proposition
3.12
), and a notion of boundary that has been defined in the context of access control (Proposition
3.15
), but does not capture the product-based possibilistic semantics nor the bag semantics.

•

In Section
4
, we show that under some conditions, some classical results for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontologies can be transferred to annotated ontologies.

–

We show that an annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology can be put in normal form while preserving the provenance of its consequences (Theorem
4.1
).

–

Given a satisfiable annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology, we define a canonical model which satisfies exactly the annotated assertions and BCQs that are entailed by the ontology (Theorem
4.5
).
We also define canonical models of the ontology and a concept
C
(resp. role
P
) satisfiable w.r.t. the ontology. They allow us to check whether the ontology entails an annotated concept inclusion between basic concepts (resp. role inclusion) with
C
(resp.
P
) in the left-hand side, under the condition that the semiring is multiplicatively idempotent and some additional conditions on the ontology in the concept case (Theorems
4.7
and
4.9
).

–

We show that the problems of deciding entailment of an annotated concept assertion and of deciding entailment of an annotated concept inclusion are mutually reducible in polynomial time if the semiring is multiplicatively idempotent (under some conditions on the ontology) (Theorem
4.10
), and that a similar result holds for role assertions and role inclusions even if the semiring is not idempotent (Theorem
4.11
).

•

In Section
5
, we show that our provenance semantics satisfies some desirable properties when restricted to commutative semirings that are additively and multiplicatively idempotent and discuss the problems that arise beyond this setting.

–

We show that when the semiring is positive, an axiom or a BCQ has a non-zero provenance iff it is entailed by the non-annotated version of the ontology
(with the additional condition that the semiring is multiplicatively idempotent in the case of a concept inclusion) (Theorem
5.1
).

–

We show that if the semiring is additively idempotent, our notion of provenance is consistent with the one defined for relational algebra queries or Datalog queries over annotated databases. This is in the sense that computing the provenance of a query in our setting can be reduced to computing the provenance of a (Datalog) query as defined by ? (?) in the following cases:
for a BCQ if the ontology contains only annotated assertions (Theorem
5.4
);
for a concept assertion query
A(a)
and an ontology consisting of annotated assertions and non-recursive concept inclusions with concept
A
as right-hand side (Theorem
5.6
);
and for a BCQ and an ontology that does not have existential role restrictions on the right nor role disjointness (that is, it is equivalent to a Datalog program) (Theorem
5.8
).
We also show that our notion of provenance coincides with an alternative provenance semantics that has been defined for Datalog even if the semiring is not idempotent (Lemma
5.11
).

–

We show that if a semiring can be homomorphically embedded in another one, one can obtain the provenance value of an axiom or a BCQ w.r.t. the ontology in the second semiring from the provenance value in the first one if the semirings are additively idempotent (and multiplicatively idempotent in the case of a concept inclusion, under some conditions on the ontology) (Theorem
5.12
).

•

In Section
6
, we focus on the problems of deciding annotated entailment and computing provenance using
the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
provenance semiring that corresponds to the well-known why-provenance in the database setting.
Since the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
semiring is additively idempotent but not multiplicatively idempotent, which yields some undesirable behavior in the case of concept inclusion entailment, we focus on assertion and BCQ entailment. We provide algorithms and complexity results for computing the provenance of assertions and BCQs in this semiring for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
and
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
.

–

We present a completion algorithm that derives in exponential time all annotated assertions that are entailed by a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology (Theorem
6.4
), which allows us to compute the provenance of assertions in exponential time (Corollary
6.7
).

–

For the case of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
​, we adapt the completion algorithm so that the exponential blow-up comes only from the annotations (Theorem
6.11
), which gives an upper bound for deciding annotated assertion entailment exponential in the size of the annotation but polynomial in the size of the ontology (Corollary
6.12
). We then improve this complexity upper bound to a
PSpace
one (Theorem
6.14
).

–

For conjunctive queries, we present an algorithm based on an annotation-aware rewriting of the query using the completion of the ontology, and obtain exponential complexity upper bounds for the problem of deciding annotated BCQ entailment and of computing the provenance of a BCQ in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
(Theorem
6.17
and Corollaries
6.18
and
6.19
) and an
NP
upper bound for the problem of deciding annotated BCQ entailment in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
if the annotation size is fixed (Theorem
6.20
).

•

In Section
7
, we consider two cases that add further conditions on the provenance semiring and correspond to the so-called positive Boolean provenance and lineage in the database setting.
Since the considered semirings
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
are additively and multiplicatively idempotent, provenance in these semirings satisfies all properties studied in Section
5
. Hence we consider all kinds of axiom in this section.
We exhibit relationships with well-known notions related to explanations in DLs and also provide algorithms and complexity results for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
and
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
.

–

We show the relationships between annotated entailments in
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
,
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
, and that the provenance in
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
can be obtained from the provenance in
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
(Propositions
7.1
and
7.3
).

–

We show that, under some conditions, provenance in the
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
semiring can be computed via the set of justifications (Proposition
7.4
). One can thus take advantage of the large body of work on DL axiom pinpointing.

–

We show that provenance in the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
semiring can be computed in polynomial time for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
ontologies (and in exponential time for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
) (Theorem
7.8
). This is done via an adaptation of the completion algorithm of Section
6
(Theorem
7.7
).

•

In Section
8
, we review the other frameworks that use some form of semiring provenance for DLs and compare them with ours.

•

In Section
9
, we conclude with a discussion of some of our results and evoke possible future work.

This paper is based on previous work by ? (?) and ? (?). In particular, it adapts several ideas, examples or algorithms given by ? (?) (cf. Section
8
for details).
? (?) consider provenance for ontology-based data access, a setting where a database is enriched with (i) a DL-Lite
R
ontology and (ii) mappings between
the database and the ontology, and ? (?) consider axiom entailment and query answering in
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
.
In these two papers, the ontology axioms are annotated with provenance variables, and consequences of the ontology (axioms or queries) are annotated with provenance polynomials expressing their provenance information.
However, although these polynomials can be used as explanations, these papers do not define the semantics of an ontology annotated by elements of an arbitrary commutative semiring and do not investigate either whether evaluating these polynomials in some semiring would capture some of the existing semantics for annotated ontologies (such as the fuzzy semantics or the bag semantics). They also do not consider any of the questions we address in Section
5
.

For the ease of reading, we chose to delegate most of the proofs (which may be long and technical but mostly rely on classical techniques) to the appendix.

2
Preliminaries

This section recalls the syntax and semantics of description logics and the framework of semiring provenance in the
context of databases.

2.1
Description Logics and the
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
Language

Our main focus is on a syntactic restriction of the DL language
{\cal E\!LHI}_{\bot}
that we call
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
(where
n
stands for “normalized”),
which encompasses several dialects of the well-known
{\cal E\!L}
(?) and
DL-Lite
(?) families of
lightweight DLs.

Syntax.

Let
{\sf N_{C}}
,
{\sf N_{R}}
and
{\sf N_{I}}
be three mutually disjoint countable sets of

concept-
,
role-
, and
individual names
, respectively.
Given a DL language
\mathcal{L}
, an
\mathcal{L}

ontology

\mathcal{O}
is a finite set of
axioms
whose form depends on
\mathcal{L}
.
We consider the following kinds of axioms.

•

Concept and role
assertions
are of the form
A(a)

and
R(a,b)
respectively, with
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
,
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
, and
a,b\in{\sf N_{I}}
.

•

General concept inclusions
(GCIs) are expressions of the form
C\sqsubseteq D
, where
C
and
D
are
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concepts

built according to the grammar rules:

C::=A\mid\exists P.C\mid C\sqcap C\mid\top\quad\quad\quad D::=A\mid\exists P.\top\mid\bot\quad\quad\quad P::=R\mid R^{-}

where
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
,
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
. We often use
\exists P
as a shorthand for
\exists P.\top
, and we call
basic concepts
the concepts of the form
A
or
\exists P
.

•

Positive
role inclusions
(RIs) are of the form
P\sqsubseteq Q
with
P,Q::=R\mid R^{-}
for
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
and

negative role inclusions
are of the form
P\sqcap Q\sqsubseteq\bot
.
1
1

1

{\cal E\!LHI}_{\bot}
normally does not allow negative RIs. We add them so that
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
encompasses DL-Lite
R
,
which allows for axioms of the form
P\sqsubseteq\neg Q
(?), which can be equivalently
expressed as
P\sqcap Q\sqsubseteq\bot
.

An
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}

ontology
is a finite set of axioms of any of the available forms.
A
DL-Lite
R
ontology
may contain assertions, positive and negative role inclusions, and GCIs of one of the
following restricted forms:
D_{1}\sqsubseteq D_{2}
or
D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}\sqsubseteq\bot
where
D_{1}
and
D_{2}

are

basic concepts
.
An
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}

ontology
may contain all the considered axioms with the restrictions that
(i)
\bot
does not occur in the ontology
and (ii) there is no inverse role (i.e.
P::=R
),
except for
range restrictions
of the form
\exists R^{-}\sqsubseteq A
.

In the standard DL literature, languages from the
{\cal E\!L}
family usually allow for GCIs of the general form

C_{1}\sqsubseteq C_{2}
with
C_{1},C_{2}
constructed as in the grammar rule for
C
introduced above.
A set of such GCIs
\mathcal{T}
can be translated into a conservative extension
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{T}
in our syntax in polynomial time
by exhaustively applying the following
rules:

•

replace
C\sqsubseteq C_{1}\sqcap C_{2}

by
C\sqsubseteq C_{1}
and
C\sqsubseteq C_{2}
,

•

replace
C_{1}\sqsubseteq\exists P.C_{2}

by
C_{1}\sqsubseteq\exists S
,
S\sqsubseteq P
and
\exists S^{-}\sqsubseteq C_{2}

where
S
is a fresh role name.

The reason for this syntactic restriction
is that conjunctions or qualified restrictions of a role on the right-hand side
of GCIs lead to counter-intuitive behavior when dealing with provenance.
We discuss this issue in further detail in Section
3.1.4
.

Given an ontology
\mathcal{O}
, we denote by
\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})
the
signature
of
\mathcal{O}
; that is, the set of concept and role names
that occur in
\mathcal{O}
; and by
\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
the set of individual names that occur in
\mathcal{O}
. For
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
, we let

\mathsf{inv}(R)=R^{-}
and
\mathsf{inv}(R^{-})=R
.

Semantics.

The semantics of DL languages is defined through
interpretations
, in the spirit of first-order
logic.
An interpretation is a pair
\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},\cdot^{\mathcal{I}})

where
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
is a non-empty set (called the
domain
of
\mathcal{I}
),
and
\cdot^{\mathcal{I}}
is the
interpretation function
, which maps every
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
to
a^{\mathcal{I}}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
;
every
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
to
A^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
;
and every
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
to
R^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.

The interpretation function

\cdot^{\mathcal{I}}
is extended to complex expressions as follows:

\displaystyle(\top)^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}};

\displaystyle(\bot)^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\emptyset;

\displaystyle(R^{-})^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\{(e,d)\mid(d,e)\in R^{\mathcal{I}}\};

\displaystyle(\exists P.C)^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\{d\mid\exists e\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\text{ s.t. }(d,e)\in P^{\mathcal{I}}\};

\displaystyle(C\sqcap D)^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle C^{\mathcal{I}}\cap D^{\mathcal{I}}.

The interpretation
\mathcal{I}

satisfies
the concept assertion
A(a)
if
a^{\mathcal{I}}\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
;
the role assertion
R(a,b)
if
(a^{\mathcal{I}},b^{\mathcal{I}})\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
;
the GCI
C\sqsubseteq D
if
C^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq D^{\mathcal{I}}
;
the positive RI
P\sqsubseteq Q
if
P^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq Q^{\mathcal{I}}
; and
the negative RI
P\sqcap Q\sqsubseteq\bot
if
P^{\mathcal{I}}\cap Q^{\mathcal{I}}=\emptyset
.
The satisfaction of an axiom
\alpha
by
\mathcal{I}
is denoted
\mathcal{I}\models\alpha
.
The interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is a
model
of the ontology
\mathcal{O}
, denoted by
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}
, iff

\mathcal{I}\models\alpha
for every
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}
.
Finally,
\mathcal{O}

entails
an axiom
\alpha
if
\mathcal{I}\models\alpha
for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
.

Queries.

A
conjunctive query
(CQ) is an existentially quantified first-order formula of the form

\exists\vec{y}\,\phi(\vec{x},\vec{y})
where
\phi(\vec{x},\vec{y})
is a conjunction of atoms of the form
A(t)
or
R(t,t^{\prime})
with
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
,
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
, and terms
t,t^{\prime}\in{\sf N_{I}}\cup\vec{x}\cup\vec{y}
. We denote by
\mathsf{atoms}(q)
and
\mathsf{terms}(q)
the sets of atoms and terms of a query
q
. A CQ consisting of a single atom is called an
atomic query
.
A
union of conjunctive queries
(UCQ) is a finite disjunction of CQs (over the same free variables). A query is
Boolean
if it has no free variables.
An interpretation
\mathcal{I}
satisfies a Boolean CQ (BCQ)
q:=\exists\vec{y}\,\phi(\vec{y})
, written
\mathcal{I}\models q
, iff there is a
match for
q
in
\mathcal{I}
, where a match for
q
in
\mathcal{I}
is a function
\pi:\mathsf{terms}(q)\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
\pi(t)=t^{\mathcal{I}}
for every
t\in{\sf N_{I}}\cap\mathsf{terms}(q)
, and for every
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{terms}(q)
:

•

\pi(t)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
for every
A(t)\in\mathsf{atoms}(q)
, and

•

(\pi(t),\pi(t^{\prime}))\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
for every
R(t,t^{\prime})\in\mathsf{atoms}(q)
.

A BCQ
q
is
entailed
by an ontology
\mathcal{O}
, written
\mathcal{O}\models q
, if and only if
\mathcal{I}\models q
for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
.
A tuple of constants
\vec{a}
is an
answer
to a CQ
q(\vec{x}):=\exists\vec{y}\,\phi(\vec{x},\vec{y})
over
\mathcal{O}
if
\vec{a}
and
\vec{x}
have the same length and
\mathcal{O}\models q(\vec{a})
where
q(\vec{a})
is the BCQ obtained by replacing the variables from
\vec{x}
with the corresponding constants from
\vec{a}
.

We will sometimes use
rooted tree-shaped queries
. Such a query is a CQ
q
such that:

•

\mathsf{terms}(q)
contains exactly one constant or free variable, all other terms being existentially quantified variables,

•

the undirected graph
\{(x,y)\mid R(x,y)\in\mathsf{atoms}(q)\}
is a tree whose root is a constant or a free variable,

•

for every
x,y\in\mathsf{terms}(q)
, there is at most one role name
R
such that
R(x,y)\in\mathsf{atoms}(q)
or
R(y,x)\in\mathsf{atoms}(q)
,
and only one of these is the case.

It is well-known that there is a
correspondence between
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concepts and rooted tree-shaped queries (see, e.g., ?, page 2):
given an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
C
, there is a rooted tree-shaped query
q_{C}(x)
that retrieves all instances of
C
and
can be built by structural induction on
C
; and given a rooted tree-shaped query
q(x)
, there is an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
C_{q}
such that
q(x)
retrieves all instances of
C_{q}
and
C_{q}
can be build by structural induction on the tree-structure of
q(x)
. For example, the
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
A\sqcap\exists R.(B\sqcap\exists S^{-}.D)
corresponds to the CQ
q(x)=\exists yz\,A(x)\wedge R(x,y)\wedge B(y)\wedge S(z,y)\wedge D(z)
.

-complete

-complete

R

in

Table 1:
Combined complexity of axiom and BCQ entailment in different DL languages.

Complexity.

Given an ontology
\mathcal{O}
(a BCQ
q
, or an axiom
\alpha
), we denote by
|\mathcal{O}|
(
|q|
, or
|\alpha|
,
respectively) and call the
size
of
\mathcal{O}
(
q
,
\alpha
, resp.) the length of the string representing
\mathcal{O}

(
q
,
\alpha
, resp.), where elements of
{\sf N_{C}}
,
{\sf N_{R}}
,
{\sf N_{I}}
and variables are considered of length one.
The complexity of axiom entailment and BCQ entailment in the different
languages we consider is summarised in Table
1
.
These results refer only to the
combined complexity
, where
everything
is part of the input and measured accordingly (i.e. the input size is
|\mathcal{O}|+|\alpha|
in the case of axiom entailment,
|\mathcal{O}|+|q|
in the case of BCQ entailment).

The
\boldsymbol{{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}}
fragment of
\boldsymbol{{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}}

To discuss lightweight DLs such as
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
and
DL-Lite
R
, we
define a fragment of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
which extends them and shares their good computational properties.
An
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}
is in
normal form
if all its GCIs are of the form

A\sqsubseteq B,\ A\sqcap A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq B,\ A\sqsubseteq\exists R,\ A\sqsubseteq\exists R^{-},\ \exists R.A\sqsubseteq B,\text{ or }\exists R^{-}.A\sqsubseteq B

with
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
,
A,A^{\prime}\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
,
B\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\bot\}
. Every
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}
can be translated in linear time into an ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
in normal form which is a conservative extension of
\mathcal{O}
(cf. Section
4.1
for the normalization process in the more general case where the ontology can be annotated). We define a binary relation
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{O}}
over
\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})\cap{\sf N_{R}}\cup\{R^{-}\mid R\in\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})\cap{\sf N_{R}}\}
as the transitive closure of the relation defined by

\{(S,P),(\mathsf{inv}(S),\mathsf{inv}(P))\mid S\sqsubseteq P\in\mathcal{O}\}\cup\{(R,R),(R^{-},R^{-})\mid R\in\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})\cap{\sf N_{R}}\}.

Definition 2.1

(
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
)
.

An
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}

belongs to

{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
if

1.

\mathcal{O}
is in normal form, and

2.

if
C\sqsubseteq\exists P_{1}\in\mathcal{O}
,
P_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{O}}P_{2}
, and
\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{2}).A\sqsubseteq B\in\mathcal{O}
, then
A=\top
.

In particular, every
DL-Lite
R
ontology is an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
ontology (its GCIs are already in normal form and do not contain qualified role restrictions) and every
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
ontology can be normalized into a conservative extension in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
(since it does not contain inverse roles except in GCIs of the form
\exists R^{-}\sqsubseteq A
).
Since the binary relation
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{O}}
can be constructed in polynomial time and all other checks are based on the syntax of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
​, one can verify whether an ontology belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
in polynomial time w.r.t. its size.

The intuition behind the definition of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
is that we want to avoid situations in which the
ontology entails pairs of consequences of the form
C\sqsubseteq\exists P
and
\exists\mathsf{inv}(P).A\sqsubseteq B
,
which together imply
C\sqcap A\sqsubseteq\exists P.B
. Observe that the subsumer in this consequence is a qualified
existential restriction. The idea of the restriction is then that each of the “anonymous parts” of a model should be
constrained by a single atomic concept; that is, adding
A(a)
should not allow to qualify the
P
-successor required by

C(a)
and
C\sqsubseteq\exists P
.

By restricting the language in this way, we can decrease the complexity of reasoning. In fact, the algorithms we develop
in Section
6
will allow us to prove the following result.

Theorem 2.2
.

For ontologies that belong to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
​, satisfiability and axiom entailment are
PTime
-complete and BCQ entailment is
NP
-complete.

2.2
Semirings and Infinite Sums

In semiring provenance, database facts are annotated with elements of algebraic structures known as
commutative semirings
.
We recall here definitions related to semirings that will be useful in this work. We refer the interested reader to Section 2.2 of the PhD thesis of ? (?) for references and discussion of alternative definitions, and to the recent work by ? (?) for a discussion of the properties infinitary operations must satisfy to provide informative provenance analysis over infinite domains.

Semirings.

A semiring
\mathbb{K}=(K,\oplus,\otimes,\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1})
consists of a set
K

with distinguished elements
\mathbb{0}
and
\mathbb{1}
,
equipped with two binary operators: the
addition

\oplus
, which is
an associative and commutative operator with
identity
\mathbb{0}
, and the
multiplication

\otimes
, which is an associative operator with identity
\mathbb{1}
such that

\otimes
distributes over
\oplus
and
\mathbb{0}
is annihilating for
\otimes
.
Explicitly, a semiring satisfies the following properties for all
a,b,c\in K
:

•

(a\oplus b)\oplus c=a\oplus(b\oplus c)
(associativity of
\oplus
);

•

a\oplus b=b\oplus a
(commutativity of
\oplus
);

•

a\oplus\mathbb{0}=\mathbb{0}\oplus a=a
(
\mathbb{0}
identity of
\oplus
);

•

(a\otimes b)\otimes c=a\otimes(b\otimes c)
(associativity of
\otimes
);

•

a\otimes\mathbb{1}=\mathbb{1}\otimes a=a
(
\mathbb{1}
identity of
\otimes
);

•

a\otimes(b\oplus c)=(a\otimes b)\oplus(a\otimes c)
(distributivity of
\otimes
over
\oplus
); and

•

a\otimes\mathbb{0}=\mathbb{0}\otimes a=\mathbb{0}
(
\mathbb{0}
annihilating for
\otimes
).

A semiring is called
commutative
when
\otimes
is commutative.
We use the convention according to which multiplication has precedence over addition to omit parentheses
(i.e.
a\oplus b\otimes c
is
a\oplus(b\otimes c)
). If additive inverses exist (i.e. for every
a\in K
, there exists
b\in K
such that
a\oplus b=\mathbb{0}
), then
\mathbb{K}
is in fact a
ring
.

A semiring is
\oplus
-idempotent
(resp.
\otimes
-idempotent
) if for every
a\in K
,

a\oplus a=a
(resp.
a\otimes a=a
).
It is
absorptive
if for every
a,b\in K
,
a\otimes b\oplus a=a
. Note that if a semiring is absorptive, then it is also
\oplus
-
idempotent (since
a\oplus a=a\otimes\mathbb{1}\oplus a=a
).
Finally, a semiring is
positive
if for every
a,b\in K
, (i)
a\otimes b=\mathbb{0}
iff

a=\mathbb{0}
or
b=\mathbb{0}
, and
(ii)
a\oplus b=\mathbb{0}
iff

a=b=\mathbb{0}
.

Example 2.3
.

We consider the following commutative semirings in our running example.

•

The counting semiring
\mathbb{N}=(\mathbb{N},+,\times,0,1)
is the set of the natural integers equipped with the usual addition and multiplication between integers.

•

The tropical semiring
\mathbb{T}=(\mathbb{R}^{\infty}_{+},\min,+,\infty,0)
, used to represent costs, is the set of non-negative real numbers extended with the symbol
\infty
, with the minimum operation as addition (
a\oplus b=\min(a,b)
) and standard addition as multiplication (
a\otimes b=a+b
).

•

The Viterbi semiring
\mathbb{V}=([0,1],\max,\times,0,1)
, used to represent confidence scores, is the set of real numbers between
0
and
1
with the maximum operation as addition (
a\oplus b=\max(a,b)
) and the usual multiplication between reals (
a\otimes b=a\times b
).

•

The fuzzy semiring
\mathbb{F}=([0,1],\max,\min,0,1)
, used to represent truth degrees, is the set of real numbers between
0
and
1
with the maximum operation as addition (
a\oplus b=\max(a,b)
) and the minimum operation as multiplication (
a\otimes b=\min(a,b)
).

•

The Łukasiewicz semiring
\mathbb{L}=([0,1],\max,\star_{L},0,1)
, used to represent truth values, is the set of real numbers between
0
and
1
with the maximum operation as addition (
a\oplus b=\max(a,b)
) and multiplication defined by
a\star_{L}b=\max(0,a+b-1)
.

•

The access control semiring
\mathbb{A}=(\{P<C<S<T<0\},\min,\max,0,P)
, used to represent clearance levels required to access data (?), is the set
\{P,C,S,T,0\}
(for “public”, “confidential”, “secret”, “top-secret”, and “nobody knows”) with the minimum and maximum operations w.r.t. the obvious total ordering
P<C<S<T<0
as addition and multiplication respectively.

From these examples, the counting semiring is the only one which is not absorptive nor
\oplus
-idempotent;
the fuzzy and the access control semirings are the only ones which are
\otimes
-
idempotent;
and the Łukasiewicz semiring is the only one that is not positive since,
for example,
0.1\star_{L}0.1=\max(0,0.1+0.1-1)=0
.

An example of a
non-commutative
semiring is the semiring of languages over a finite alphabet
A
:
(2^{A^{*}},\cup,\cdot,\emptyset,\{\epsilon\})
where
2^{A^{*}}
is the set of possible sets of words over
A
,
\epsilon
is the empty word and
\cdot
is element-wise concatenation:
S_{1}\cdot S_{2}=\{w_{1}w_{2}\mid w_{1}\in S_{1},w_{2}\in S_{2}\}
.
∎

Infinite sums.

In this work we need to compute the addition over possibly uncountably many semiring elements and thus consider the notion of
complete

semirings (?, ?).
A semiring
\mathbb{K}=(K,\oplus,\otimes,\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1})
is
complete
(resp.
\omega
-complete
) if for every (resp. at most countable) family
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
of elements in
\mathbb{K}
indexed by
I
, we can define an element
\bigoplus_{i\in I}a_{i}
in
K
satisfying:

•

if
I
is empty:
\bigoplus_{i\in I}a_{i}=\mathbb{0}
;

•

if
I=\{1,\dots,n\}
is non-empty finite:
\bigoplus_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}a_{i}=a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}
;

•

\bigoplus_{i\in I}a_{i}=\bigoplus_{j\in J}(\bigoplus_{i\in I_{j}}a_{i})
if
\bigcup_{j\in J}I_{j}=I
and
I_{j}\cap I_{j^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for every
j\neq j^{\prime}
;

•

for every
b\in K
:
b\otimes(\bigoplus_{i\in I}a_{i})=\bigoplus_{i\in I}(b\otimes a_{i})
and
(\bigoplus_{i\in I}a_{i})\otimes b=\bigoplus_{i\in I}(a_{i}\otimes b)
.

Given a semiring
\mathbb{K}=(K,\oplus,\otimes,\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1})
, let
\leq

be the binary relation over
K
such that
a\leq b
if and only if there exists
c\in K
with
a\oplus c=b
.
The semiring
\mathbb{K}
is
naturally ordered
if
\leq
is a partial order,
\oplus
and
\otimes
are monotone in each argument and
\mathbb{0}
is the least element. Datalog provenance was originally defined for
\omega
-continuous
semirings (?), which are naturally ordered
\omega
-complete semirings that additionally satisfy some conditions related to the existence of least upper bounds according to
\leq
(?, ?).

Example 2.4
.

The counting semiring
\mathbb{N}
is the only semiring among those introduced in Example
2.3
that is not
\omega
-complete, hence not complete. A complete version
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
of
\mathbb{N}
is obtained by extending it with
\infty
, with both operations extended as expected, and
\Sigma_{i\in I}n_{i}
defined as follows for every family
(n_{i})_{i\in I}
of elements from
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
:

\Sigma_{i\in I}n_{i}=\infty
if there exists
i\in I
such that
n_{i}=\infty
;

\Sigma_{i\in I}n_{i}=\infty
if there are infinitely many
i\in I
such that
n_{i}\neq 0
;
and
\Sigma_{i\in I}n_{i}=\Sigma_{i\in I_{f}}n_{i}
otherwise, with
I_{f}=\{i\in I\mid n_{i}\neq 0\}
.
∎

Homomorphisms.

A
semiring homomorphism
from a semiring
\mathbb{K}=(K,\oplus,\otimes,\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1})
to another
\mathbb{K^{\prime}}=(K^{\prime},+,\cdot,0,1)
is a mapping
h:K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
such that
h(\mathbb{0})=0
,
h(\mathbb{1})=1
, and for all
a,b\in K
,
h(a\oplus b)=h(a)+h(b)
and
h(a\otimes b)=h(a)\cdot h(b)
.
A semiring homomorphism between (
\omega
-)complete semirings is
(
\omega
-)complete
if
h(\bigoplus_{i\in I}a_{i})=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{i\in I}h(a_{i})
for every (countable) family
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
.
A semiring homomorphism between
\omega
-continuous semirings is
\omega
-continuous
if it preserves least upper bounds.

2.3
Semiring Provenance in the Database Setting

In the database setting, the framework of
semiring provenance
generalizes the semantics of queries over
annotated databases.
We explain the main notions of this field next, assuming a basic understanding of relational databases (?).

Queries over annotated databases.

In semiring provenance, database facts are annotated with elements of commutative semirings.
The semantics of positive relational algebra queries (using operations select, project, natural join, rename and union, which are known to be equivalent to union of conjunctive queries and SQL
select-from-where-union
queries) over databases annotated with elements of any commutative
semiring is defined inductively on the structure of the query (?, ?). Intuitively, joint
use of the data corresponds to the semiring multiplication while alternative use of the data corresponds to the semiring
addition. In other words, we
multiply
the annotations of
all the facts that together produce an answer, and

add
such products over all combinations yielding the same answer.

Example 2.5
.

Consider the three facts
\alpha_{1}=\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus})
,
\alpha_{2}=\mathsf{mother}(\mathsf{Dionysus},\mathsf{Semele})
and
\alpha_{3}=\mathsf{mother}(\mathsf{Dionysus},\mathsf{Demeter})
, stating that Dionysus is a deity who has mothers Semele and Demeter, and the query
q:=\exists xy\,\mathsf{Deity}(x)\wedge\mathsf{mother}(x,y)
that asks if there is a deity who has a mother.
The answer is
yes
and it can be derived either using
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
or using
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{3}
, interpreting
y
by either
\mathsf{Semele}
or
\mathsf{Demeter}
. We now annotate the facts with elements of the semirings introduced in Example
2.3
.
Table
2
shows possible annotations and the resulting annotation of the query answer in each semiring.∎

\alpha_{1}

\alpha_{2}

\alpha_{3}

q

3

2

1

3\times 2+3\times 1=9

\mathbb{N}=(\mathbb{N},+,\times,0,1)

Costs

1

5

8

\min(1+5,1+8)=6

\mathbb{T}=(\mathbb{R}^{\infty}_{+},\min,+,\infty,0)

0.9

0.6

0.4

\max(0.9\times 0.6,0.9\times 0.4)=0.54

\mathbb{V}=([0,1],\max,\times,0,1)

\mathbb{A}

)

Table 2:
The results of provenance computation over different semirings and annotations.

? (?) also define semiring provenance for
Datalog queries (i.e. queries formed by a set of Datalog rules with a distinguished output predicate), using derivation trees. The approach associates to the query the sum over all its derivation trees of
the products of the annotations of the tree leaves (which correspond to database facts).
To be able to handle infinitely many derivation trees and relate the provenance of a Datalog query with the least fixpoint of a system of fixpoint equations, they consider
\omega
-continuous semirings. However, their definition of provenance for Datalog queries is already well-defined for semirings that are only
\omega
-complete.

Provenance semirings.

Provenance semirings were introduced to abstract from a specific semiring and compute a representation of the provenance.
Given a finite set
{\sf X}
of

variables
2
2

2

In the literature
{\sf X}
is often only assumed to be countable. Following ? (?), we assume
{\sf X}
to be finite for simplicity, since variables are only used to annotate the elements of finite sets.

which are used to annotate the database facts and can be thought of as identifiers, a
provenance semiring

\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]=(\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}],\oplus,\otimes,\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1})
parametrized by
{\sf X}

is a semiring over a space of

provenance expressions
built from variables from
{\sf X}
,
\mathbb{0}
,
\mathbb{1}
,
\oplus
and
\otimes
.

We recall below the definitions of provenance semirings that have been considered in the literature (?, ?, ?).

•

The
provenance polynomials semiring for
{\sf X}
is
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]=(\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}],+,\times,0,1)
where
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
is the set of polynomials with variables from
{\sf X}
and coefficients from
\mathbb{N}
, with the operations defined as usual.

•

The
Boolean provenance polynomials semiring for
{\sf X}
is
\mathbb{B}[{\sf X}]=(\mathbb{B}[{\sf X}],+,\times,0,1)
where
\mathbb{B}[{\sf X}]
is the set of polynomials over variables
{\sf X}
with Boolean coefficients.

•

The
trio semiring for
{\sf X}
, denoted
\sf{Trio}[{\sf X}]
, is the quotient semiring of
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
by
\approx_{f}
, where
\approx_{f}
is the congruence relation defined by
p_{1}\approx_{f}p_{2}
iff
f(p_{1})=f(p_{2})
with
f
the function that “drops exponents”.

•

The
sorp semiring for
{\sf X}
, denoted
\sf{Sorp}[{\sf X}]
, is the quotient semiring of
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
by
\approx
, where
\approx
is the smallest congruence relation on
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
that identifies polynomials according to absorption.

•

The
why-provenance semiring for
{\sf X}
is
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]=(\sf{Why}[{\sf X}],\cup,\Cup,\emptyset,\{\emptyset\})
where
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
consists of the set of all possible sets of subsets of
{\sf X}
and
\Cup
denotes pairwise union:
S_{1}\Cup S_{2}=\{s_{1}\cup s_{2}\mid s_{1}\in S_{1},s_{2}\in S_{2}\}
.

•

The
semiring of positive Boolean functions over
{\sf X}
is
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]=(\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}],\vee,\wedge,0,1)
where
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
is the set of classes of equivalent positive Boolean expressions over variables
{\sf X}

(which involve only disjunction, conjunction, and constants
1
and
0
for true and false but without any negations).
We identify elements of
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
with their irredundant disjunctive normal form.
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
is isomorphic to the bounded distributive lattice freely generated by
{\sf X}
.

•

The
lineage semiring for
{\sf X}
is
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]=(\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}],\cup,\cup^{*},\emptyset^{*},\emptyset)
where
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]=2^{\sf X}\cup\{\emptyset^{*}\}
is the set of all subsets of
{\sf X}
extended with an element
\emptyset^{*}
,
\cup
is the usual union,
S_{1}\cup^{*}S_{2}=S_{1}\cup S_{2}
if
S_{1},S_{2}\neq\emptyset^{*}
and
\emptyset^{*}\cup S=S\cup\emptyset^{*}=S
,
\emptyset^{*}\cup^{*}S=S\cup^{*}\emptyset^{*}=\emptyset^{*}
. Intuitively,
\emptyset^{*}
and
\cup^{*}
are introduced because taking
\oplus=\otimes=\cup
and
\mathbb{0}=\mathbb{1}=\emptyset
would not satisfy the requirement that in a semiring,
\mathbb{0}
is annihilating for
\otimes
.
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
is a semiring such that for every
a,b\neq\mathbb{0}
,
a\oplus b=a\otimes b
(denoted by
\oplus\approx\otimes
).

We will often write a set of variables
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
as their product, i.e. as the
monomial

x_{1}\times\dots\times x_{n}
(or
x_{1}\cdots x_{n}
) and see a set of such sets (i.e. an element of
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
) as a
sum of monomials
. Similarly we can write, e.g., the formula
x_{1}\wedge x_{2}\vee x_{3}
as
x_{1}\times x_{2}+x_{3}
, and represent all provenance expressions from
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
,
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
or
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
as
polynomials
.
Figure
4
shows the hierarchy of expressiveness for these provenance semirings (intuitively, annotating a query result by
x^{2}+xy\in\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
expresses that it can be obtained either by using twice a fact annotated by
x
or once a fact annotated by
x
and once a fact annotated with
y
, while annotation
x+xy\in\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
only indicates that the query result can be obtained by using a fact annotated with
x
or two facts annotated with
x
and
y
, thus is less informative).
In this work, we mostly focus on the provenance semirings
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
,
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
and discuss the difficulties that arise for
more expressive provenance semirings. These provenance semirings correspond to notions of query explanation that have long been considered in the
context of databases:
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
corresponds to the
why-provenance
, or
witness basis
,
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
corresponds to
the
minimal witness basis
, and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
corresponds to

lineage
(?, ?).

\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
\mathbb{B}[{\sf X}]

\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
\sf{Trio}[{\sf X}]
\oplus
-
idempotent
drop exponents
absorptive

-

4

The original picture by ? has a typo:
\sf{Trio}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
are not
\otimes
-idempotent (since
(x+y)\times(x+y)=x+y+x\times y
in these semirings) but rather can be obtained by “dropping exponents” (cf. ?, for the precise definition of
\sf{Trio}[{\sf X}]
).
).
\mathbb{K}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}_{2}
means that there exists a surjective semiring homomorphism from
\mathbb{K}_{1}
to
\mathbb{K}_{2}
.

As mentioned before, for Datalog queries, infinite provenance expressions may be needed.
For example, the Datalog query that consists of the recursive rule
A(x)\leftarrow A(x)
and the rule

\mathsf{goal}\leftarrow A(x)
where
\mathsf{goal}
is the output predicate, evaluated
over the database that contains a single fact
A(a)
annotated with
x_{0}\in{\sf X}
, has infinitely many derivation trees and its provenance as defined by ? (?) is an infinite sum of
x_{0}
, which is not an element of
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
.
A formal power series with variables from
{\sf X}
and coefficients from
C
is a mapping that associates to
each monomial over
{\sf X}
a coefficient in
C
. A formal power series
S
can be written as a possibly
infinite sum
S=\Sigma_{m\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})}S(m)m
where

\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
is the set of all monomials over
{\sf X}
.

C\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
denotes the set of formal power series with variables from
{\sf X}

and coefficients from
C
.
? (?) define the

Datalog provenance semiring
as
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
, the commutative
\omega
-continuous semiring of formal power
series with coefficients from
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
We can obtain a hierarchy similar to that of Figure
4
for
\omega
-continuous commutative provenance semirings where
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
is replaced by
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
,
\mathbb{B}[{\sf X}]
is replaced by the semiring of formal power series with Boolean coefficient
\mathbb{B}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
,
and
\sf{Trio}[{\sf X}]
is replaced by
\sf{Trio}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
(obtained from
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
as
\sf{Trio}[{\sf X}]
is obtained from
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
). Note that since we assume that
{\sf X}
is finite, there is no difference between polynomials and formal power series when considering
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
,
\sf{Sorp}[{\sf X}]
,
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
.
There is no hierarchical relationship between
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
and
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
because there is no
surjective
semiring
homomorphism from
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
to
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
nor from
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
to
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
.

A provenance semiring
\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]

specializes
correctly to a semiring
\mathbb{K}
, if any function
\nu:{\sf X}\rightarrow K
extends uniquely to a (
\omega
-continuous if
\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]
and
\mathbb{K}
are
\omega
-continuous) semiring homomorphism
h:\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]\rightarrow K
such that
h(x)=\nu(x)
for every
x\in{\sf X}
(assuming that
{\sf X}\subseteq\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]
), allowing the computations for
\mathbb{K}
to factor through the computations for
\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]
(?, ?). A provenance semiring
\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]
is
universal for a class of semirings
if it specializes correctly to each semiring of this class. This is equivalent to say that
\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]
has the universal mapping property for this class over
{\sf X}
, or that
\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]
is the free algebra generated by
{\sf X}
for this class.
? (?) showed that
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
is universal for commutative semirings and
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
is universal for commutative
\omega
-continuous semirings.
It is also well-known that

\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
specializes correctly to every commutative semiring that is
\otimes
-idempotent and absorptive.
Note that a provenance semiring specializing correctly to another does not mean that the former is more expressive than the latter. For instance,
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
specializes correctly to
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
, but is not more expressive than
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
.

Example 2.6
.

If we consider the semirings from Example
2.3
and the provenance semirings in Figure
4
, only

\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
specializes correctly to the counting semiring
(\mathbb{N},+,\times,0,1)
;
\sf{Sorp}[{\sf X}]
and all provenance
semirings above it specialize correctly to the tropical semiring
(\mathbb{R}^{\infty}_{+},\min,+,\infty,0)
, the Viterbi semiring

([0,1],\max,\times,0,1)
and the Łukasiewicz semiring
([0,1],\max,\star_{L},0,1)
; and
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
along with all provenance
semirings above it specialize correctly to the fuzzy semiring
([0,1],\max,\min,0,1)
and the access control semiring

(\{P<C<S<T<0\},\min,\max,0,P)
.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

Interestingly,
\sf{Trio}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
do not specialize correctly to themselves: if
\nu(x)=x+y
and
\nu(z)=z
for every
z\in{\sf X}\setminus\{x\}
, there is no semiring homomorphism
h
that extends
\nu
because it would imply that
x+y=h(x)=h(x^{2})=h(x)\times h(x)=x+y+xy
.

3
A Semiring Provenance Semantics for Description Logics

In this section, we define a provenance semantics for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontologies. We explain our design choices and the restrictions of our setting. Finally, we investigate whether it captures some well-known semantics for ontologies annotated with specific kinds of information. A more detailed analysis of the properties of our semantics is conducted in Sections
4
and
5
.

3.1
Provenance Semantics for Annotated Ontologies

Let
\mathbb{K}=(K,\oplus,\otimes,\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1})
be a commutative semiring and
\mathcal{L}
be a DL language
encompassed by
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
.
A
(
\mathbb{K}
-)annotated
\mathcal{L}
ontology

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is a pair
\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
where

\mathcal{O}
is an
\mathcal{L}
ontology and
\lambda
is an annotation function

\lambda:\mathcal{O}\mapsto K\setminus\{\mathbb{0}\}
. We often treat
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
as the set of pairs

\{(\alpha,\lambda(\alpha))\mid\alpha\in\mathcal{O}\}
and call such pairs
(\alpha,\lambda(\alpha))

annotated axioms
.
We next define the semantics of
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
\mathcal{L}
ontologies before showing that annotations do not impact satisfiability and discussing our design choices.

3.1.1
Semantics Definition

The semantics of annotated
ontologies extends the classical notion of
interpretations with annotations.
A
(
\mathbb{K}
-)annotated interpretation
is a triple

\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},K,\cdot^{\mathcal{I}})

where
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
is a non-empty set (the

domain
of
\mathcal{I}
),
and
\cdot^{\mathcal{I}}
maps

•

every
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
to
a^{\mathcal{I}}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

every
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
to

A^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times K
;

•

every
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
to

R^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times K
.

We extend

\cdot^{\mathcal{I}}
to complex expressions as follows:

\displaystyle(\top)^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{\mathbb{1}\};

\displaystyle(\bot)^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\emptyset;

\displaystyle(R^{-})^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\{(e,d,\kappa)\mid(d,e,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}}\};

\displaystyle(\exists P.C)^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\{(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\mid\exists e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\text{ s.t. }(d,e,\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{I}},(e,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\};

\displaystyle(C\sqcap D)^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\{(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\mid(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}},(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}\};

\displaystyle(P\sqcap Q)^{\mathcal{I}}={}

\displaystyle\{(e,d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\mid(e,d,\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{I}},(e,d,\kappa^{\prime})\in Q^{\mathcal{I}}\}.

The annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}

satisfies
:

\displaystyle({C\sqsubseteq D},{\kappa}),

\displaystyle\text{if, for all }d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa^{\prime}\in K,\ (d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\text{ implies }(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}\!;

\displaystyle({P\sqsubseteq Q},{\kappa}),

\displaystyle\text{if, for all }d,e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa^{\prime}\in K,\ (d,e,\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}\text{ implies }(d,e,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in Q^{\mathcal{I}}\!;

\displaystyle({P\sqcap Q\sqsubseteq\bot},{\kappa}),

\displaystyle\text{if }(P\sqcap Q)^{\mathcal{I}}=\emptyset;

\displaystyle({A(a)},{\kappa}),

\displaystyle\text{if }(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}\!;\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle({R(a,b)},{\kappa}),

\displaystyle\text{if }(a^{\mathcal{I}},b^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}}\!;

Note that since
\bot^{\mathcal{I}}=\emptyset
, the satisfaction of a GCI of the form
(C\sqsubseteq\bot,\kappa)
(i.e.
(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
implies

(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in\bot^{\mathcal{I}}
) is equivalent to
C^{\mathcal{I}}=\emptyset
.
An annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is a
model
of the annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, denoted
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, if
it satisfies all annotated axioms in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
;

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}

entails
an annotated axiom

(\alpha,\kappa)
, denoted
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
,
if
\mathcal{I}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

Some remarks are in place. Intuitively, each annotation of a domain element
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
in the interpretation
C^{\mathcal{I}}
of an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
C
corresponds to some match of the corresponding rooted tree-shaped query
q_{C}(d)
in
\mathcal{I}
seen as an annotated database. We explain in Section
3.1.3
why we choose to allow for multiple annotations rather than using a single annotation that sums over all such matches.
Then GCIs propagate these annotations, multiplying them by their own annotation. That is why the interpretation of a concept
name may contain several pairs
(d,\kappa)

with the same domain element
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
,
allowing for several provenance annotations to be dealt with simultaneously. Interpretations of role names behave similarly.

Contrary to the ontology annotations, we allow for
\mathbb{0}
to occur in annotated interpretations (indeed, if the semiring is not positive and there are some
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}\neq\mathbb{0}
such that
\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime}=\mathbb{0}
, an interpretation may need to use
\mathbb{0}
to satisfy some axiom). We shall however see in Theorem
5.1
that when the semiring is positive,
\mathcal{O}\models A(a)
iff there exists
\kappa\neq\mathbb{0}
such that
(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
for every
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, and similarly for role assertions.

The interpretation of
\top
by
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{\mathbb{1}\}
yields that
A^{\mathcal{I}}\not\subseteq\top^{\mathcal{I}}
in general, in contrast with the classical semantics of DL, and that
\emptyset\not\models(A\sqsubseteq\top,\mathbb{1})
. However, interpreting
\top
by
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times K
would “flood” the annotations of models of annotated ontologies such that
\top
occurs in the left-hand side of some GCIs: for example,
\{(A(a),\mathbb{1}),(A\sqsubseteq\exists R,\mathbb{1}),(\exists R.\top\sqsubseteq B,\mathbb{1})\}
would entail
(B(a),\kappa)
for every
\kappa\in K
while it should only entail
(B(a),\mathbb{1})
.

Provenance.

The
provenance annotations
of an axiom
\alpha
in an annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is the set
{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}}({\alpha})=\{\kappa\mid\mathcal{I}\models(\alpha,\kappa)\}
.
If the semiring
\mathbb{K}
is complete (or if
\mathbb{K}
is
\omega
-complete and
K
is countable), we define the
provenance
of
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}

as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}):=\bigoplus_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)}\kappa.

(1)

Thanks to the conditions on
\mathbb{K}
, the sum in Equation (
1
) is well-defined even if there are infinitely many
\kappa\in K
such that

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
(note that there can be uncountably many such
\kappa
only if
K
is uncountable). Moreover, if there is no such element (i.e. the sum is empty),
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathbb{0}
.

Remark 3.1
.

In the case where
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is satisfiable,
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\bigoplus_{\kappa\in\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}}({\alpha})}\kappa
, while for an unsatisfiable
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\bigoplus_{\kappa\in K}\kappa
, as in this case
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
for every
\kappa\in K
.

Remark 3.2

(Unsatisfiable concept/role)
.

If an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
C
is unsatisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
, i.e. such that for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
,

C^{\mathcal{I}}=\emptyset
, then for every
\mathbb{K}
-annotated version

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
,
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
D
and
\kappa\in K
, it holds that

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
, so that

\mathcal{P}(C\sqsubseteq D,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\bigoplus_{\kappa\in K}\kappa
. The same holds for unsatisfiable roles.
Such provenance is not informative, in the same way that
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq\top,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathbb{0}
is not, but in both cases, these axioms are tautologies and one is probably not interested in computing their provenance.

Example 3.3
.

Consider the following ontology:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\{

\displaystyle\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus}),\mathsf{mother}(\mathsf{Dionysus},\mathsf{Semele}),\mathsf{mother}(\mathsf{Dionysus},\mathsf{Demeter}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Demeter}),\mathsf{father}(\mathsf{Dionysus},\mathsf{Zeus}),\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Zeus}),

\displaystyle\exists\mathsf{parent}.\mathsf{Deity}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Deity},\mathsf{mother}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{parent},\mathsf{father}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{parent}\}.

We define the annotated ontologies
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{T}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\mathbb{T}}\rangle
, and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\mathbb{F}}\rangle
, annotated with variables, costs and truth degrees respectively, as follows.

\lambda_{\sf X}

\lambda_{\mathbb{T}}

\lambda_{\mathbb{F}}

\mathsf{Deity}
(
\mathsf{Dionysus}
)

x_{1}

(

,
\mathsf{Semele}
)

\mathsf{mother}
(

,

)

Let
\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}
be a
{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
-annotated interpretation such that
\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}\models\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
. Recall that
{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
is commutative, so that the order of variables in monomials does not matter (we write them below in the lexicographic order).

•

Since
\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}\models(\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus}),x_{1})
, then
(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{1})\in\mathsf{Deity}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
.

Since
\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}\models(\mathsf{mother}(\mathsf{Dionysus},\mathsf{Demeter}),x_{3})
,

.
So

(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},\mathsf{Demeter}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{3}y_{2})\in\mathsf{parent}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}\models(\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Demeter}),x_{4})
implies

(\mathsf{Demeter}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{4})\in\mathsf{Deity}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
. Hence from

\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}\models(\exists\mathsf{parent}.\mathsf{Deity}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Deity},y_{1})
one obtains

(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2})\in\mathsf{Deity}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
.

•

Similarly, since we have
\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}\models(\mathsf{father}(\mathsf{Dionysus},\mathsf{Zeus}),x_{5})

, and

it holds that
(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{5}x_{6}y_{1}y_{3})\in\mathsf{Deity}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
.

Hence, it holds that
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\models(\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus}),x_{1})
,
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\models(\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus}),x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2})
,
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\models(\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus}),x_{5}x_{6}y_{1}y_{3})
, and the provenance of
\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus})
w.r.t.

is

.

-annotated interpretations that are models of

and

respectively. Recall that the multiplication of the tropical semiring

is the usual addition and its addition is

, while the multiplication of the fuzzy semiring

is

and its addition is
\max
.

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{T}}\models(\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus}),1)
,

,

;

,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}\models(\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus}),0.2)
,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}\models(\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus}),0.5)
, and
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Deity}(\mathsf{Dionysus}),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})=0.9
. ∎

3.1.2
Satisfiability

The next lemma shows how annotated and non-annotated models are related, and that satisfiability does not depend on the annotations.

Definition 3.4
.

A (classical) interpretation
\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},\cdot^{\mathcal{I}})
and a
\mathbb{K}
-annotated interpretation

\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}},K,\cdot^{\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}})

coincide on their non-annotated part
iff (i)
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}}
;
(ii) for all
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
,
a^{\mathcal{I}}=a^{\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}}
;
(iii) for all
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
and
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, it holds that
d\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
iff there exists
\kappa\in K
such that

(d,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}}
; and
(iv) for all
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
and
d,e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, it holds that
(d,e)\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
iff there exists
\kappa\in K
such
that
(d,e,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
be a
\mathbb{K}
-annotated ontology. The following claims hold.

(i)

For every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
, there exists a model
\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}
coincide on their non-annotated part.

(ii)

For every model
\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
there exists a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
such that
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}
coincide on their non-annotated part.

Proof.

(i) Given a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
, we obtain a model
\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
as follows:
a^{\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}}=a^{\mathcal{I}}
for every
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
,
A^{\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}}=A^{\mathcal{I}}\times K
for every
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
and
R^{\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}}=R^{\mathcal{I}}\times K
for every
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
.
(ii) Given a model
\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, we obtain a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
by dropping the annotations from
\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
∎

3.1.3
Provenance Design Choices

One could think of many other ways of defining provenance for description logics. We explain the choices we made here.

Use of annotated models.

The most established notion of provenance for Datalog queries is based on proof trees, and one could try to extend such definition to DLs instead of proposing a definition based on annotated models.
Defining proof trees in DL in a way that leads to a reasonable notion of provenance is however far from being straightforward. In particular, the set of
tree proofs for
\mathcal{O}\models\alpha
defined by ? (?, ?) contains all trees consisting of a single hyperedge
(\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\alpha)
with
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
such that
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\models\alpha
, hence using this set to define provenance would not make sense beyond
\otimes
-idempotent and absorptive semirings (for which it will amount to consider only minimal such
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
). To obtain meaningful tree proofs, ? thus consider only those that can be found in a
derivation structure produced by a deriver
, which defines the class of allowed inference steps.
However, a semantics for annotated DLs based on such tree proofs would depend on the choice of the deriver, which does not seem a proper definition for a
semantics
and is not in line with the way semantics of existing annotated DLs (such as fuzzy DLs) are defined.

Definition of annotated models.

One could think of many options for the definition of annotated interpretations.
First, one could interpret complex concepts by, e.g., tuples of the form
(d,\bigoplus_{(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}},(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}}\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})
for
(C\sqcap D)^{\mathcal{I}}
(i.e. using the “relational database provenance” of the query
q_{C\sqcap D}(x)=C(x)\wedge D(x)
in
\mathcal{I}
to obtain the annotations). However, “extra tuples” would then make most entailments of annotated assertions fail: if
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(A(a),\kappa),(B(a),\kappa^{\prime}),(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq C,\mathbb{1})\}
, we would not obtain
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(C(a),\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})
since
\mathcal{I}
defined by
A^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa),(a^{\mathcal{I}},\epsilon)\}
,
B^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa^{\prime})\}
and
C^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime}\oplus\epsilon\otimes\kappa^{\prime}\}
will be an annotated model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
which does not satisfy the expected annotated assertion.

A workaround to avoid this problem while using a single semiring annotation per tuple of domain elements
has been considered in the context of Datalog queries, in which
? (?) proposed two alternative ways of
defining provenance semantics based on annotated models. One (called
set-annotated model-based semantic
) is
similar to the one we choose here (cf. Lemma
5.11
) while the other (called
annotated
model-based semantics
) is defined on a restricted class of semirings where every set of semiring elements has a
greatest lower bound
. This second semantics requires that each tuple in an interpretation is annotated by a unique semiring element, with the condition that, e.g.,
\mathcal{I}\models(A(a),\kappa)
if
(a^{\mathcal{I}},\chi)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
\chi\geq\kappa
and
\mathcal{I}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
if
(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
implies
(d,\chi)\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
\chi\geq\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime}
. The provenance of
\alpha
is then defined as the infimum of
\kappa
such that
\mathcal{I}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
over all models
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. However, this definition is more complex and it has been shown for the case of Datalog that both semantics coincide when
\oplus
is idempotent and suffer from undesirable behaviors when
\oplus
is not idempotent.

We shall indeed see that our semantics may have some counter-intuitive behaviors when
\oplus
is not idempotent.
This is due to the use of sets of annotated tuples to interpret concepts and roles, which does not allow us to account for
the fact that the same semiring element can be used multiple times: e.g., if

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(R(a,b),\kappa),(R(a,c),\kappa),(\exists R.\top\sqsubseteq A,\mathbb{1})\}
, then

\mathcal{P}(A(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\kappa
while we could arguably expect it to be
\kappa\oplus\kappa
to take into account
both assertions (we will come back to this question in more details in Section
5
).
A natural way to obtain the desired provenance in this specific example would be to use
multisets
instead of sets to interpret concepts and roles (so that
(\exists R.\top)^{\mathcal{I}}
would be defined by the multiset
\{(d,\chi)\mid(d,e,\chi)\in R^{\mathcal{I}}\}
and contain
(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)
at least twice in a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
), and set conditions such as
\mathcal{I}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
means that if
(d,\kappa^{\prime})
occurs
k
times in
C^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})
must occur at least
k
times in
D^{\mathcal{I}}
. However, multisets cannot deal with multiplicities in all cases:
if
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(A(a),\mathbb{1}),(A\sqsubseteq B_{1},\kappa),(A\sqsubseteq B_{2},\kappa),(B_{1}\sqsubseteq C,\mathbb{1}),(B_{2}\sqsubseteq C,\mathbb{1})\}
, one would still obtain a provenance of
\kappa
instead of
\kappa\oplus\kappa
for
C(a)
or
A\sqsubseteq C
. To take into account the two axioms
(A\sqsubseteq B_{1},\kappa)
and
(A\sqsubseteq B_{2},\kappa)
, we would need to define
C^{\mathcal{I}}
as the (multiset) union of
B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
, which cannot be done while defining axiom satisfaction independently from the other ontology axioms. We thus choose to stick with sets which allow for simpler definitions and a homogeneous treatment of the above examples.

Finally, one could require that annotations in interpretations are closed under
\oplus
, e.g. that
(c,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(c,\kappa^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
implies that
(c,\kappa\oplus\kappa^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
. However, this would lead to arguably undesirable entailments
even if
\oplus
is required to be idempotent.
Consider for instance the ontology

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}=\{

\displaystyle(A_{1}(a),x_{1}),(A_{2}(a),x_{2}),(A\sqsubseteq\exists R,y),(\exists R^{-}.\top\sqsubseteq B_{1},z),

\displaystyle(\exists R^{-}.\top\sqsubseteq B_{2},u),(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C,v),(\exists R.C\sqsubseteq D,w)\qquad\}.

One can verify that under our semantics,

\mathcal{P}(D(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=x_{1}yzuvw+x_{2}yzuvw=(x_{1}+x_{2})yzuvw,

witnessing as expected the two possible ways to obtain
D(a)
, using either
A_{1}(a)
or
A_{2}(a)
and all GCIs.
If we require that interpretations are closed under
+
, we would obtain instead
(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2})yzuvw
while there is no way to derive
D(a)
using both assertions together. Indeed, in every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
such that
\mathcal{I}
is closed under
+
, we have that
(a^{\mathcal{I}},x_{1}+x_{2})\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, which implies that there exists
(a^{\mathcal{I}},e,(x_{1}+x_{2})y)\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
, so that
(e,(x_{1}+x_{2})yz)\in B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(e,(x_{1}+x_{2})yu)\in B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
, hence
(e,(x_{1}+x_{2})(x_{1}+x_{2})yzuv)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.
(e,(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2})yzuv)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
(a^{\mathcal{I}},(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2})yzuvw)\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
.

Provenance definition from annotated models.

We could define
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
by considering sums of annotations for each annotated model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
separately and then defining
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
to be the biggest common sum. However, we can show that this more complex definition would lead to the same provenance values. This follows from the next proposition.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology and
\alpha
be an assertion, RI or GCI. If
\kappa\in K
is such that for every annotated model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, there exists
(\kappa_{\mathcal{I}}^{i})_{i\in I_{\mathcal{I}}}
(with all
\kappa_{\mathcal{I}}^{i}
distinct) such that
\bigoplus_{i\in I_{\mathcal{I}}}\kappa_{\mathcal{I}}^{i}=\kappa
and
\mathcal{I}\models(\alpha,\kappa_{\mathcal{I}}^{i})
for every
i\in I_{\mathcal{I}}
, then there exists
(\kappa^{i})_{i\in I}
(with all
\kappa^{i}
distinct) such that
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\kappa^{i}=\kappa
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa^{i})
for every
i\in I
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is unsatisfiable, then
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
and so the result holds trivially. We next assume that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is satisfiable. In the case where
\alpha
is an assertion, the proposition follows from the existence of a model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(\alpha,\chi)
iff
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\chi)
(Theorem
4.5
).
In the case where
\alpha
is a GCI or an RI, we show the contrapositive (assuming that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
does not contain any assertion to simplify the writing, since assertions of a satisfiable ontology have no impact on the entailment of annotated GCIs and RIs). Let
S=\{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}\mid\bigoplus_{i\in I}\chi^{i}=\kappa,\chi^{i}\neq\chi^{j}\text{ if }i\neq j\}
and assume that for every
(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}\in S
, there exists
j\in I
such that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(\alpha,\chi^{j})
, i.e. there exists
\mathcal{J}_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that
\mathcal{J}_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}}\not\models(\alpha,\chi^{j})
.
Assume w.l.o.g. that all such
\mathcal{J}_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}}
have disjoint domains and let
\mathcal{J}
be the annotated interpretation defined by
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}=\bigcup_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}\in S}\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}}}
,
A^{\mathcal{J}}=\bigcup_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}\in S}A^{\mathcal{J}_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}}}
for every
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
and
R^{\mathcal{J}}=\bigcup_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}\in S}R^{\mathcal{J}_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}}}
for every
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
. It is easy to check that
\mathcal{J}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. Moreover, for every
(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}\in S
, there exists
j\in I
such that
\mathcal{J}\not\models(\alpha,\chi^{j})
(since
\mathcal{J}_{(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}}\not\models(\alpha,\chi^{j})
) so
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that there is no
(\chi^{i})_{i\in I}
(with all
\chi^{i}
distinct) such that
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\chi^{i}=\kappa
and
\mathcal{J}\models(\alpha,\chi^{i})
for every
i\in I
.
∎

3.1.4
Choice of DL Language

We explain here the syntactic restrictions we impose on
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
and the difficulties of extending the semantics to non-Horn DLs.

Syntactic restrictions.

We start with the restrictions on the form of the right-hand side of the
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
GCIs (namely, GCIs have right-hand side
D::=A\mid\exists P.\top\mid\bot
, forbidding to use a conjunction or qualified role existential restriction contrary to what is usually allowed in the
{\cal E\!L}
family).
Example
3.7
illustrates a counter-intuitive behavior when a conjunction occurs on the right-hand side of a GCI, if we extend the semantics to GCI with conjunctions in the right-hand side as expected:
\mathcal{I}\models(C\sqsubseteq C_{1}\sqcap C_{2},\kappa)
if
(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
implies
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in(C_{1}\sqcap C_{2})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Qualified role restrictions lead to the same kind of behavior as they can be seen as a kind of implicit conjunction.

Example 3.7
.

Consider
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(A\sqsubseteq B\sqcap C,\kappa),(A(a),\kappa^{\prime})\}
where
\mathbb{K}
is a commutative
\otimes
-idempotent
semiring. The following interpretations
which interpret
a
by itself are models of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ }l@{\ }l}A^{\mathcal{I}_{1}}=\{(a,\kappa^{\prime})\},&\quad B^{\mathcal{I}_{1}}=\{(a,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\},&C^{\mathcal{I}_{1}}=\{(a,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\}\\
A^{\mathcal{I}_{2}}=\{(a,\kappa^{\prime})\},&\quad B^{\mathcal{I}_{2}}=\{(a,\kappa)\},&C^{\mathcal{I}_{2}}=\{(a,\kappa^{\prime})\}\\
A^{\mathcal{I}_{3}}=\{(a,\kappa^{\prime})\},&\quad B^{\mathcal{I}_{3}}=\{(a,\mathbb{1})\},&C^{\mathcal{I}_{3}}=\{(a,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\}\end{array}

Since the semantics does not provide a unique way to “split” the semiring element
\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime}
between the two elements of the conjunction, if
