\kappa\neq\mathbb{1}
or
\kappa^{\prime}\neq\mathbb{1}
, then
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(B(a),\chi)
for any
\chi\in K
, i.e.
\mathcal{P}(B(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathbb{0}
. In particular,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(B(a),\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})
which is arguably counter-intuitive since
we intuitively know that
a
is in
A
with provenance
\kappa^{\prime}
and that
A
is a subclass of the intersection of
B
and
C
with provenance
\kappa
.
The syntactic restriction ensures that if
\mathbb{K}
is positive, for every assertion
\alpha
such that
\mathcal{O}\models\alpha
,
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})\neq\mathbb{0}
(Theorem
5.1
).
∎

A similar assumption has been made in previous work on provenance for knowledge bases. In particular, the most similar works considered either DL-Lite, which does not allow for conjunction or qualified role restriction (?), or
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
with the same syntactic restriction (?). In the same way, ? (?) defined several notions of provenance for Datalog assuming that rules are normalized to have only one atom in the head. Dealing with conjunction in the head has also proven difficult in other contexts related to annotated databases or knowledge bases. For example, ? (?) defined two bag semantics in the context of data exchange and proved that in the case where the mappings do not have existentially quantified variables in the head, query answering under both semantics is in
PTime
w.r.t. data complexity when mappings have only one atom in the head and becomes
coNP
-complete when two atoms are allowed. ? (?) defined bag semantics for ontology-based data access, where the assertions of a DL-Lite ontology are created via mappings from a database, only for the case where the mappings have one atom in the head.
Moreover, the decision algorithm for fuzzy
{\cal E\!L}
by ? (?) is only correct if conjunctions on the right are disallowed and the undecidability results for the cases where
\otimes
is not
idempotent (?) rely heavily on the right-hand side conjunctions and existential restrictions.
Finally, in the context of explanations for DLs, ? (?) showed that even if existential
restrictions are disallowed, conjunctions on the right-hand side of GCIs increase the complexity of computing justifications.

Partially normalizing the ontology before annotating it, or more specifically, replacing e.g. annotated GCIs of the form

(C\sqsubseteq C_{1}\sqcap C_{2},\kappa)
by
(C\sqsubseteq C_{1},\kappa)
and
(C\sqsubseteq C_{2},\kappa)
,
may be acceptable in most cases, even if the rewritten ontology leads to additional—arguably natural—consequences compared to
the original one. For instance, even if
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(A\sqsubseteq B,\kappa)

in Example
3.7
,
in many cases a user would accept to change the GCI of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
to
(A\sqsubseteq B,\kappa)
and
(A\sqsubseteq C,\kappa)
as it may reflect the original intention of the GCI.

One could argue that it would be better to define the semantics so that only
\mathcal{I}_{1}
was a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
in Example
3.7
, instead of restricting the language as we do. We explain next why this is not so simple.

One possibility is to change the definition of satisfaction of a GCI by an interpretation such that
\mathcal{I}\models(A\sqsubseteq B\sqcap C,\kappa)
iff for every
(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in B^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
, and similarly for qualified role restrictions.
This approach leads to a counter-intuitive behavior. For instance if
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\emptyset
, then
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq A\sqcap B,\mathbb{1})
, since there is a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that
A^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a,\kappa)\}
and
B^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a,\kappa^{\prime})\}
, so that
(A\sqcap B)^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\}
, and
(a,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\notin A^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(a,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\notin B^{\mathcal{I}}
.
In contrast, our definition of satisfaction ensures that for every interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and concept
C
,
\mathcal{I}\models(C\sqsubseteq C,\mathbb{1})
.

Another possibility is to modify the interpretation of conjunctions and qualified role restrictions such that

\displaystyle(C\sqcap D)^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle{}=\{(d,\kappa)\mid(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}},(d,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}}\}

and

\displaystyle(\exists R.C)^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle{}=\{(d,\kappa)\mid\exists e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\text{ s.t. }(d,e,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}},(e,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}.

However, this would lead to the loss of many natural and wanted entailments, for example

\{(A(a),\kappa),(B(a),\kappa^{\prime}),(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq C,\mathbb{1})\}\models(C(a),\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})
would not hold.

Hence, restricting the syntax to prevent conjunctions on the right and defining the semantics as usual in DLs seems to be the most natural way of handling provenance in DL languages with conjunction.
Since
{\cal E\!L}
ontologies are often already expressed in normal form, the main restriction in our language is the avoidance of qualified existential restrictions on the right-hand side.

Semantics of non-Horn DLs.

It is not clear how to extend the semantics to a DL language featuring disjunction (
C\sqcup D
) and negation (
\neg C
) such as
\mathcal{ALC}
. Indeed, while it seems natural to define
(C\sqcup D)^{\mathcal{I}}=C^{\mathcal{I}}\cup D^{\mathcal{I}}
, so that, e.g.,
\{(C\sqcup D\sqsubseteq A,\kappa)\}
is equivalent to
\{(C\sqsubseteq A,\kappa),(D\sqsubseteq A,\kappa)\}
, and
\mathcal{I}\models(A\sqsubseteq C\sqcup D,\kappa)
iff
(e,\kappa^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
implies that either
(e,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
or
(e,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
, it is unclear how to define
(\neg C)^{\mathcal{I}}
. In particular, we would like that
(A\sqsubseteq C\sqcup D,\kappa)
is equivalent to
(A\sqcap\neg C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
, which would not hold if we define
(\neg C)^{\mathcal{I}}
by
(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times K)\setminus C^{\mathcal{I}}
.

3.2
Querying Annotated Ontologies

To define the provenance of (Boolean) CQs, we consider
extended CQs
, which replace concept and role predicates with binary and ternary predicates respectively, where the last term of the tuple is used for provenance information.
Given a CQ
q(\vec{x})=\exists\vec{y}\,\phi(\vec{x},\vec{y})
, its
extended version
is
\boldsymbol{q}(\vec{x})=\exists\vec{y}\exists\vec{z}\,\phi^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})
where
\vec{z}
is a tuple of variables disjoint from
\vec{x}\cup\vec{y}
and
\phi^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})
is obtained from
\phi(\vec{x},\vec{y})
by replacing each
A(t)
by
A(t,z)
and each
R(t,t^{\prime})
by
R(t,t^{\prime},z)
with
z\in\vec{z}
such that
z
does not occur anywhere else in
\phi^{\prime}(\vec{x},\vec{y},\vec{z})
.
We use
P(\vec{t},t)
to refer to an atom which is either
A(t_{1},t)

or
R(t_{1},t_{2},t)
, and
P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}
if
P(\vec{t},t)
occurs in
\boldsymbol{q}
.
A
\mathbb{K}
-annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}
satisfies an extended BCQ
\boldsymbol{q}:=\exists\vec{y}\exists\vec{z}\,\phi(\vec{y},\vec{z})
, written
\mathcal{I}\models\boldsymbol{q}
, iff there is a
match for
\boldsymbol{q}
in
\mathcal{I}
, where a match for
\boldsymbol{q}
in
\mathcal{I}
is a function
\pi:\mathsf{terms}(\boldsymbol{q})\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\cup K
such that
\pi(t)=t^{\mathcal{I}}
for every
t\in{\sf N_{I}}\cap\mathsf{terms}(\boldsymbol{q})
, and
\pi(\vec{t},t)\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
for every
P(\vec{t},t)\in\mathsf{atoms}(\boldsymbol{q})
, where
\pi(\vec{t},t)
is a shorthand for
(\pi(t_{1}),\pi(t))
or
(\pi(t_{1}),\pi(t_{2}),\pi(t))
depending on the arity of
P
.
An extended BCQ
\boldsymbol{q}
is
entailed
by an annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, written
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models\boldsymbol{q}
, if and only if
\mathcal{I}\models\boldsymbol{q}
for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
The following proposition is a direct consequence of the definition of (extended) BCQ entailment and Lemma
3.5
.

Proposition 3.8
.

For every annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and BCQ
q
,
\mathcal{O}\models q
iff
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models\boldsymbol{q}
where
\boldsymbol{q}
is the extended version of
q
.

Given an extended BCQ
\boldsymbol{q}
and a
\mathbb{K}
-annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}
, let
\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q})
denote
the set of all matches of
\boldsymbol{q}
in
\mathcal{I}
.
The
provenance annotations
of
\boldsymbol{q}
in
\mathcal{I}
is defined as the set

{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}}({\boldsymbol{q}})=\left\{\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi(t)\mid\pi\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q})\right\}
of elements of
\mathbb{K}
, where
\pi(t)
is the last element of the tuple
\pi(\vec{t},t)\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
(which is a semiring element).
We write
(q,\kappa)
to denote an
annotated BCQ
.
The semantics of annotated BCQs is as expected:

\mathcal{I}\models(q,\kappa)
if
\kappa\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}}({\boldsymbol{q}})
where
\boldsymbol{q}
is the extended version of
q
, and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(q,\kappa)

if
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
implies
\mathcal{I}\models(q,\kappa)
, for all
\mathbb{K}
-
annotated interpretations
\mathcal{I}
. If
\mathbb{K}
is complete (or
\mathbb{K}
is
\omega
-complete and
K
is countable), we define the
provenance
of
q
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}):=\bigoplus_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(q,\kappa)}\kappa.

When
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is satisfiable, it holds that
\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\bigoplus_{\kappa\in\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}}({\boldsymbol{q}})}\kappa
, while for an unsatisfiable
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\bigoplus_{\kappa\in K}\kappa
.

Example 3.9

(Example
3.3
continued)
.

Consider the extended version of the query that asks for the deities having some parent,
\boldsymbol{q}(x)=\exists yt_{1}t_{2}\,\mathsf{Deity}(x,t_{1})\wedge\mathsf{parent}(x,y,t_{2})
.

We saw in Example
3.3
that all models
\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
satisfy

(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{1})\in\mathsf{Deity}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,

(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2})\in\mathsf{Deity}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
, and

(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{5}x_{6}y_{1}y_{3})\in\mathsf{Deity}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
. In addition, it holds
that

(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},\mathsf{Demeter}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{3}y_{2})\in\mathsf{parent}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
and

(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},\mathsf{Zeus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{5}y_{3})\in\mathsf{parent}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
.
It also holds that
(\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},\mathsf{Semele}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}},x_{2}y_{2})\in\mathsf{parent}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
.
Hence, for every model
\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
, there are the following matches for
\boldsymbol{q}(\mathsf{Dionysus})
in
\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}
:

•

\pi_{1}(\mathsf{Dionysus})=\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{1}(y)=\mathsf{Semele}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{1}(t_{1})=x_{1}
,
\pi_{1}(t_{2})=x_{2}y_{2}
;

•

\pi_{2}(\mathsf{Dionysus})=\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{2}(y)=\mathsf{Demeter}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{2}(t_{1})=x_{1}
,
\pi_{2}(t_{2})=x_{3}y_{2}
;

•

\pi_{3}(\mathsf{Dionysus})=\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{3}(y)=\mathsf{Zeus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{3}(t_{1})=x_{1}
,
\pi_{3}(t_{2})=x_{5}y_{3}
;

•

\pi_{4}(\mathsf{Dionysus})=\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{4}(y)=\mathsf{Semele}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{4}(t_{1})=x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2}
,
\pi_{4}(t_{2})=x_{2}y_{2}
;

•

\pi_{5}(\mathsf{Dionysus})=\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{5}(y)=\mathsf{Demeter}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{5}(t_{1})=x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2}
,
\pi_{5}(t_{2})=x_{3}y_{2}
;

•

\pi_{6}(\mathsf{Dionysus})=\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{6}(y)=\mathsf{Zeus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{6}(t_{1})=x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2}
,
\pi_{6}(t_{2})=x_{5}y_{3}
;

•

\pi_{7}(\mathsf{Dionysus})=\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{7}(y)=\mathsf{Semele}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{7}(t_{1})=x_{5}x_{6}y_{1}y_{3}
,
\pi_{7}(t_{2})=x_{2}y_{2}
;

•

\pi_{8}(\mathsf{Dionysus})=\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{8}(y)=\mathsf{Demeter}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{8}(t_{1})=x_{5}x_{6}y_{1}y_{3}
,
\pi_{8}(t_{2})=x_{3}y_{2}
;

•

\pi_{9}(\mathsf{Dionysus})=\mathsf{Dionysus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{9}(y)=\mathsf{Zeus}^{\mathcal{I}^{\sf X}}
,
\pi_{9}(t_{1})=x_{5}x_{6}y_{1}y_{3}
,
\pi_{9}(t_{2})=x_{5}y_{3}
.

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(q(\mathsf{Dionysus}),\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]})={}

\displaystyle x_{1}x_{2}y_{2}+x_{1}x_{3}y_{2}+x_{1}x_{5}y_{3}+x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2}+x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2}+{}

\displaystyle x_{3}x_{4}x_{5}y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}+x_{2}x_{5}x_{6}y_{1}y_{3}y_{2}+x_{3}x_{5}x_{6}y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}+x_{5}x_{6}y_{1}y_{3}.

Recall that
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
is commutative and such that
x^{2}=x
for every
x\in{\sf X}
so we can drop the exponents (e.g. we can write the fourth term
x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2}
instead of
x_{3}x_{4}y_{1}y_{2}x_{2}y_{2}
).

We obtain the following results similarly, by considering the matches for
\boldsymbol{q}(\mathsf{Dionysus})
in the
\mathbb{T}
-annotated and
\mathbb{F}
-annotated interpretations that are models of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{T}}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}
respectively.

•

\mathcal{P}(q(\mathsf{Dionysus}),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{T}})=4
;

•

\mathcal{P}(q(\mathsf{Dionysus}),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})=0.9
. ∎

Given a possibly complex
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
C
, let
q_{C}(x)
be the rooted tree-shaped query that retrieves all instances of
C
(cf. Section
2.1
).
The following lemma
establishes the relationship between the answers of
\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)
and their provenance annotations in an interpretation and the interpretation of
C
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For every
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
C
, if
\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)
is the extended version of the rooted tree-shaped query
{q_{C}}(x)
, then
for every annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and for every
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, we have that
\{\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)}\pi(t)\mid\pi\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)),\pi(x)=d\}=\{\kappa\mid(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}
.

Theorem
3.11
establishes that computing the provenance of query answers for CQs that are tree-shaped and rooted can be reduced in polynomial time to computing the provenance of assertions, using the fact
that a rooted tree-shaped CQ can be seen as a syntactic variant of a possibly complex
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
C
(cf. Section
2.1
). The idea of the reduction is to introduce a fresh concept name
A_{C}
that subsumes
C
.
This theorem allows us to extend all results given for assertion entailment in this paper to rooted tree-shaped BCQ entailment.

Theorem 3.11
.

For every annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
C
and
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
,
we have
\mathcal{P}(q_{C}(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{P}(A_{C}(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\})
, where
A_{C}
is a fresh concept name.

3.3
Relationship with Semantics for Specific Annotations

We now discuss some weighted reasoning tasks which have been studied in the literature, and show how some of them fall
into our general approach.
Regarding the use of provenance as explanation, we will discuss in details in Section
7.1
the relationship between our provenance semantics and one of the most studied related problems in the DL community, axiom
pinpointing (?).

Fuzzy DL.

The original work on fuzzy DLs introduced by
? (?) considers fuzzy assertions of the
form
(\alpha,n)
where
n\in[0,1]
represents the membership degree for the assertion
\alpha
, and the
terminological axioms are all considered to be classical; that is, they are precise.
Fuzzy interpretations
generalise classical interpretations by considering concepts and roles as
fuzzy
unary and binary predicates,
respectively. More precisely, every concept name
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
is interpreted as a function
A^{\mathcal{I}}:\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\to[0,1]
and
likewise role names
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
are interpreted as (binary) functions
R^{\mathcal{I}}:\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\to[0,1]
.
Following the
then-popular Zadeh semantics (?), which for the scope of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
in the setting of
? coincides with the standard Gödel semantics of mathematical fuzzy logic (?), conjunctions
are interpreted through the minimum (
\min
) operator over the interval
[0,1]
, while disjunctions and existential
restrictions are based on the maximum (
\max
) operator.
A GCI
C\sqsubseteq D
is satisfied by a fuzzy interpretation
\mathcal{I}
if the membership degree for
C
is less than or
equal to the degree for
D
at every domain element; i.e., if for every

d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
it holds that
C^{\mathcal{I}}(d)\leq D^{\mathcal{I}}(d)
,
and a fuzzy assertion
(A(a),n)
is satisfied by
\mathcal{I}
if
A^{\mathcal{I}}(a^{\mathcal{I}})\geq n
.
The entailment relation in this logic is defined in the standard manner: given a fuzzy ontology

\mathcal{O}^{f}
, we get that
\mathcal{O}^{f}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\approx$}}(C(a),n)
iff
C^{\mathcal{I}}(a^{\mathcal{I}})\geq n
in every fuzzy
model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{f}
.
The general notions have later been extended to consider also membership degrees in the
TBox (?) and reasoning tasks such as query answering (?, ?).
In such a setting, ? (?) showed that for DL-Lite and BCQs,
\mathcal{O}^{f}\mathrel{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\shortmid$}\mkern-3.1mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\approx$}}(\alpha,n)
iff

\mathcal{O}_{\geq n}\models\alpha
where
\mathcal{O}_{\geq n}
is the classical ontology—called the
n
-cut
—containing only those axioms of
\mathcal{O}^{f}
annotated with a degree greater or equal to
n
.
Hence, the next proposition and Theorem 19 by ? (?) allow us to reduce BCQ (hence also assertion) entailments over fuzzy DL-Lite ontologies to computation of provenance in the fuzzy semiring.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
\mathbb{F}=([0,1],\max,\min,0,1)
be the fuzzy semiring. For every satisfiable
\mathbb{F}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}
and
n\in[0,1]
,

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})\geq n\text{ iff }\mathcal{O}_{\geq n}\models\alpha\quad\text{holds if:}

1.

\alpha
is a BCQ, an assertion, or an RI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
; or

2.

\alpha
is a GCI between basic concepts whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side.

The following example shows that we cannot extend the proposition to GCIs or RIs with unsatisfiable left-hand side.

Example 3.13
.

If
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}=\{(A\sqsubseteq B,0.5),(A\sqsubseteq C,0.5),(B\sqcap C\sqsubseteq\bot,0.9)\}
, then
we have
\mathcal{O}_{\geq 0.9}\not\models A\sqsubseteq D
for every
D\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\{A\}
; but
since
A
is unsatisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
, it follows that
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq D,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})=1

(cf. Remark
3.2
). Note that one can replace concept names by role names in this example.
∎

The reason for the requirement on the GCIs of
\mathcal{O}
in the case where
\alpha
is a GCI is illustrated below.

Example 3.14
.

If
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}=\{(\top\sqsubseteq D,1)\}
, then
\mathcal{O}_{\geq 1}\models C\sqsubseteq D
for every
C\in{\sf N_{C}}
, but for every
C\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\{D\}
,
\mathcal{P}(C\sqsubseteq D,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})=0
. Indeed, the annotated model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}
defined by
D^{\mathcal{I}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{1\}
and
C^{\mathcal{I}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{0.5\}
does not satisfy
(C\sqsubseteq D,n)
for any
n\in[0,1]
.
∎

The
n
-cuts are widely used in the fuzzy logic community. In the fuzzy DL literature, it is more common to use a
method called
crispification
in which different concept names are used in a classical interpretation to represent the
truth degrees assigned by a fuzzy interpretation (?, ?, ?).
While crispification allows for a more fine-grained semantic analysis in general, by “cutting” also the interpretations at
the desired degree
n
, one obtains an interpretation which is a model of the
n
-cut whenever the fuzzy interpretation
is a model of the original ontology, thus preserving the result.

Possibilistic DL.

? (?) originally introduced a possibilistic extension of the DL
\mathcal{ALCN}
by annotating each axiom with either a
necessity
or a
possibility
value but most recent works (covering various DLs such as
\mathcal{ALC}
or DL-Lite) only consider one kind of annotation, which corresponds to necessity value and is also called certainty or confidence degree (?, ?).
In this context, a possibilistic ontology
\mathcal{O}^{p}
is a set of annotated axioms
(\alpha,n)
with
n\in]0,1]
.
The semantics is based on a possibility distribution
\pi_{\mathcal{O}^{p}}
over all the possible interpretations
\mathcal{I}
, which depends on the degrees of the axioms not satisfied by
\mathcal{I}
:
\pi_{\mathcal{O}^{p}}(\mathcal{I})=1
if
\mathcal{I}\models\alpha
for every
(\alpha,n)\in{\mathcal{O}^{p}}
, and
\pi_{\mathcal{O}^{p}}(\mathcal{I})=\min\{1-n\mid(\alpha,n)\in\mathcal{O}^{p},\mathcal{I}\not\models\alpha\}
otherwise. For a
satisfiable
ontology,
\mathcal{O}^{p}\models_{\pi}(\alpha,n)
iff
n\geq 1-\sup\{\pi_{\mathcal{O}^{p}}(\mathcal{I})\mid\mathcal{I}\not\models\alpha\}
.
Although at first sight this semantics does not exactly fit our definition, it has been shown that reasoning in possibilistic DLs can be reduced to classical reasoning over
n
-cuts, where the
n
-cut
\mathcal{O}_{\geq n}
of
\mathcal{O}^{p}
is defined as in the fuzzy case:
\mathcal{O}^{p}\models_{\pi}(\alpha,n)
iff
\mathcal{O}_{\geq n}\models\alpha
(?, Section 2.2). This comes from a general result on possibilistic first-order logic knowledge bases (?, Proposition 11).
A tight necessity degree can be computed by taking the maximum over
n
such that
\mathcal{O}_{\geq n}\models\alpha
.
Hence, by Proposition
3.12
, possibilistic reasoning can be reduced
to provenance computation over the
fuzzy semiring.

The same is not true for the
product-based
variant of possibilistic semantics, where
\pi_{\mathcal{O}^{p}}(\mathcal{I})=1
if
\mathcal{I}\models\alpha
for every
(\alpha,n)\in{\mathcal{O}^{p}}
, and
\pi_{\mathcal{O}^{p}}(\mathcal{I})=\Pi_{(\alpha,n)\in\mathcal{O}^{p},\mathcal{I}\not\models\alpha}(1-n)
otherwise (?). If
\mathcal{O}^{p}=\{(A(a),0.5),(B(a),0.5),(A\sqsubseteq C,1),(B\sqsubseteq C,1)\}
, then
\mathcal{O}^{p}\models_{\pi}(C(a),0.75)
under this semantics while
\mathcal{O}_{\geq 0.75}\not\models C(a)
. Actually, for every commutative semiring
\mathbb{K}=([0,1],\oplus,\otimes,0,1)
, if
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is
\mathcal{O}^{p}
interpreted as a
\mathbb{K}
-annotated ontology,
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=0.5
(since
0.5\otimes 1=0.5
and there is an annotated model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that
C^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},0.5\otimes 1)\}
).

Possibilistic semantics often target reasoning with unsatisfiable ontologies, by using the inconsistency degree
1-\sup\{\pi_{\mathcal{O}^{p}}(\mathcal{I})\}
to define
\mathcal{O}^{p}\models_{\pi}(\alpha,n)
when the ontology may be unsatisfiable.
Since the provenance of any axiom over an unsatisfiable ontology is the sum of all the semiring elements, we restrict our comparison to the case of satisfiable ontologies.

Access control.

? (?) proposed to annotate axioms in an ontology with a label belonging
to a so-called
access lattice

(L,\leq)
, which can be seen as a generalization of the dual of the access control semiring of Example
2.3
. In this context, a clearance level
\ell\in L
gives access to all axioms of greater level in the annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{a}
, i.e. to
\mathcal{O}_{\geq\ell}=\{\alpha\mid(\alpha,v)\in\mathcal{O}^{a},v\geq\ell\}
. Contrary to the access control semiring, clearance levels may be incomparable. Recall that a lattice is a partially ordered set in which every set of elements has a unique supremum (join) and a unique infimum (meet).
Given an ontology with access levels
\mathcal{O}^{a}
and an axiom
\alpha
,
\ell\in L
is called an
(\mathcal{O}^{a},\alpha)
-boundary if for every
\ell^{\prime}\in L
that is join prime relative to the set of labels that annotate
\mathcal{O}^{a}
, it holds that
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
iff
\mathcal{O}_{\geq\ell^{\prime}}\models\alpha
. Each ontology defines a lattice element
computed as the meet of the labels of its axioms. One of the main results of ? (?) (Theorem 4.3) shows that a boundary of
\alpha
is the join of the labels of all subontologies that entail
\alpha
: formally,
\sup\{\inf_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}(\lambda(\beta))\mid\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O},\mathcal{M}\models\alpha\}
is an
(\mathcal{O}^{a},\alpha)
-boundary. A bounded distributive lattice
(L,\leq)
is a lattice such that the operations of join and meet distribute over each other and there exists a greatest element
\top
and a least element
\bot
such that
\bot\leq\ell\leq\top
for every
\ell\in L
. It naturally corresponds to a semiring
\mathbb{L_{A}}=(L,\sup,\inf,\bot,\top)
where both operations (the join (
\sup
) and meet (
\inf
) of the lattice) are idempotent. Proposition
3.15
relates the
(\mathcal{O}^{a},\alpha)
-boundary given by ? (?) and the provenance of
\alpha
in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{L_{A}}}
, where
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{L_{A}}}
is
\mathcal{O}^{a}
interpreted as an
\mathbb{L_{A}}
-annotated ontology, with the same restrictions as Proposition
3.12
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
(L,\leq)
be a bounded distributive lattice and
\mathbb{L_{A}}=(L,\sup,\inf,\bot,\top)
. For every satisfiable
\mathbb{L_{A}}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{L_{A}}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
,

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{L_{A}}})=\sup\{\inf_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}(\lambda(\beta))\mid\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O},\mathcal{M}\models\alpha\}\quad\text{holds if:}

1.

\alpha
is a BCQ, an assertion, or an RI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
; or

2.

\alpha
is a GCI between basic concepts whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side.

Bag semantics.

? (?, ?) defined a bag semantics for
DL-Lite
R
ontologies whose assertions are annotated by multiplicities from
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
The semantics is based on bag interpretations, which are very similar to fuzzy interpretations, except that concept and role names are interpreted by bags
A^{\mathcal{I}}:\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and
R^{\mathcal{I}}:\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
The interpretation of inverse roles is as expected and
(\exists R)^{\mathcal{I}}
maps each
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
to
\Sigma_{e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}R^{\mathcal{I}}(d,e)
.
A bag interpretation
\mathcal{I}
satisfies an assertion
A(a)
with multiplicity
n
if
A^{\mathcal{I}}(a^{\mathcal{I}})\geq n
and a GCI
C\sqsubseteq D
if
C^{\mathcal{I}}(d)\leq D^{\mathcal{I}}(d)
for every
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Given a bag ontology
\mathcal{O}^{b}
, entailment of an assertion
A(a)
under bag certain semantics is defined by
\mathcal{O}^{b}\models^{b}(A(a),n)
if
n
is the minimum
A^{\mathcal{I}}(a^{\mathcal{I}})
over all bag models of
\mathcal{O}^{b}
. This semantics is not captured by provenance in
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}=(\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\},+,\times,0,1)
. Consider
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{b}=\{(A(a),2),(B(a),3),(A\sqsubseteq C,1),(B\sqsubseteq C,1)\}
. It holds that

\mathcal{O}_{1}^{b}\models^{b}(C(a),3)
while if
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}}}
is
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{b}
interpreted as a
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
-annotated ontology,
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}_{1}^{{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}}})=5
(since every annotated model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}}
is such that
\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},2),(a^{\mathcal{I}},3)\}\subseteq C^{\mathcal{I}}
).
Note that replacing
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
by
\mathbb{K}=(\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\},\max,\times,0,1)
does not capture the bag semantics either. While we would have
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{K}})=3
, if we consider
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{b}=\{(R(a,b),2),(R(a,c),3),(\exists R\sqsubseteq C,1)\}
, then
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{b}\models^{b}(C(a),5)
but
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}_{2}^{\mathbb{K}})=3
.
Actually, for every
\mathbb{K}=(\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\},\oplus,\otimes,0,1)
, we can see that for both
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{\mathbb{K}}
, every annotated model
\mathcal{I}
is such that
\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},2),(a^{\mathcal{I}},3)\}\subseteq C^{\mathcal{I}}
(since
n\otimes 1=n
), so that
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}_{2}^{\mathbb{K}})=2\oplus 3
, while the multiplicity of
C(a)
under the bag semantics is not the same in
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{b}
and in
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{b}
. One could modify the provenance semantics to capture bag semantics by adapting the so-called annotated model-based semantics for Datalog defined by ? (?) for a restricted class of semirings, but as already discussed in Section
3.1.3
, both semantics suffer from undesirable behaviors when
\oplus
is not idempotent and coincide otherwise.

4
Extension of Results from Classical DL

In this section, we investigate under which conditions some classical results for
DLs of the
{\cal E\!L}
family
can be transferred to
\mathbb{K}
-annotated ontologies.

4.1
Normal Form

In DL, it is customary to consider ontologies in
normal form
when working with languages from the
{\cal E\!L}
family, to simplify the reasoning methods. We can do the same thing in the annotated case. An annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is in
normal form
if for every GCI
(\alpha,\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
\alpha
is of one of the forms

A\sqsubseteq B,\ A\sqcap A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq B,\ A\sqsubseteq\exists R,\ A\sqsubseteq\exists R^{-},\ \exists R.A\sqsubseteq B,\text{\ or \ }\exists R^{-}.A\sqsubseteq B

with
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
,
A,A^{\prime}\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
,
B\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\bot\}
. Every annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}

can be transformed, in polynomial time, into an ontology
\mathsf{NF}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
in normal form, which entails the same annotated axioms as
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
over the signature of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
\mathsf{NF}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
is built by applying exhaustively the following rules, where
\widehat{C},\widehat{D}\notin{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
and
A
is a fresh concept name.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ }r@{\ }l@{\ }l}\mathsf{NF}_{1}:&(C\sqcap\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa)&\longrightarrow&(\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),(C\sqcap A\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa)\\
\mathsf{NF}_{2}:&(\widehat{C}\sqcap D\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa)&\longrightarrow&(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),(A\sqcap D\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa)\\
\mathsf{NF}_{3}:&(\exists P.\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa)&\longrightarrow&(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),(\exists P.A\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa)\\
\mathsf{NF}_{4}:&(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa)&\longrightarrow&(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa)\end{array}

Note that
\mathsf{NF}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
does not contain any pair of annotated axioms
(\alpha,\kappa)
,
(\alpha,\kappa^{\prime})
with
\kappa\neq\kappa^{\prime}
, so

\mathsf{NF}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
is a proper annotated ontology. Indeed, observe that each fresh concept name
A
introduced by applying a normalization rule occurs (i) in the left-hand side of a single annotated axiom (built by the rule application that introduced
A
and in normal form), and (ii) in the right-hand side of possibly several annotated axioms (obtained by normalizing the axiom
(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1})
built by the rule application that introduced
A
) which are all annotated by
\mathbb{1}
, so the same axiom cannot be annotated with several elements of
K
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative semiring,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
a
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology,
\alpha
an axiom, and
\kappa\in K
.
Let
\mathsf{NF}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
be obtained by applying exhaustively Rules
\mathsf{NF}_{1}
-
\mathsf{NF}_{4}
to
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

•

If
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
, then
\mathsf{NF}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})\models(\alpha,\kappa)
.

•

If
\mathsf{NF}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})\models(\alpha,\kappa)
and every concept name occurring in
\alpha
also occurs in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, then
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
.

Corollary 4.2
.

If
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathsf{NF}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
are as in Theorem
4.1
, then for every
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
axiom
\alpha
over the signature of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathsf{NF}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}))
.

4.2
Canonical Model

In DLs of the
{\cal E\!L}
family, every ontology has a
canonical (or universal) model
which can be homomorphically mapped into any other model of the ontology and is useful to demonstrate results (see ?, Section 3.2, for a definition of the canonical model of an
{\cal E\!L}
ontology). In particular, a BCQ is entailed by an ontology if and only if it holds in its canonical model. Such a model can be constructed via a forward chaining procedure similar to the
oblivious chase
for databases (?).
We adapt the construction of the canonical model of
{\cal E\!L}
ontologies to annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontologies (adapting to the DL context the definition of the oblivious chase as given by ?).

Given an annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
that is satisfiable, we define a canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
as follows.
Let
\mathcal{I}_{0}
be the
\mathbb{K}
-annotated interpretation such that:

•

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}:={\sf N_{I}}
;

•

a^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}:=a
,
for all
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
;

•

(a,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
iff

(A(a),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
;

•

(a,b,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
iff

(R(a,b),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

The following “chase rule” defines an annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}_{n+1}
from
\mathcal{I}_{n}
. Choose
(\alpha,\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
then

•

if
(\alpha,\kappa)=({P\sqsubseteq Q},{\kappa})
, choose
(d,d^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
and let
Q^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}=Q^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\cup\{(d,d^{\prime},\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\}
;

•

if
(\alpha,\kappa)=({C\sqsubseteq A},{\kappa})
, choose
(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
and let
A^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\cup\{(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\}
;

•

if
(\alpha,\kappa)=({C\sqsubseteq\exists P},{\kappa})
, choose
(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
and let
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}:=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\cup\{d_{f}\}
with
d_{f}
a fresh element, and

P^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}=P^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\cup\{(d,d_{f},\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\}
. Note that
P
can be a role name or an inverse role.

There exists a (potentially infinite) sequence
\mathcal{I}_{0},\mathcal{I}_{1},\dots
such that

(i)

for every
i\geq 0
,
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}_{i}
by applying the chase rule by choosing
(\alpha_{i},\kappa_{i})\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
(\vec{d}_{i},\kappa^{\prime}_{i})\in E^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
(with
E
the concept or role in the left-hand side of
\alpha_{i}
),

(ii)

for every
i,j\geq 0
such that
i\neq j
,
(\alpha_{i},\kappa_{i})=(\alpha_{j},\kappa_{j})
implies that
(\vec{d}_{i},\kappa^{\prime}_{i})\neq(\vec{d}_{j},\kappa^{\prime}_{j})
(the rule is not applied twice with the same choice of
(\alpha,\kappa)
and
(\vec{d},\kappa^{\prime})
), and

(iii)

for every
i\geq 0
if there is
(\alpha_{i},\kappa_{i})\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
(\vec{d}_{i},\kappa^{\prime}_{i})\in E^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
, then there is
j\geq 0
such that
\mathcal{I}_{j+1}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}_{j}
by applying the chase rule with
(\alpha_{i},\kappa_{i})\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
(\vec{d}_{i},\kappa^{\prime}_{i})\in E^{\mathcal{I}_{j}}
(fairness condition).

Existence of such a sequence can be shown by applying the rule in a breadth-first fashion (see ?, for full details).
We define
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}:=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{I}_{n}
, i.e.
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
; for every
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
,
a^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}=a
; for every
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
,
A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}A^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
; and for every
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
,
R^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}R^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
. Even if there may be different sequences
\mathcal{I}_{0},\mathcal{I}_{1},\dots
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is unique up to renaming of the fresh domain elements introduced (by the same arguments that show uniqueness of the oblivious chase, ?), which allows us to talk about
the
canonical model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
Proposition
4.3
is an easy consequence of the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
.

Proposition 4.3
.

If
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is a satisfiable
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology then
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},K,\cdot^{\mathcal{I}})
and
\mathcal{J}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}},K,\cdot^{\mathcal{J}})
be
\mathbb{K}
-annotated interpretations and
d,e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.
A
homomorphism

g:\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
is a function from
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
to
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
such that:

•

for all individual names
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
,
g(a^{\mathcal{I}})=a^{\mathcal{J}}
;

•

for all concept names
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
, if
(d,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
then
(g(d),\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
;

•

for all role names
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
, if
(d,e,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
then
(g(d),g(e),\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
.

Lemma
4.4
states that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
has the usual property of canonical models.

Lemma 4.4
.

For every model
\mathcal{I}
of a satisfiable
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
there is a homomorphism

g:\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
.

Theorem
4.5
states that the canonical model behaves as expected for annotated assertions and BCQs entailment (note that annotated BCQs actually subsume annotated assertions, so that the two first points are consequences of the last one).

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative semiring. For every satisfiable
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
the following hold:

•

for every
\kappa\in K
,

a\in{\sf N_{I}}
, and
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(A(a),\kappa)
iff
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(A(a),\kappa)
;

•

for every
\kappa\in K
,
a,b\in{\sf N_{I}}
, and
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(R(a,b),\kappa)
iff
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(R(a,b),\kappa)
;

•

for every
\kappa\in K
and
BCQ
q
,

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(q,\kappa)
iff
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(q,\kappa)
.

In the classical
{\cal E\!L}
family,
one can define a canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}}
of a concept
C
and an ontology
\mathcal{O}
such that if
C
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
(i.e. there exists a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
such that
C^{\mathcal{I}}\neq\emptyset
), then for every concept
D
,
\mathcal{O}\models C\sqsubseteq D
iff
d_{C}\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}}}
where
d_{C}
is a distinguished domain element that “represents”
C
(see e.g. ?). Intuitively,
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}}
is built as the canonical model of
\mathcal{O}
but starts from a modified
\mathcal{I}_{0}
which is such that
d_{C}\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
. For example, if
C=A\sqcap\exists R.B
,
d_{C}
is added to
A^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
,
(d_{C},x_{RB})
to
R^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
and
x_{RB}
to
B^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
.
Example
4.6
illustrates the difficulty to extend this result to the annotated case, i.e. obtain a canonical model
\mathcal{J}
of
C
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that
(d_{C},\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
iff
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
.

Example 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}=\{(A\sqsubseteq D,x)\}
and
B\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\{A\}
.
One can show that there is no
\kappa\in\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
such that
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}\models(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
. Indeed, the
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}
below is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}
and there is no
\kappa
such that
x=\kappa\times y
.

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,1)\}\quad B^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,y)\}\quad(A\sqcap B)^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,y)\}\quad D^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,x)\}

However, if we try to define a canonical model
\mathcal{J}
of
A\sqcap B
and
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}
in a natural way by setting
A^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}=\{(d_{A\sqcap B},1)\}
,
B^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}=\{(d_{A\sqcap B},1)\}
and applying the chase rule, we obtain
D^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(d_{A\sqcap B},x)\}
.
Actually, for every annotated model
\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}
, if there is
(d,\chi)\in(A\sqcap B)^{\mathcal{J}}
as we would expect for a representative
d
of
A\sqcap B
, there must be some
(d,\chi^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
so that
(d,\chi^{\prime}\times x)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
, while
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}\not\models(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq D,\chi^{\prime}\times x)
.
∎

We thus restrict our attention to
basic concepts
(i.e. concept names and concepts of the form
\exists P
). For such a basic concept
C
, we define the canonical model

\mathcal{I}_{C}
as the interpretation
with domain
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{C}}:=\{d_{C}\}\cup{\sf N_{I}}
if
C
is a concept name, and
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{C}}:=\{d_{C},d_{f}\}\cup{\sf N_{I}}
otherwise, such that
a^{\mathcal{I}_{C}}:=a
for all

a\in{\sf N_{I}}
,

A^{\mathcal{I}_{C}}:=\{(d_{A},\mathbb{1})\}
if
C=A\in{\sf N_{C}}
,

R^{\mathcal{I}_{C}}:=\{(d_{\exists R},d_{f},\mathbb{1})\}
if
C=\exists R
, and
R^{\mathcal{I}_{C}}:=\{(d_{f},d_{\exists R^{-}},\mathbb{1})\}
if
C=\exists R^{-}
, and all other concept and role names are mapped to the empty set.
The canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
for a basic concept
C
and a

\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}

such that
C
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is defined in the same way as the canonical model for
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}

except that
\mathcal{I}_{0}
starts with
\mathcal{I}_{C}
(and assertions if they are present in the ontology).

Theorem 4.7
.

Assume
\mathbb{K}
is a commutative
\otimes
-idempotent semiring.
Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}

be a satisfiable
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
such that
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side,
and let
C,D
be
basic
concepts such that
C
is satisfiable
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
Then,
for every
\kappa\in K
,
(d_{C},\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
iff
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
.

Note that
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
implies
(d_{C},\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
even without the restrictions on
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathbb{K}
, since
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. However, the other direction of Theorem
4.7
does not hold
if
\mathcal{O}
contains some GCI with
\top
as its left-hand side; for example, if
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(\top\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)\}
(since in this case one can show that
\mathcal{O}\not\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
for
C\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\{D\}
as we did in Example
3.14
). Example
4.8
shows that the
\otimes
-idempotency condition is also necessary.

Example 4.8
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}=\{(A\sqsubseteq B_{1},1),(A\sqsubseteq B_{2},1),(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C,1)\}
.
It is easy to see that
(d_{A},1)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{A,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}}}
.
However, there is no
\kappa\in\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
such that
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\models(A\sqsubseteq C,\kappa)
.
Indeed, the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}
below is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
and there is no
\kappa
such that
x+y+xy=\kappa\times(x+y)
.

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}}=

\displaystyle\{(e,x+y)\}\quad B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,x+y)\}\quad B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,x+y)\}\quad C^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,x+y+xy)\}

Intuitively, this is because when the semiring is not multiplicatively idempotent,
A\sqcap A
and
A
are not equivalent:
{\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}}\models(A\sqcap A\sqsubseteq C,1)
but
{\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}}\not\models(A\sqsubseteq C,1)
.
∎

Finally, we briefly show how role inclusions can be captured using a canonical model. For a role
P
(with
P\in{\sf N_{R}}
or
P=R^{-}
for some
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
), we define
\mathcal{I}_{P}
as the interpretation with domain
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{P}}:=\{d_{1},d_{2}\}\cup{\sf N_{I}}
such that
a^{\mathcal{I}_{P}}:=a
for all
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
,
P^{\mathcal{I}_{P}}:=\{(d_{1},d_{2},\mathbb{1})\}
, and all other concept and role names mapped to the empty set. The canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{P,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
for a role
P
and a

\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}

such that
P
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
(i.e. there exists a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that
P^{\mathcal{I}}\neq\emptyset
) is defined in the same way as the canonical model for
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}

except that
\mathcal{I}_{0}
starts with
\mathcal{I}_{P}
(and assertions if they are present in the ontology).
Contrary to the GCI case, we do not need the semiring to be
\otimes
-idempotent to obtain the following result.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative semiring.
Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}

be a satisfiable
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
and let
P,Q
be two roles such that
P
is satisfiable
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
Then,
for every
\kappa\in K
,
(d_{1},d_{2},\kappa)\in Q^{\mathcal{I}_{P,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
iff
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(P\sqsubseteq Q,\kappa)
.

4.3
Reduction Between Assertion and GCI or RI Entailment

It is well-known that in
{\cal E\!LHI}_{\bot}
, GCI or RI entailment can be reduced to assertion entailment in polynomial time, and reciprocally.
In the case of
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontologies, we obtain similar reductions, with a restriction on the semiring in the case of GCI entailment.

Theorem 4.10
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a commutative
\otimes
-idempotent semiring then, for every
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
such that
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, the following hold.

•

For every GCI between basic concepts
C\sqsubseteq D
and
\kappa_{0}\in K
,

{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa_{0})\text{ iff }{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}\models(E(a_{0}),\kappa_{0})

where
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}=\{(D\sqsubseteq E,\mathbb{1})\}
,

\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}=\{(C(a_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}
if
C\in{\sf N_{C}}
,
and
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}=\{(P(a_{0},b_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}
if
C=\exists P
(where
P(a_{0},b_{0})
denotes
R(b_{0},a_{0})
if
P=R^{-}
),
with
a_{0},b_{0}\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
E\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

For every concept assertion
B(a_{0})
and
\kappa_{0}\in K
,

{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(B(a_{0}),\kappa_{0})\text{ iff }\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa_{0})

where
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
is defined as follows (assuming that for all
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
,
C_{a}
and
R_{ab}
are fresh concept and role names respectively):

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\bigcup_{a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C_{a}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C_{a}}=

\displaystyle\{(C_{a}\sqsubseteq A,\kappa)\mid(A(a),\kappa)\in{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\}\ \cup

\displaystyle\{(R_{ab}\sqsubseteq R,\kappa),(C_{a}\equiv\exists R_{ab},\mathbb{1}),(C_{b}\equiv\exists R_{ab}^{-},\mathbb{1})\mid(R(a,b),\kappa)\in{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\}.

The reason for the restriction of the use of
\top
in the left-hand side of GCIs is the same as in Theorem
4.7
: if
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(\top\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)\}
, it holds that
\mathcal{O}\not\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
for every
C\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\{D\}
but
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}\models(E(a_{0}),\kappa)
because in every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}
,
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\in\top^{\mathcal{I}}
so
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq E^{\mathcal{I}}
.
The annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
defined in Example
4.8
shows that the
\otimes
-idempotency condition is necessary for the first item of the theorem:
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\not\models(A\sqsubseteq C,1)
while
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq E,1)\}\cup\{(A(a_{0}),1)\}\models(E(a_{0}),1)
. For the second item, we can consider
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\cup\{(A(a_{0}),1)\}
and check that it entails
(C(a_{0}),1)
while
\mathcal{T}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}=\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\cup\{(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq A,1)\}
does not entail
(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq C,1)
.
However, the idempotency requirement can be lifted for RIs.

Theorem 4.11
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a commutative semiring then, for every
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
, the following hold.

•

For every positive role inclusion
P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2}
and
\kappa_{0}\in K
,

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}{\models}(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{0})\text{ iff }\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(P_{1}(a_{0},b_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}{\models}(P_{2}(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})

where
a_{0},b_{0}\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
P_{i}(a_{0},b_{0})
denotes
R(b_{0},a_{0})
if
P_{i}=R^{-}
.

•

For every role assertion
R(a_{0},b_{0})
and
\kappa_{0}\in K
,

{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(R(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})\text{ iff }\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}\models(S\sqsubseteq R,\kappa_{0})

where
S\in{\sf N_{R}}\setminus\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(S\sqsubseteq R^{\prime},\kappa)\mid(R^{\prime}(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\}\cup{}

\displaystyle\{(S\sqsubseteq R^{\prime-},\kappa)\mid(R^{\prime}(b_{0},a_{0}),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\}.

5
Properties of the Provenance Semantics

Inspired by the generic definition of a provenance semantics for Datalog queries and properties proposed by ? (?, Definition 3 and Properties 1 and 3), we show in this section
that under some restrictions on the semiring, our semiring
provenance definition satisfies properties that are expected for a provenance semantics.

5.1
Preservation of Entailment

Theorem
5.1
ensures that the semantics reflects the entailment or non-entailment of axioms and queries from the non-annotated ontology.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative complete semiring (or
\omega
-complete with
K
countable). For every
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
and every

\alpha
that is an assertion, a BCQ, an RI, or a GCI between basic concepts:

•

\mathcal{O}\not\models\alpha
implies
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathbb{0}
;

•

if
\mathbb{K}
is positive,
\alpha
is an assertion, a BCQ, or an RI, and
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathbb{0}
, then
\mathcal{O}\not\models\alpha
;

•

if
\mathbb{K}
is positive and
\otimes
-idempotent,
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, and
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathbb{0}
, then
\mathcal{O}\not\models\alpha
.

Unfortunately, the third point of Theorem
5.1
does not hold if
\alpha
is a GCI with a complex concept on its left-hand side, as we can see on Example
5.2
.

Example 5.2
.

Consider
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}=\{(A\sqsubseteq D,x)\}
. We have already shown
(Example
4.6
) that there is no
\kappa\in\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
such that
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}\models(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
. Hence it follows that
\mathcal{P}(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq D,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]})=0
, while
\mathcal{O}\models A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq D
and
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
is positive and idempotent.
∎

The reason for the requirement on GCIs without
\top
as left-hand side in the third point is the same explained
in the previous section: if
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(\top\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)\}
, then
\mathcal{O}\models C\sqsubseteq D
for every
C\in{\sf N_{C}}
, but for every
C\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\{D\}
, it may be the case that
\mathcal{P}(C\sqsubseteq D,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathbb{0}
(cf. Example
3.14
).
The next example illustrates the impact of
\otimes
-idempotency
in the case where
\alpha
is a GCI.

Example 5.3
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a
\otimes
-idempotent commutative semiring and consider the ontology

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(A\sqsubseteq B_{1},\kappa_{1}),(A\sqsubseteq B_{2},\kappa_{2}),(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C,\kappa_{3})\}.

If
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and

(e,\epsilon)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
(e,\epsilon\otimes\kappa_{1})\in B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(e,\epsilon\otimes\kappa_{2})\in B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
so
(e,\epsilon\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\epsilon\otimes\kappa_{2})\in(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2})^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.
(e,\epsilon\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2})^{\mathcal{I}}
by
\otimes
-idempotency, which implies
(e,\epsilon\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes\kappa_{3})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Thus we get that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(A\sqsubseteq C,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes\kappa_{3})
and
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes\kappa_{3}
.

We have seen in Example
4.8
that this intuitive behavior is lost if we consider the
semiring
\mathbb{K}=\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
and
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=\kappa_{3}=1
, since in this case there is no
\kappa\in\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
such that
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\models(A\sqsubseteq C,\kappa)
, so that
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]})=0
.
∎

Requiring
\otimes
-idempotency disregards the number of times an axiom is used in a derivation.
Consider the ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]}=\{(A\sqsubseteq B,x_{1}),(B\sqsubseteq A,x_{2})\}
.
If
\mathit{Prov}[{\sf X}]=\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
, which is not
\otimes
-idempotent,

\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq B,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket})=\sum_{n\geq 0}x_{1}(x_{1}x_{2})^{n}
, while if we consider the
\otimes
-idempotent semirings

\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
or
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
, we obtain
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq B,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]})=x_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq B,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]})=x_{1}x_{2}
respectively.

5.2
Consistency with Semiring Provenance for UCQs

Theorems
5.4
and
5.6
ensure that our notion of provenance is
consistent with the one defined for relational algebra queries over annotated databases.
Given an annotated set of facts
\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{D},\lambda\rangle
and a Boolean UCQ

q=\exists\vec{x}\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(\vec{x})
, we denote the relational provenance of
q
over

\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}}
seen as an annotated database by

\mathcal{P}_{DB}(q,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\bigoplus_{\pi\in\Pi(\varphi_{i},\mathcal{D})}\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t})\in\varphi_{i}}\lambda(\pi(P(\vec{t}))),

where
\Pi(\varphi_{i},\mathcal{D})
is the set of matches of
\varphi_{i}
in
\mathcal{D}
(?). Note that in this section we do not need to assume that the semiring is (
\omega
-)complete since the number of matches in
\mathcal{D}
is finite.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative
\oplus
-
idempotent semiring. For every
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
containing only assertions (i.e. being an annotated set of facts), and every
BCQ
q
,
\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{P}_{DB}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
.

The proof of Theorem
5.4
is based on the facts that (i) when
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is a set of annotated assertions, the canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
is simply the model with domain
{\sf N_{I}}
that satisfies exactly these assertions, so the matches for
q
in
\mathcal{O}
correspond with the matches of the extended version
\boldsymbol{q}
of
q
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
, and (ii)
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(q,\kappa)
iff
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(q,\kappa)
(by Theorem
4.5
).
Example
5.5
shows that
\oplus
-idempotency is a necessary condition of Theorem
5.4
.

Example 5.5
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative semiring which is not
\oplus
-
idempotent and
\kappa\in K
such that
\kappa\oplus\kappa\neq\kappa
.
Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(A(a),\kappa),(A(b),\kappa)\}
and
q=\exists y\,A(y)
.
There are exactly two matches for the extended version
\boldsymbol{q}=\exists yt\,A(y,t)
of
q
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
:
\pi_{1}(y)=a
and
\pi_{1}(t)=\kappa
, and
\pi_{2}(y)=b
and
\pi_{2}(t)=\kappa
.
Hence
\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\bigoplus_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(q,\chi)}\chi=\bigoplus_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(q,\chi)}\chi=\kappa
, which is different from the relational provenance of
q
over
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{DB}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\kappa\oplus\kappa
.

Theorem
5.4
does not hold when
\oplus
is not idempotent even if all
assertions have distinct annotations. Consider

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket}=\{(A(a),x_{1}),(A(b),x_{2})\}
and
q=\exists yz\,A(y)\land A(z)
.
There are exactly four matches for the extended version
\boldsymbol{q}=\exists yzt_{1}t_{2}\,A(y,t_{1})\wedge A(z,t_{2})
of
q
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket}}
, obtained by mapping
(y,t_{1})
either to
(a,x_{1})
or to
(b,x_{2})
, and similarly for
(z,t_{2})
.
We thus get

\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket})=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}
while
\mathcal{P}_{DB}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket})=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+\mathbf{2}x_{1}x_{2}
. Our notion of provenance does not distinguish between the match that maps
y
to
a
and
z
to
b
and the one that maps
y
to
b
and
z
to
a
.

This is actually not surprising since our provenance is defined by summing over a set of semiring elements (cf. Section
3.1.3
for the discussion of this design choice).
∎

The following theorem extends the comparison between our notion of provenance and provenance for relational databases
to the case of concept assertion queries where the ontology is allowed to have a restricted form of GCIs. The intuition behind the theorem is that one can
rewrite atomic concept queries into UCQs by unfolding the wanted concept name into the prerequisites for deriving it.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative
\oplus
-
idempotent semiring, and
A
a fixed (but arbitrary)
concept name. For every

\mathbb{K}
-annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
that contains only (i) assertions and
(ii) GCIs of the form
C\sqsubseteq A
, labelled with
\mathbb{1}
, where

C
is an
{\cal E\!LHI}
concept not containing
A
, for every

a\in{\sf N_{I}}
,
\mathcal{P}(A(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{P}_{DB}(q,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}})
where
\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}}
is the set of the annotated assertions of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and

q:=A(a)\vee\bigvee_{C\sqsubseteq A\in\mathcal{O}}q_{C}(a),

with
q_{C}(x)
the rooted tree-shaped query that retrieves all instances of
C
(cf. Section
2.1
).

Remark 5.7

(On the possibility of extending Theorem
5.6
to UCQ-rewritable queries)
.

A query
q
is
UCQ-rewritable
w.r.t. a set of GCIs and RIs (TBox)
\mathcal{T}
if there exists a UCQ
q^{\prime}
such that for every ontology
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{A}
with
\mathcal{A}
a set of assertions (ABox), it holds that
\mathcal{O}\models q
iff
q^{\prime}
is satisfied by the interpretation
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
with domain
{\sf N_{I}}
that satisfies exactly the assertions in
\mathcal{A}
. For example, it is well-known that every BCQ is UCQ-rewritable w.r.t. a
DL-Lite
TBox (?).
One could try to extend Theorem
5.6
to
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontologies whose GCIs and RIs are annotated by
\mathbb{1}
and BCQs that are UCQ-rewritable w.r.t. the set of GCIs and RIs in
\mathcal{O}
, in order to obtain
\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{P}_{DB}(q^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}})
with
q^{\prime}
a UCQ rewriting of
q
.
However, Theorem
5.6
cannot be extended in this way without further restrictions. Consider
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{B}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket}=\{(A(a),x_{1}),(B(a),x_{2}),(A\sqsubseteq C,1),(B\sqcap C\sqsubseteq A,1)\}
and
q=A(a)
. Clearly,
q
is UCQ-rewritable w.r.t. the GCIs in
\mathcal{O}
(a UCQ-rewriting is
A(a)\vee(B(a)\wedge C(a))
) but
\mathcal{P}(A(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{B}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket})=\Sigma_{i\geq 0}x_{1}x_{2}^{i}
while the provenance of a UCQ over a finite set of annotated facts in
\mathbb{B}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
is always a
finite
sum of monomials.

Even if we add some kind of non-recursivity condition on
\mathcal{O}
, extending Theorem
5.6
would not be straightforward. Indeed, existing rewriting algorithms crucially rely on unification and minimization to allow, e.g. to rewrite
\exists yzR(x,y)\wedge R(z,y)
into
A(x)
w.r.t.
A\sqsubseteq\exists R
.
Hence, if
q^{\prime}
is obtained with such an algorithm, the result would not hold.
Consider the following case:
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(A(a),\kappa),(A\sqsubseteq\exists R,\mathbb{1}),(\exists R^{-}\sqsubseteq B,\mathbb{1}),(\exists R.B\sqsubseteq C,\mathbb{1})\}
.
A UCQ rewriting of
C(a)
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
is a disjunction of the following CQs (or of a subset of irredundant queries):
C(a)
,
\exists yR(a,y)\wedge B(y)
,
\exists yzR(a,y)\wedge R(z,y)
,
\exists yR(a,y)\wedge R(a,y)
,
\exists yR(a,y)
, and
A(a)
. Hence its relational provenance over
\{(A(a),\kappa)\}
is
\kappa
.
However,
\mathcal{P}(A(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\kappa\otimes\kappa
. Extending Theorem
5.6
to UCQ-rewritable queries would thus require to design rewriting algorithms that rewrite, e.g.
\exists yzR(x,y)\wedge R(z,y)
into
A(x)\wedge A(x)
in this example.

5.3
Consistency with Semiring Provenance for Datalog Queries

Theorem
5.8
ensures that our notion of provenance is also
consistent with the one defined for Datalog queries over annotated databases (?).
Following the notation of ? (?), given an annotated database
\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{D},\lambda\rangle
and Datalog progam

\Sigma
, we denote the Datalog provenance of a fact
\alpha
w.r.t.
\Sigma
and

\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}}
by

\mathcal{P}_{Dat}(\Sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}},\alpha)=\bigoplus_{t\in T^{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{D}}(\alpha)}\ \bigotimes_{v\text{ is a leaf of $t$}}\lambda(v)

where
T^{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{D}}(\alpha)
is the set of all derivation trees for
\alpha
w.r.t.
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{D}
.
In the Datalog provenance literature, semirings are usually assumed to be
\omega
-continuous, but

\omega
-completeness is sufficient for
\mathcal{P}_{Dat}(\Sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}},\alpha)
to be well-defined. Hence, in this section, we consider
\omega
-complete semirings. We do not need to require that the semirings are complete because the ontologies we consider (corresponding to Datalog programs) are trivially satisfiable,
which means that the set
\{\kappa\mid\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)\}
is guaranteed to be countable even in the case where the semiring domain is not.

Theorem 5.8
.

For each
{\cal E\!LHI}
concept
C
, let
q_{C}(x)
be the rooted tree-shaped CQ that corresponds to
C
, and for every GCI or RI
\alpha
, let
F_{\alpha}
be a fresh nullary predicate.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative
\oplus
-
idempotent
\omega
-complete semiring. For every
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
whose GCIs have only concept names as right-hand sides and RIs are positive, for every
BCQ
q
,
\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{P}_{Dat}(\Sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}},\mathsf{goal})
with

\displaystyle\Sigma=\{q_{C}(x)\wedge F_{C\sqsubseteq A}\rightarrow A(x)\mid C\sqsubseteq A\in\mathcal{O}\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cup\{P(x,y)\wedge F_{P\sqsubseteq Q}\rightarrow Q(x,y)\mid P\sqsubseteq Q\in\mathcal{O}\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cup\{q\rightarrow\mathsf{goal}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{D},\lambda^{\prime}\rangle

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=\{A(a)\mid A(a)\in\mathcal{O}\}\cup\{R(a,b)\mid R(a,b)\in\mathcal{O}\}\cup\{F_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\text{ is a GCI or RI of }\mathcal{O}\}

and

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}(\alpha)=\lambda(\alpha)\text{ if }\alpha\text{ is an assertion of }\mathcal{O}

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}(F_{\alpha})=\lambda(\alpha)\text{ if }\alpha\text{ is a GCI or RI of }\mathcal{O}.

Since the Datalog provenance of a CQ seen as a Datalog query coincides with its relational provenance (formally, for every BCQ
q=\exists\vec{y}\phi(\vec{y})
, and annotated set of facts
\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{DB}(q,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{P}_{Dat}(\{\exists\vec{y}\phi(\vec{y})\rightarrow\mathsf{goal}\},\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}},\mathsf{goal})
) (?, ?), Theorem
5.8
does not hold when the addition is not idempotent.

Example 5.9
.

Consider
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket}=\{(A(a),x_{1}),(A(b),x_{2})\}
defined in Example
5.5
and
\Sigma=\{\exists yz\,A(y)\land A(z)\rightarrow\mathsf{goal}\}
.
\mathcal{P}_{Dat}(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket},\mathsf{goal})=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+2x_{1}x_{2}
because
\mathsf{goal}
has the following derivation trees (?, Definition 1).

\mathsf{goal}
A(a)
A(a)

\mathsf{goal}
A(b)
A(b)

\mathsf{goal}
A(a)
A(b)

\mathsf{goal}
A(b)
A(a)

∎

We prove Theorem
5.8
with the help of Proposition 3 of ? (?) which states that when
\mathbb{K}
is
\oplus
-
idempotent, then

\mathcal{P}_{Dat}(\Sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}},\mathsf{goal})=\mathcal{P}_{Dat}^{\texttt{SAM}}(\Sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}},\mathsf{goal})
where
\mathcal{P}_{Dat}^{\texttt{SAM}}
is an alternative notion of provenance semantics for Datalog (set-annotated model-based provenance semantics) defined as follows:

\mathcal{P}_{Dat}^{\texttt{SAM}}(\Sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}},\mathsf{goal})=\bigoplus_{\kappa\in\bigcap_{(I,\mu^{I})\models(\Sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}})}\mu^{I}(\mathsf{goal})}\kappa,

where
(I,\mu^{I})
–with
I
a set of facts and
\mu^{I}
a function from
I

to the power-set of
K
–is such that
(I,\mu^{I})\models(\Sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}})
if

1.

\mathcal{D}\subseteq I
, and for every
\alpha\in\mathcal{D}
,
\lambda^{\prime}(\alpha)\in\mu^{I}(\alpha)
;

2.

for every
\phi(\vec{x},\vec{y})\rightarrow H(\vec{x})
in
\Sigma
with
\phi(\vec{x},\vec{y})=\alpha_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\alpha_{n}
, if there is a homomorphism
h:\{\alpha_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\mapsto I
,
then
h(H(\vec{x}))\in I
and if

h(\alpha_{1})\wedge\dots\wedge h(\alpha_{n})=\beta_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\beta_{n}
, then
\{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}\mid(\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{n})\in\mu^{I}(\beta_{1})\times\dots\times\mu^{I}(\beta_{n})\}\subseteq\mu^{I}(h(H(\vec{x})))
.

Example 5.10
.

Consider the same
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket}
as in Example
5.9
. It then holds
that
\mathcal{P}_{Dat}^{\texttt{SAM}}(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket},\mathsf{goal})=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}
. Indeed,

(I,\mu^{I})\models(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket})
means that:

1.

\{A(a),A(b)\}\subseteq I
,
x_{1}\in\mu^{I}(A(a))
and
x_{2}\in\mu^{I}(A(a))
;

2.

whenever there is a homomorphism
h:\{A(y),A(z)\}\mapsto I
, then
\mathsf{goal}\in I
and if
h(A(y))\wedge h(A(z))=\beta_{1}\wedge\beta_{2}
, then
\{p_{1}\times p_{2}\mid(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mu^{I}(\beta_{1})\times\mu^{I}(\beta_{2})\}\subseteq\mu^{I}(\mathsf{goal})
.

It follows that for every
(I,\mu^{I})\models(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket})
,
\mathsf{goal}\in I
and
\{x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{1}x_{2}\}\subseteq\mu^{I}(\mathsf{goal})
. Moreover, one can check that
(I,\mu^{I})
with
I=\{A(a),A(b),\mathsf{goal}\}
and
\mu^{I}(A(a))=\{x_{1}\}
,
\mu^{I}(A(b))=\{x_{2}\}
,
\mu^{I}(\mathsf{goal})=\{x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{1}x_{2}\}
is such that
(I,\mu^{I})\models(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket})
. Hence,
\bigcap_{(I,\mu^{I})\models(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket})}\mu^{I}(\mathsf{goal})=\{x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{1}x_{2}\}
.

Notice that in this example we use a semiring which is
not

\oplus
-
idempotent and that
\mathcal{P}_{Dat}^{\texttt{SAM}}(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket},\mathsf{goal})\neq\mathcal{P}_{Dat}(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket},\mathsf{goal})
.

∎

Theorem
5.8
is thus a direct consequence of the following lemma, which shows the correspondance between our provenance semantics and the set-annotated model-based Datalog provenance semantics (note that the semiring is not required to be
\oplus
-
idempotent here).

Lemma 5.11
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a commutative
\omega
-complete semiring, then for every
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
whose GCIs have only concept names as right-hand sides and RIs are positive, for every
BCQ
q
,
\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{P}_{Dat}^{\texttt{SAM}}(\Sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}},\mathsf{goal})
where
\Sigma
and

\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{K}}
are defined as in Theorem
5.8
.

5.4
Commutation with Homomorphisms

Theorem
5.12
states the relationship between the provenance values computed in different semirings: under some conditions, provenance computation “commutes” with homomorphisms in the sense that if a
\mathbb{K}_{2}
-annotated ontology is obtained from a
\mathbb{K}_{1}
-annotated ontology via a semiring homomorphism, then the consequence provenance in
\mathbb{K}_{2}
can be obtained by applying this homomorphism to the consequence provenance in
\mathbb{K}_{1}
. This is particularly useful to ensure that the provenance expression computed in a provenance semiring (such as
\mathbb{B}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket
or
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
) can be used to compute the provenance value in any semiring to which it specializes correctly. Since the theorem applies to satisfiable ontologies, we only need to require the semiring to be
\omega
-complete since
\{\kappa\mid\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)\}
is guaranteed to be countable.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
\mathbb{K}_{1}
and
\mathbb{K}_{2}
be commutative
\omega
-complete semirings such that there is a
\omega
-complete semiring homomorphism
h
from
\mathbb{K}_{1}
to
\mathbb{K}_{2}
. For every satisfiable
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}
and annotated versions
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}_{1}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}_{2}}=\langle\mathcal{O},h\circ\lambda\rangle
,

h(\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}_{1}}))=\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}_{2}})\quad\text{holds if:}

1.

\mathbb{K}_{1}
and
\mathbb{K}_{2}
are
\oplus
-
idempotent and
\alpha
is a BCQ, an assertion, or an RI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
; or

2.

\mathbb{K}_{1}
and
\mathbb{K}_{2}
are
\oplus
-
idempotent and
\otimes
-idempotent,
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, and
\alpha
is a GCI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
.

The proof of Theorem
5.12
relies on the canonical models of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}_{2}}
and Theorems
4.5
,
4.7
and
4.9
. The following example illustrates how this theorem can be used and shows why the assumption that
\mathbb{K}_{1}
and
\mathbb{K}_{2}
are
\otimes
-idempotent is necessary for the GCI case.

Example 5.13
.

Consider
\mathcal{O}=\{A(a),\ A\sqsubseteq B_{1},\ A\sqsubseteq B_{2},\ B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C\}.

•

It is well-known that there exists a semiring homomorphism from the tropical semiring

\mathbb{T}=(\mathbb{R}^{\infty}_{+},\min,+,\infty,0)
to the Viterbi semiring
\mathbb{V}=([0,1],\max,\times,0,1)

defined by
h(x)=e^{-x}
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
h(\infty)=0
. Let

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{T}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\mathbb{T}}\rangle
with
\lambda_{\mathbb{T}}(A(a))=0
,

\lambda_{\mathbb{T}}(A\sqsubseteq B_{1})=2
,
\lambda_{\mathbb{T}}(A\sqsubseteq B_{2})=3
, and

\lambda_{\mathbb{T}}(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C)=0
, and consider the
\mathbb{V}
-annotated ontology

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{V}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\mathbb{V}}\rangle
with
\lambda_{\mathbb{V}}=h\circ\lambda_{\mathbb{T}}
. Since
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{V}
are additively idempotent,
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{V}})=h(\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{T}}))=h(5)=e^{-5}
by point 1 of Theorem
5.12
.

•

Consider now fully idempotent semirings
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\mathbb{F}=([0,1],\max,\min,0,1)
, and let
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
with
\lambda_{\sf X}(A(a))=y
,
\lambda_{\sf X}(A\sqsubseteq B_{1})=x_{1}
,
\lambda_{\sf X}(A\sqsubseteq B_{2})=x_{2}
, and
\lambda_{\sf X}(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C)=x_{3}
, and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\mathbb{F}}\rangle
with
\lambda_{\mathbb{F}}=h\circ\lambda_{\sf X}
for the unique semiring homomorphism
h:\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]\rightarrow\mathbb{F}
such that
h(y)=0.2
,
h(x_{1})=0.5
,
h(x_{2})=0.6
,
h(x_{3})=1
, and
h(z)=1
for every other
z\in{\sf X}
. In this case we obtain
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})=h(\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}}))=h(yx_{1}x_{2}x_{3})=0.2
by point 1 and
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})=h(\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}}))=h(x_{1}x_{2}x_{3})=0.5
by point 2 of Theorem
5.12
.

•

To see why
\mathbb{K}_{1}
has to be
\otimes
-idempotent when
\alpha
is a GCI, take

\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
and
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
with

\lambda_{\sf X}
as in the previous point. There is a unique semiring homomorphism
h:\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]\rightarrow\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
such that
h
is the identity over
{\sf X}
. Since the semirings are
\oplus
-
idempotent, it holds that
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]})=h(\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}))=h(yx_{1}x_{2}x_{3})=yx_{1}x_{2}x_{3}
. However, since
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
is not
\otimes
-idempotent, one can show (in the same way as in Example
4.8
) that
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]})=0
. Hence
h(\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}))=h(0)=0
is different from
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]})=x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}
.

•

Finally, to see why
\mathbb{K}_{2}
has to be
\otimes
-idempotent for the GCI case, we define the

\oplus
-
idempotent commutative semiring
\mathbb{K}=(\{0,1,c\},\oplus,\otimes,0,1)
where
1\oplus c=1
and

c\otimes c=0
(all other values of sums or products are implied by the properties of a
\oplus
-
idempotent commutative
semiring). One can check that this indeed defines a semiring which is not
\otimes
-idempotent since
c\otimes c\not=c
.
We then consider the ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
with
\lambda_{\sf X}

as before and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\mathbb{K}}\rangle
with

\lambda_{\mathbb{K}}=h\circ\lambda_{\sf X}
for the unique semiring homomorphism

h:\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]\rightarrow\mathbb{K}
such that
h(z)=1
for every
z\in{\sf X}
(i.e.
h(p)=1
for every

p\in\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]\setminus\{0\}
).
As we have seen already,
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]})=x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}
so
h(\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}))=1
. However, one can show that
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=0
by considering the following
\mathbb{K}
-annotated model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}}=

\displaystyle\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},1),(e,c)\}\ B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},1),(e,c)\}\ B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},1),(e,c)\}\ C^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},1),(e,0)\}

Indeed, since there is no
\kappa\in\{0,1,c\}
such that
1\otimes\kappa=1
and
c\otimes\kappa=0
, there is no
\kappa
such that
\mathcal{I}\models(A\sqsubseteq C,\kappa)
.∎

Example
5.14
shows that Theorem

5.12
does not hold without the assumption that the addition is idempotent, even for simple ontologies with only concept name inclusions.

Example 5.14
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative
\omega
-complete semiring which is not
\oplus
-
idempotent and
\kappa\in K
such that
\kappa\oplus\kappa\neq\kappa
.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=

\displaystyle\{A(a),B(a),A\sqsubseteq C,B\sqsubseteq C\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket}=

\displaystyle\{(A(a),x),(B(a),y),(A\sqsubseteq C,u),(B\sqsubseteq C,v)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=

\displaystyle\{(A(a),\kappa),(B(a),\kappa),(A\sqsubseteq C,\mathbb{1}),(B\sqsubseteq C,\mathbb{1})\}.

The provenance of
C(a)
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket}
is
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket})=xu+yv
, hence, if we consider the semiring homomorphism
h
such that
h(x)=h(y)=\kappa
and
h(u)=h(v)=\mathbb{1}
,
h(\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket}))=\kappa\oplus\kappa
.
However,
\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\kappa
since there exists a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that
C^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\}
.
Hence
