h(\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\llbracket{\sf X}\rrbracket}))\neq\mathcal{P}(C(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
.
∎

Theorem
5.12
does not hold if the ontology
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable or if
\alpha
is a GCI or an RI whose left-hand side is unsatisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
since in these cases the provenance of
\alpha
is the sum of all the elements of the semiring (see Remarks
3.1
and
3.2
). Indeed, if
\mathbb{K}_{1}=\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
,
\mathbb{K}_{2}=(\{0,1,a\},\oplus,\otimes,0,1)
is the commutative semiring such that both operations are idempotent and
1\oplus a=a
(all other values of sums or products are implied by the properties of a fully idempotent commutative semiring) and

h
is the semiring homomorphism defined by
h(0)=0
,
h(1)=1
and
h(x)=1
for every
x\in{\sf X}
, then
we have that

h(\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}_{1}}))=h(0\vee 1\vee\bigvee_{S\subseteq{\sf X}}\bigwedge_{x\in S}x)=1
while
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}_{2}})=0\oplus 1\oplus a=a
. However, if we require
\mathbb{K}_{1}
and
\mathbb{K}_{2}
to be absorptive, the theorem still holds for unsatisfiable ontologies: if
\mathbb{K}_{1}=(K_{1},\oplus,\otimes,\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1})
and
\mathbb{K}_{2}=(K_{2},+,\cdot,0,1)
are commutative complete (or
\omega
-complete and
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
are countable) and absorptive semirings,
h
is a (
\omega
-)complete homomorphism from
\mathbb{K}_{1}
to
\mathbb{K}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable, then

h(\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}_{1}}))=h(\bigoplus_{\kappa\in K_{1}}\kappa)=h(\mathbb{1})=1=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{\kappa^{\prime}\in K_{2}}\kappa^{\prime}.

Indeed, if
\mathbb{K}
is absorptive,
\mathbb{1}\oplus\kappa=\mathbb{1}\oplus(\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa)=\mathbb{1}
for every
\kappa\in K
, which means that

\bigoplus_{\kappa\in K}\kappa=\mathbb{1}\oplus\bigoplus_{\kappa\in K\setminus\{\mathbb{1}\}}\kappa=\mathbb{1}
.

6
Computing Query Provenance in the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
Semiring

We now focus on computing the provenance of assertions and BCQs in
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-
annotated ontologies.
Recall from the previous section that since
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
is
\oplus
-
idempotent, all desirable properties we considered hold for the provenance of assertions and BCQs.
While the annotations of such an ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
can normally take any values from
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]\setminus\{0\}
,
we focus here on the special case where
\mathcal{O}
is annotated
by a function
\lambda_{\sf X}:\mathcal{O}\mapsto{\sf X}\cup\{1\}
. Indeed, we are interested in capturing the well-known
why-provenance
defined in the database context, which has recently received interest in the context of Datalog (?, ?, ?, ?)
and associates to a query result the sets of database tuples from which the query can be derived, representing tuples with identifiers. This can be done by annotating each axiom of
\mathcal{O}
by a distinct variable from
{\sf X}
(assuming that
{\sf X}
has a greater cardinality than
\mathcal{O}
). However, since we will require the ontologies to be in
normal form
, and the normalization process described in Section
4.1
introduces axioms annotated with
1
, we allow
\lambda_{\sf X}
to also take this value.
Recall that
by Theorem
4.1
and Corollary
4.2
, one can normalize an annotated ontology in polynomial time while preserving annotated entailments and provenance of axioms and queries.

Thus, for the rest of this section, a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology is a pair
\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
where

\lambda_{\sf X}:\mathcal{O}\mapsto{\sf X}\cup\{1\}
. For brevity, we omit the superscript and identify
\mathcal{O}
and

\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
. We denote by
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
the cardinality of
\mathcal{O}
(that is, the number of axioms in
\mathcal{O}
), and given
p\in\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
, we denote by
|p|
the length of the string that represents it as a sum of monomials, where variables from
{\sf X}
are considered of length one.

We start with a general proposition that shows that we can focus on
monomials
. Recall that we denote by
\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
the set of all monomials over
{\sf X}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a satisfiable
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology and
m
be an element of
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
.
If
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
where
\alpha
is an assertion or a BCQ,
then
m\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
.

We can thus compute
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O})
by finding all monomials
m
over
{\sf X}
such that
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
.

6.1
Annotated Assertion Entailment from
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
Ontologies

In this section, we present a completion algorithm for deriving annotated assertions entailed from a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology. For DLs of the
{\cal E\!L}
family, completion algorithms are a classical way to derive axioms by saturating an ontology in normal form by applying so-called
completion, or saturation, rules
(?, ?).

6.1.1
Completion Algorithm for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}

if

then
\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}\cup\{\Phi\}

\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{0}

(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,m_{0}),(P\sqsubseteq P_{1},m_{1}),(P\sqsubseteq P_{2},m_{2}),(P_{1}\sqcap P_{2}\sqsubseteq\bot,m_{3})\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(A\sqsubseteq\bot,{m_{0}\times m_{1}\times m_{2}\times m_{3}})

\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{1}

(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},m_{1}),(P_{2}\sqsubseteq P_{3},m_{2})\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{3},{m_{1}\times m_{2}})

\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}

(M\sqsubseteq A,m_{1}),(A\sqcap N\sqsubseteq C,m_{2})\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(M\sqcap N\sqsubseteq C,{m_{1}\times m_{2}})

\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}

(A\sqsubseteq\exists Q,m_{0})
,
(Q\sqsubseteq P,m)
,

(Q\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{i}),(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i},n_{i})
,
1\leq i\leq k
,
k\geq 0
,

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},o_{i})
,
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
,
k^{\prime}\geq 0
,

(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq C,n),

\Phi=(A\sqcap A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}\sqsubseteq D,

(\exists P.C\sqsubseteq D,o)\in\mathcal{S}

m\times n\times o\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{i}\times n_{i})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}o_{i})

\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}

(A_{i}(a),m_{i}),1\leq i\leq k,(A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}\sqsubseteq B,m)\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(B(a),{m_{1}\times\dots\times m_{k}\times m})

\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{2}

(R(a,b),m_{1}),(A(b),m_{2}),(\exists R.A\sqsubseteq B,m_{3})\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(B(a),{m_{1}\times m_{2}\times m_{3}})

\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{3}

(R(b,a),m_{1}),(A(b),m_{2}),(\exists R^{-}.A\sqsubseteq B,m_{3})\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(B(a),{m_{1}\times m_{2}\times m_{3}})

\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{4}

(R(a,b),m_{1}),(R\sqsubseteq S,m_{2})\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(S(a,b),{m_{1}\times m_{2}})

\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{5}

(R(a,b),m_{1}),(R\sqsubseteq S^{-},m_{2})\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(S(b,a),{m_{1}\times m_{2}})

\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{6}

(R(a,b),m_{1}),(S(a,b),m_{2}),(R\sqcap S\sqsubseteq\bot,m_{3})\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(\bot(a),{m_{1}\times m_{2}\times m_{3}})

\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{7}

(R(a,b),m_{1}),(S(b,a),m_{2}),(R\sqcap S^{-}\sqsubseteq\bot,m_{3})\in\mathcal{S}

\Phi=(\bot(a),{m_{1}\times m_{2}\times m_{3}})

Table 3:
Completion rules.
A_{(i)},B_{(i)},C,D\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top,\bot\}
,
R,S\in{\sf N_{R}}
,
P_{(i)},Q:=R\mid R^{-}
,
M,N
are (possibly empty) conjunctions over
{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
, and
m_{(i)},n_{(i)},o_{(i)}\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
(recall that by definition of
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
,
monomials do not contain repeated variables).
Empty conjunctions are written
\top
and non-empty conjunctions are written without
\top
(in particular in
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
, if some but not all
A_{i}
are equal to
\top
, they do not explicitly occur in the conjunction
A\sqcap A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}
).
Conjunctions are treated as multisets with maximal multiplicity
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
: the order does not matter but there may contain repetitions such as
A\sqcap A
and each concept name occurs at most
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
times (i.e.
\underbrace{A\sqcap\dots\sqcap A}_{\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})+k\text{ times}}
is written as

\underbrace{A\sqcap\dots\sqcap A}_{\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})\text{ times}}
).

We design completion rules for
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontologies, inspired by the completion rules given by ? (?, Section 4.3) for
{\cal E\!LHI}_{\bot}
. The algorithm receives as input an annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}
in normal form and exhaustively applies completion rules from Table
3
after an initialization step that adds, e.g., some tautologies annotated with
1
. The resulting set
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
of annotated axioms, called the
saturation
of
\mathcal{O}
, is such that if
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable,
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
contains all entailed annotated assertions over the signature of
\mathcal{O}
and only those (see Theorem
6.4
for the complete statement regarding the completion algorithm).
We cannot directly use the rules presented by ? (?)
because they use qualified role restrictions on the right side of GCIs that may introduce wrong provenance information, as illustrated in the next example.

Example 6.2
.

Consider the following annotated ontology:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\{(\top\sqsubseteq A,x),\ (B\sqsubseteq\exists R.\top,y),\ (\exists R.\top\sqsubseteq C,z),\ (R\sqsubseteq R,1),\ (B(a),v)\}.

If we apply the completion rules T6, T7 and A1 below, which were originally given by ? (?),
while propagating the provenance annotations as expected, we obtain (among others) the following axioms:

(B\sqsubseteq\exists R.A,xy)
(by rule T6),
(B\sqsubseteq C,xyz)
(by rule T7) and
(C(a),vxyz)
(by rule A1).

\text{T6}\ \frac{M\sqsubseteq\exists R.(N\sqcap N^{\prime})\quad N\sqsubseteq A}{M\sqsubseteq\exists R.(N\sqcap N^{\prime}\sqcap A)}\quad\quad\text{T7}\ \frac{M\sqsubseteq\exists R.(N\sqcap A)\quad\exists S.A\sqsubseteq B\quad R\sqsubseteq S}{M\sqsubseteq B}

\text{A1}\ \frac{A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{n}\sqsubseteq B\quad A_{i}(a)\ (1\leq i\leq n)}{B(a)}\quad\text{ with }M,N^{(^{\prime})}\text{ conjunctions over }{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top,\bot\}

However,
\mathcal{O}\not\models(C(a),vxyz)
because the following model of
\mathcal{O}
does not satisfy
(C(a),vxyz)
:
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}={\sf N_{I}}\cup\{\star\}
,
b^{\mathcal{I}}=b
for all
b\in{\sf N_{I}}
, and

A^{\mathcal{I}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{x\},\quad B^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a,v)\},\quad C^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a,vyz)\},\quad R^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a,\star,vy)\}.

Intuitively,
(\top\sqsubseteq A,x)
is not needed when considering the
provenance of elements of
C
,
but T7 can be applied on
B\sqsubseteq\exists R.A
(which uses
\top\sqsubseteq A
) and
\exists R.\top\sqsubseteq C
to get
B\sqsubseteq C
.

In contrast, our completion algorithm cannot produce a conjunction or qualified role restriction on the right-hand side. When applied to
\mathcal{O}
, it works as follows:

•

\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
(with
k=0
and
k^{\prime}=1
) applied to
(B\sqsubseteq\exists R,y)
,
(R\sqsubseteq R,1)
,
(\top\sqsubseteq\top,1)
and
(\exists R.\top\sqsubseteq C,z)
produces
(B\sqsubseteq C,yz)
(note that basic tautologies are added before applying the completion rules, see definition of
\mathcal{S}
below);

•

\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
(with
k=1
) applied to
(B\sqsubseteq C,yz)
and
(B(a),v)
produces
(C(a),yzv)
;

•

\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
(with
k=1
) applied to
(\top\sqsubseteq A,x)
and
(\top(a),1)
produces
(A(a),x)
, and similarly
(A(a_{\top}),x)
is produced from
(\top(a_{\top}),1)
(see again definition of
\mathcal{S}
below for
(\top(a),1)
and
(\top(a_{\top}),1)
);

•

no other rule is applicable. ∎

Given a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology in normal form
\mathcal{O}
, the completion algorithm starts with
\mathcal{O}
completed with some trivially entailed axioms expressed in an extension of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
(featuring assertions of the form
\top(a)
and GCIs of the form
\exists P.\bot\sqsubseteq\bot
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{O}

\displaystyle\cup\{(\top(a),1)\mid a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})\cup\{a_{\top}\}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(A\sqsubseteq A,1)\mid A\in({\sf N_{C}}\cap\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O}))\cup\{\top,\bot\}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(R\sqsubseteq R,1),(R^{-}\sqsubseteq R^{-},1),(\exists R.\bot\sqsubseteq\bot,1),(\exists R^{-}.\bot\sqsubseteq\bot,1)\mid R\in{\sf N_{R}}\cap\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(\mathsf{inv}(P_{1})\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P_{2}),v)\mid(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},v)\in\mathcal{O}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(\mathsf{inv}(P_{1})\sqcap\mathsf{inv}(P_{2})\sqsubseteq\bot,v)\mid(P_{1}\sqcap P_{2}\sqsubseteq\bot,v)\in\mathcal{O}\},

where
a_{\top}
is a fresh individual name from
{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
. Contrary to the saturation procedure described by ?, we do not add the axiom
A\sqsubseteq\top
: since
\top^{\mathcal{I}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{1\}
,
(A\sqsubseteq\top,1)
does not hold in every interpretation.

After this initialization step, the completion algorithm extends
\mathcal{S}
with axioms through an iterative application of the rules from Table
3
until
\mathcal{S}
becomes
saturated
, i.e., no more rules are applicable. The resulting set is
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. To ensure termination, a rule is applicable only if its conclusion is not in
\mathcal{S}
.

The algorithm adds to
\mathcal{S}
axioms annotated with
monomials
and the definition of
\times
in
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
ensures that all monomials have at most
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
variables (since they represent sets of variables that annotate
\mathcal{O}
).
It may add annotated axioms such that the axiom is already present with some other annotation, so
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
is not an annotated ontology per say, but rather a
set of annotated axioms
.

The axioms added by the completion rules respect the syntactic restriction of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
(since they are RIs or GCIs with a concept name or
\bot
in the right-hand side) but may not be in normal form. In particular, conjunctions of arbitrary size (bounded by
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})*|\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})|
) can be introduced in the left-hand side of GCIs by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
.
The following example shows why it is important to allow for repetitions in the left-hand side of the GCIs introduced by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
.

Example 6.3
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\{

\displaystyle(A_{1}(a),x_{1}),(A_{2}(a),x_{2}),(A_{1}\sqsubseteq A,y_{1}),(A_{2}\sqsubseteq A,y_{2}),(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,u),

\displaystyle(\exists P^{-}.A\sqsubseteq B_{1},v_{1}),(\exists P^{-}.A\sqsubseteq B_{2},v_{2}),(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C,w),(\exists P.C\sqsubseteq D,z)\}.

One can verify by considering an arbitrary model of
\mathcal{O}
that
\mathcal{O}\models(D(a),x_{1}y_{1}uv_{1}v_{2}wz)
,
\mathcal{O}\models(D(a),x_{2}y_{2}uv_{1}v_{2}wz)
and
\mathcal{O}\models(D(a),x_{1}x_{2}y_{1}y_{2}uv_{1}v_{2}wz)
.
Our algorithm derives these annotated assertions as follows:

•

by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
, we obtain
(A\sqcap A\sqcap A\sqsubseteq D,uv_{1}v_{2}wz)
(by instantiating
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
with
k=2
,
k^{\prime}=0
, using the ontology GCIs and the required tautologies from
\mathcal{S}
);

•

for
i\in\{1,2\}
, by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
applied to
(A_{i}(a),x_{i})
and
(A_{i}\sqsubseteq A,y_{i})
, we obtain
(A(a),x_{i}y_{i})
;

•

for
i\in\{1,2\}
, by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
applied to
(A(a),x_{i}y_{i})
and
(A\sqcap A\sqcap A\sqsubseteq D,uv_{1}v_{2}wz)
, we obtain
(D(a),x_{i}y_{i}uv_{1}v_{2}wz)
;

•

by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
applied to
(A(a),x_{1}y_{1})
,
(A(a),x_{2}y_{2})
, and
(A\sqcap A\sqcap A\sqsubseteq D,uv_{1}v_{2}wz)
, we obtain
(D(a),x_{1}x_{2}y_{1}y_{2}uv_{1}v_{2}wz)
.

We would not obtain the last annotated assertion (whose annotation witnesses the fact that we can use the two ontology assertions
together
to obtain
D(a)
) if we wrote
A\sqsubseteq D
instead of
A\sqcap A\sqcap A\sqsubseteq D
. Intuitively, the repetition of
A
means that it plays three roles (requiring a
P
-successor, enforcing that this successor belongs to
B_{1}
, and enforcing that this successor belongs to
B_{2}
), and each of these roles can be fulfilled by a different “cause” for
A
.
∎

Observe that the
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
bound on the number of occurrences of the same concept name in a conjunction on the left-hand side of a GCI does not prevent completeness of the algorithm because monomials have at most
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
variables, so that the provenance information brought by additional repetitions will be redundant.

We now show that the completion algorithm is sound and complete for deciding assertion entailment and runs in exponential time.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology in normal form,
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
the result of saturating
\mathcal{O}
,
m\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
, and
\alpha
an
assertion
.

1.

If
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
then
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
. Moreover, if
\alpha=A(a_{\top})
, then for every
c\in{\sf N_{I}}
,
\mathcal{O}\models(A(c),m)
.

2.

If
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable and
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
,

(a)

if
\alpha
is of the form
A(a)
or
R(a,b)
with
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, then
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
;

(b)

if
\alpha
is of the form
A(c)
with
c\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, then
(A(a_{\top}),m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

3.

If
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable, then
(\bot(a),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
for some
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})\cup\{a_{\top}\}
and
n\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
.

4.

\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
is computable in exponential time w.r.t. the size of
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof Sketch.

For (1), we show that for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
,
for every
(\alpha,{m})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
: (i) if
\alpha
is an assertion or an RI (positive or negative), then
\mathcal{I}\models(\alpha,{m})
, and
(ii) if
\alpha
is a GCI of the form
C\sqsubseteq D
, then for every
monomial

n
and domain element
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(e,n)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
implies
(e,n\times m)\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
.
The proof is by induction on the number of completion rule applications before
(\alpha,m)
is added to
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

We show (2.a) by contrapositive:
assuming that
(\alpha,{m})\notin\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, we show that the canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\mathcal{I}_{i}
of
\mathcal{O}
is such that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\not\models(\alpha,m)
. We show by induction on
i
that for every assertion
\beta
on the vocabulary of
\mathcal{O}
and every monomial
n
,
(\beta,{n})\notin\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
implies that
\mathcal{I}_{i}\not\models(\beta,n)
. In a nutshell, the induction step shows that if
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}\models(\beta,n)
, then
(\beta,{n})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
via a case analysis on the chase rule applied to construct
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
from
\mathcal{I}_{i}
. The most technical part is to handle the case where this chase rule uses an axiom of the form
(\exists P.A\sqsubseteq B,o)
and an anonymous domain element
x\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\setminus{\sf N_{I}}
. For (2.b), we remark that if
\mathcal{O}\models(A(c),m)
for some
c\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, then
\mathcal{O}\models(A(d),m)
for every
d\in{\sf N_{I}}
, and, in particular,
\mathcal{O}\models(A(a_{\top}),m)
. We then consider an ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
that extends
\mathcal{O}
with an assertion
(\mathit{Top}(a_{\top}),1)
for some fresh concept
\mathit{Top}
and conclude using (2.a).

For (3), we consider the set of annotated axioms
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
obtained from
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
by replacing
\bot
by a fresh concept name
\mathit{Bot}
in assertions and GCIs and removing negative RIs and assertions on
a_{\top}
. We show that if
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable, there exists
(\mathit{Bot}(a),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
for some
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})\cup\{a_{\top}\}
, which implies that
(\bot(a),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. Otherwise, we could get a model of
\mathcal{O}
via the canonical model construction with the annotated axioms in
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

Finally, for (4), we observe that the completion rules add at most an exponential number of axioms (
O(|\mathcal{O}|^{2|\mathcal{O}|+1})
) annotated with an exponential number of monomials (
O(2^{|\mathcal{O}|})
), hence in total an exponential number of annotated axioms. Each rule application adds one annotated axiom and can be performed in polynomial time w.r.t. the number of annotated axioms already present, hence in exponential time. It follows that the algorithm runs in exponential time.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

The completion algorithm is sound for
annotated assertion entailment
but not for annotated GCI entailment because
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
is not multiplicatively idempotent. Consider for example
\mathcal{O}=\{(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,x),(\exists P^{-}.B\sqsubseteq C,y),(\exists P.C\sqsubseteq D,z)\}
. One can check that
(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq D,xyz)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
. However, the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated interpretation
\mathcal{I}
such that
A^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,u+v)\}
,
B^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,w)\}
,
P^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,d,ux+vx)\}
,
C^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(d,uwxy+vwxy)\}
, and
D^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(e,uwxy+vwxy+uvwxy)\}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}
such that
(e,uw+vw)\in(A\sqcap B)^{\mathcal{I}}
while
(e,uwxyz+vwxyz)\notin D^{\mathcal{I}}
because
(u+v)^{2}\neq(u+v)
in the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
semiring.

Due to Theorems
6.4
and
4.1
,
we obtain the following corollaries. Note that if an assertion
\alpha
is of the form
R(a,b)
with
a
or
b
in
{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, one can directly conclude that
\mathcal{O}\not\models(\alpha,m)
if
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable, so Theorem
6.4
shows that the completion algorithm can be used to decide entailment of any annotated assertion. Moreover, for every assertion
\alpha
,
if
m
is a monomial in
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
(hence without variable repetition) such that
|m|>|\mathcal{O}|
,
m
contains variables that do not occur in
\mathcal{O}
and one can directly conclude that
\mathcal{O}\not\models(\alpha,m)
if
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable, so the size of
m
does not matter.

Corollary 6.6
.

If
\mathcal{O}
is a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology, for every assertion

\alpha
and monomial
m\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
,
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
is decidable in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.

Corollary 6.7
.

If
\mathcal{O}
is a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology, for every assertion

\alpha
,
the provenance
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O})=\sum_{\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)}m
can be computed in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.

Since subsumption checking in
\mathcal{ELI}
is
ExpTime
-hard (?) and assertion entailment in
\mathcal{ELI}
can be reduced to subsumption checking, we obtain a matching lower bound for the annotated assertion entailment problem and cannot hope for computing the why-provenance of axioms in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
more efficiently.
However, there is an additional source of complexity when considering provenance, as illustrated by the following example that shows that even if the ontology is formulated in a simple DL in which entailment can be checked in polynomial time, there may be exponentially many monomials
m
such that
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
.

Example 6.8
.

Consider the ontology

\mathcal{O}=\{(A\sqsubseteq A_{i},v_{i}),(A_{i}\sqsubseteq B,u_{i})\mid 0\leq i\leq n\}\cup\{(B(a),x),(B\sqsubseteq A,u)\}.

For every
S\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
,
\mathcal{O}\models(A(a),x\times u\times\Pi_{i\in S}u_{i}\times v_{i})
.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

If one is only interested in checking the entailment of a given annotated assertion
(\alpha,m)
, it is possible
to apply the completion algorithm using only the axioms annotated with relevant variables. More precisely, one can use
the ontology

\mathcal{O}_{m}=\{(\beta,v)\mid(\beta,v)\in\mathcal{O},v\text{ occurs in }m\}\cup\{(\beta,1)\mid(\beta,1)\in\mathcal{O}\}

instead of
\mathcal{O}
. In the case where each axiom of
\mathcal{O}
is annotated with a distinct variable,
\mathcal{O}_{m}
has exactly
|m|
axioms and
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
is decidable in exponential time w.r.t.
|m|
.

6.1.2
The Case of Lightweight DLs

We show that in the fragment
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
(cf. Definition
2.1
), the exponential complexity of deciding annotated assertion entailment comes only from the size of the monomial.
Intuitively, this comes from the fact that in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
, the axioms
A\sqsubseteq\exists Q
,
Q\sqsubseteq P_{i}
and
\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i}
in the premise of
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
have to be such that
A_{i}=\top
, which means that the conclusion is simply
A\sqsubseteq D
. This allows us to modify the completion rules in a way that avoids introducing conjunctions of more than two concept names, and hence, the number of (non-annotated) GCIs that can be constructed remains polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
. Recall that by definition of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
, an ontology that belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
is already in normal form.

•

\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
is restricted by fixing
|M|\leq 2
and
N
is empty, i.e. :

“If
\mathcal{S}
contains
(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq A,m_{1})
and
(A\sqsubseteq C,m_{2})
, then
\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}\cup\{\Phi\}
where

\Phi=(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C,{m_{1}\times m_{2}})
.”

•

\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
is restricted by fixing
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
and
A_{i}=\top
for every
1\leq i\leq k
, i.e. :

“Given
k,k^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
, if
\mathcal{S}
contains
(A\sqsubseteq\exists Q,m_{0})
,
(Q\sqsubseteq P,m)
,
(Q\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{i})
,
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},n_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq k
,

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},o_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
,

(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq C,n)
, and
(\exists P.C\sqsubseteq D,o)
,
then
\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}\cup\{\Phi\}
where

\Phi=(A\sqsubseteq D,m\times n\times o\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{i}\times n_{i})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}o_{i})
.”

•

\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
is defined by:

“Given
k,k^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
, if
\mathcal{S}
contains
(P\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{i}),(\exists P_{i}.\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},n_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq k
,

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},o_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
, and

(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq C,n)
,
then
\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}\cup\{\Phi\}
where

\Phi=(\exists P.\top\sqsubseteq C,n\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{i}\times n_{i})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}o_{i})
.”

•

\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{5}
is defined by:

“If
\mathcal{S}
contains
(\top\sqsubseteq A_{1},m_{1})
,
(\top\sqsubseteq A_{2},m_{2})
and
(A_{1}\sqcap A_{2}\sqsubseteq B,n)
, then
\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}\cup\{\Phi\}
where

\Phi=(\top\sqsubseteq B,m_{1}\times m_{2}\times n)
.”

Figure 2:
Modified completion rules for the case where
\mathcal{O}
belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
. Note that in
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
, the conjunction
B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}
contains at most two concept names since
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
.

We modify the completion algorithm presented in Section
6.1.1
as follows.
First, we modify the rules from Table
3
as explained in Figure
2
. Intuitively, we restrict the rules that may introduce exponentially many axioms (
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
) so that they cannot introduce conjunctions in the left anymore. We also need to include two additional rules
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{5}
.
Example
6.10
demonstrates how the modified completion rules work, and in particular why
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{5}
are needed.

Example 6.10
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\{

\displaystyle(A\sqsubseteq\exists R,x),(\exists R^{-}.\top\sqsubseteq B_{1},y_{1}),(\exists R^{-}.\top\sqsubseteq B_{2},y_{2}),(\top\sqsubseteq B_{3},y_{3}),(\top\sqsubseteq B_{4},y_{4}),

\displaystyle(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C_{1},z_{1}),(B_{3}\sqcap B_{4}\sqsubseteq C_{2},z_{2}),(C_{1}\sqcap C_{2}\sqsubseteq C,z_{3}),(\exists R.C\sqsubseteq D,u),(A(a),v)\}.

The completion algorithm for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
that uses the rules from Table
3
derives, among many
others, the following axioms:

•

(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqcap C_{2}\sqsubseteq C,z_{1}z_{3})
with
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
;

•

(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqcap B_{3}\sqcap B_{4}\sqsubseteq C,z_{1}z_{2}z_{3})
with
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
;

•

(A\sqsubseteq D,xy_{1}y_{2}y_{3}y_{4}z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}u)
with
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
;

•

(D(a),xy_{1}y_{2}y_{3}y_{4}z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}uv)
with
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
.

In contrast, the completion algorithm for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
that uses the modified rules derives the following axioms (among others):

•

(\exists R^{-}.\top\sqsubseteq C_{1},y_{1}y_{2}z_{1})
with
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
;

•

(\top\sqsubseteq C_{2},y_{3}y_{4}z_{2})
with
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{5}
;

•

(A\sqsubseteq D,xy_{1}y_{2}y_{3}y_{4}z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}u)
with the modified version of
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
;

•

(D(a),xy_{1}y_{2}y_{3}y_{4}z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}uv)
with
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
.

Intuitively,
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{5}
allow us to derive the “range information” for
R
that was directly handled in
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
when the size of the conjunction in the left was not bounded.

∎

Second, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
be the set of annotated axioms obtained from
\mathcal{S}
(defined as in Section
6.1.1
) through the modified completion rules restricted to
monomials of size at most
k
(i.e. that have at most
k
variables). We call
\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
the
k
-saturation of
\mathcal{O}
.
The next theorem states that
\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
can be computed in polynomial time w.r.t. the size of
\mathcal{O}
and exponential time w.r.t.
k
,
and that when
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable,
\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
suffices for deciding entailment of all annotated assertions
(\alpha,m)
with
|m|\leq k
.

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontology belonging to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
,
m\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
, and
\alpha
an assertion.

1.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, if
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
, then
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
. Moreover, if
\alpha=A(a_{\top})
, then
\mathcal{O}\models(A(c),m)
for every
c\in{\sf N_{I}}
.

2.

If
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable and
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
with
|m|\leq k
,

(a)

if
\alpha
is of the form
A(a)
or
R(a,b)
with
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, then
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
;

(b)

if
\alpha
is of the form
A(c)
with
c\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, then
(A(a_{\top}),m)\in\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
.

3.

\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
is computable in
p(|\mathcal{O}|^{k})
where
p
is a polynomial function.

Moreover, the algorithm can be modified by ignoring the monomials in the annotated axioms to check in polynomial time whether
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable (which shows the complexity of satisfiability in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
stated in Theorem
2.2
). It follows that to decide whether
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
, we can first check that
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable in polynomial time, then compute
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
in polynomial time w.r.t. the size of
\mathcal{O}
and exponential time w.r.t.
|m|
.

Corollary 6.12
.

If
\mathcal{O}
is a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontology in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
then, for every assertion

\alpha
and monomial
m
,
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
is decidable in
p(|\mathcal{O}|^{|m|})
where
p
is a polynomial function.

Remark 6.13
.

As explained in Remark
6.9
, one can apply the completion algorithm using only the axioms annotated with variables from
m
or
1
, and obtain a bound in
p(|m|^{|m|})
in the case where each axiom of
\mathcal{O}
is annotated with a distinct variable.

Inspired by ? (?), who showed how to transform a completion algorithm into a tree automaton, and ? (?), who gave conditions on tree automata to obtain
PSpace
algorithms, we obtain an improved
PSpace
upper bound for the problem of annotated assertion entailment in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
.

Theorem 6.14
.

If
\mathcal{O}
is a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontology belonging to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
, then for every assertion

\alpha

and monomial
m
,
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
is decidable in
PSpace
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.2
, one can check in polynomial time whether
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable. If it is not the case,
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
trivially. Otherwise, by Theorem
6.11
,
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
iff
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
(or
(A(a_{\top}),m)\in\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
in the case where
\alpha=A(c)
for some
c\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
). This is equivalent to the existence of a derivation tree w.r.t. the completion algorithm, i.e. a labelled tree
t
such that (i) the root of
t
is labelled by
(\alpha,m)
, (ii) every leaf of
t
is labelled by some annotated axiom in
\mathcal{S}
(which can be constructed in polynomial time), and (iii) every non-leaf node is labelled by an annotated axiom
(\beta,n)
and its children’s labels
(\beta_{1},n_{1}),\dots,(\beta_{\ell},n_{\ell})
are such that
(\beta_{1},n_{1}),\dots,(\beta_{\ell},n_{\ell})
and
(\beta,n)
correspond respectively to the premises and conclusion of the instantiation of a completion rule (using the modified completion rules for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
). Such a tree has arity bounded by 8 (maximum number of premises in the completion rules for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
), and its depth is polynomially bounded (indeed, for every leaf, the monomial in the node labels on the path from the leaf to the root can only increases, and the number of (non-annotated) axioms that can be built by the completion rules for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
is polynomial). Moreover, the size of each node label (annotated axiom) is polynomial, and for each node, there are only exponentially many options of possible children so one can guess a node’s children in polynomial time. It is thus possible to define a non-deterministic algorithm that checks whether a derivation tree for
(\alpha,m)
exists using depth-first search.
∎

6.2
Conjunctive Queries over
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
Ontologies

We now provide algorithms to decide entailment of annotated BCQs and compute the provenance of BCQs w.r.t.
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontologies. For the rest of this section,
\mathcal{O}
is a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology in normal form and
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
is the result of saturating
\mathcal{O}
.

We have seen that
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
is expressive enough to reduce entailment of rooted tree-shaped BCQs to assertion entailment (cf. Theorem
3.11
).
However, this does not apply to general BCQs that may, for example, contain cycles. To deal with such queries, we adapt the method for conjunctive query answering in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
described by ? (?, Section 5.2). In a nutshell, the idea is to rewrite some parts of the query w.r.t. the saturated ontology into a query that can be matched in the assertions of the saturated ontology.
Before introducing the query rewriting process formally, we illustrate the approach on an example.

Example 6.15
.

Consider
q(x)=\exists yD(x)\wedge E(y)\wedge P(x,y)\wedge R(y,x)
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\{

\displaystyle(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v_{1}),(P\sqsubseteq R^{-},v_{2}),(\exists R.B\sqsubseteq C,v_{3}),(\exists P.C\sqsubseteq D,v_{4}),(C\sqsubseteq E,v_{5}),

\displaystyle(A(a),u_{1}),(B(a),u_{2})\}.

In every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
there exists
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(a^{\mathcal{I}},e,u_{1}v_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(e,a^{\mathcal{I}},u_{1}v_{1}v_{2})\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(e,u_{1}u_{2}v_{1}v_{2}v_{3})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(a^{\mathcal{I}},u_{1}u_{2}v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}v_{4})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(e,u_{1}u_{2}v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}v_{5})\in E^{\mathcal{I}}
, so there is a match for
\boldsymbol{q(a)}
in
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{O}\models(q(a),u_{1}u_{2}v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}v_{4}v_{5})
.

Our algorithm starts by computing
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. One can verify that
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
extends
\mathcal{O}
with the tautologies added upon initialization (in particular,
(P\sqsubseteq P,1)
) and the two following axioms:
(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq D,v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}v_{4})
and
(D(a),u_{1}u_{2}v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}v_{4})
.

Then the algorithm rewrites
q
into a set of CQs using the GCIs and RIs of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, while keeping track of the annotations of the axioms used in the process. The goal of the rewriting is to
eliminate some existentially quantified variables
. Since our example query has only one existential variable
y
, our goal is to rewrite
q
into a conjunction of atoms that do not contain
y
. Intuitively, this is done with the following steps.

•

Write
q
as
\exists yD(x)\wedge E(y)\wedge P(x,y)\wedge R^{-}(x,y)
, so that
y
occurs in second position of role atoms.

•

Consider the following axioms of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
:
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v_{1})
,
(P\sqsubseteq P,1)
,
(P\sqsubseteq R^{-},v_{2})
,
(C\sqsubseteq E,v_{5})
,
(\exists R.B\sqsubseteq C,v_{3})
. They allow us to rewrite
\exists yE(y)\wedge P(x,y)\wedge R^{-}(x,y)
into
A(x)\wedge B(x)
.

•

We obtain the CQ
q^{\prime}(x)=D(x)\wedge A(x)\wedge B(x)
associated with the monomial
v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}v_{5}
that keeps track of the axioms used for this rewriting.

Since there is no other option to rewrite
\exists yE(y)\wedge P(x,y)\wedge R^{-}(x,y)
and there is no other existentially quantified variable to consider, the full rewriting is the set of pairs of a CQ and a monomial
\{(q(x),1),(q^{\prime}(x),v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}v_{5})\}
.
Finally, we evaluate all CQs of the rewriting in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. There is no match for
\boldsymbol{q(a)}
but there is one for
\boldsymbol{q^{\prime}(a)}
that uses the three annotated assertions of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, so we multiply the monomial of
q^{\prime}(x)
with the monomials of the annotated assertions to obtain that
\mathcal{O}\models(q(a),u_{1}u_{2}v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}v_{4}v_{5})
.
∎

In the following definition, the intuition behind
(q,m)\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q^{\prime},m^{\prime})
is that the CQ
q^{\prime}
can be obtained from
q
by a rewriting step that removes an existential variable from
q
and
m^{\prime}
is the product of
m
and the annotations of the axioms used to rewrite
q
into
q^{\prime}
.

Definition 6.16

(Adapted from Definition 8 of ? (?))
.

For a CQ
q
, a monomial
m
and a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}
in normal form, we write
(q,m)\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q^{\prime},m^{\prime})
if
(q^{\prime},m^{\prime})
can be obtained from
(q,m)
by applying the following steps.

(S1)

Select in
q
an arbitrary existentially quantified variable
x_{0}
such that there are no atoms of the form
R(x_{0},x_{0})
in
q
.

(S2)

Replace each role atom of the form
R(x_{0},y)
in
q
, where
y
and
R
are arbitrary, by the atom
\mathsf{inv}(R)(y,x_{0})
.

(S3)

Let
V_{p}=\{y\mid Q(y,x_{0})\in q\text{ for some }Q\}
and select some
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)\in\mathcal{O}
(recall that
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
since
\mathcal{O}
is in normal form) such that:

(a)

for every
Q(y,x_{0})\in q
, there is some
(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
;

(b)

for every
C(x_{0})\in q
, there exist
p,p^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
(B_{1}{\sqcap}\dots{\sqcap}B_{p}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}{\sqcap}\dots{\sqcap}B^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and

*

for every
1\leq i\leq p
, there exist
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i},v_{i})\in\mathcal{O}
and
(P\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and

*

for every
1\leq i\leq p^{\prime}
, there exists
(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},o_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

For the selected
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)\in\mathcal{O}
, let
At(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)=\{A\}
and
mon(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)=v
.

(a)

For every
Q(y,x_{0})\in q
, arbitrarily select one
(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})
in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and update
mon(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\leftarrow mon(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\times m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}
.

(b)

For every
C(x_{0})\in q
, arbitrarily select some
(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{p}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, for every
1\leq i\leq p
select arbitrarily a pair
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i},v_{i})\in\mathcal{O}
and
(P\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, and for every
1\leq i\leq p^{\prime}
select arbitrarily some
(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},o_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
Update
At(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\leftarrow At(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\cup\{A_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq p\}
and
mon(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\leftarrow mon(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\times n_{C}\times m_{C}
where
m_{C}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}(v_{i}\times m_{i})\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{\prime}}o_{i}
.

(S4)

Drop from
q
every atom that contains
x_{0}
.

(S5)

Select a variable
y_{0}\in V_{p}
and replace every occurrence of every
y^{\prime}\in V_{p}
in
q
by
y_{0}
.

(S6)

Add the atoms
\{D(y_{0})\mid D\in At(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\}
to
q
and multiply
m
by
mon(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
.

We write
(q,1)\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}^{*}(q^{*},m^{*})
if
(q,1)=(q_{0},m_{0})
and
(q^{*},m^{*})=(q_{k},m_{k})
for some finite rewriting sequence
(q_{0},m_{0})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{1},m_{1})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}\dots\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{k},m_{k})
for
k\geq 0
, and we say that
the set
\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})=\{(q^{*},m^{*})\mid(q,1)\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}^{*}(q^{*},m^{*})\}
is the

annotated rewriting of
q
w.r.t.
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

Note that there may be exponentially many pairs of the form
(q^{*},m_{1}),(q^{*},m_{2}),\dots
in
\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
since the same query
q^{*}
can be obtained by choosing different annotated inclusions in the rewriting steps and an axiom can be annotated with exponentially many monomials in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(e.g. in Example
6.8
,
(B\sqsubseteq A,u\times\Pi_{i\in S}u_{i}\times v_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
for every
S\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
).

Theorem 6.17
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a satisfiable
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology in normal form,
q(\vec{x})
be a CQ that does not contain any individual name from
{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
\vec{a}
be a tuple of individuals from
\mathcal{O}
of the same length as
\vec{x}
. We denote by
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
the non-annotated version of
\mathcal{O}
, by
\mathcal{D}
the set of annotated assertions in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, by
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}
the annotated interpretation with domain
{\sf N_{I}}
that satisfies exactly the annotated assertions in
\mathcal{D}
, and by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
the corresponding set of (non-annotated) assertions. Then

1.

\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\models q(\vec{a})
iff there is
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
such that there is a match for
q^{*}(\vec{a})
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

2.

For every
m\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
,
\mathcal{O}\models(q(\vec{a}),m)
iff there exist
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
and
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}(\vec{a})}})
such that
m=m^{*}\times o
.

3.

\mathcal{P}(q(\vec{a}),\mathcal{O})=\sum_{(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})}m^{*}\times\Sigma_{o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}(\vec{a})}})}o
.

4.

\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
is computable in exponential time in
|\mathcal{O}|+|q|
.

We show that for every
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
,
(q^{*},m^{*})
is of polynomial size. Since by Theorem
6.4
,
\mathcal{D}
can be computed in exponential time, hence is of exponential size, deciding whether
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}(\vec{a})}})
or computing
\Sigma_{o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}(\vec{a})}})}o
can be done in exponential time in
|\mathcal{O}|+|q|
(by finding all matches for
\boldsymbol{q^{*}(\vec{a})}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}
).
This yields the following corollaries of Theorem
6.17
. Again, note that for every BCQ
q
, if
m
is a monomial in
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
such that
|m|>|\mathcal{O}|
,
m
contains variables that do not occur in
\mathcal{O}
and one can directly conclude that
\mathcal{O}\not\models(q,m)
if
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable, so the size of
m
does not matter.

Corollary 6.18
.

If
\mathcal{O}
is a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology, then for every BCQ
q
that does not contain any individual name from
{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
m\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
,

\mathcal{O}\models(q,m)
is decidable in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|+|q|
.

Corollary 6.19
.

If
\mathcal{O}
is a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology, then for every BCQ
q
that does not contain any individual name from
{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
,
the provenance
\mathcal{P}(q,\mathcal{O})
can be computed in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|+|q|
.

Since BCQ entailment is already
ExpTime
-complete for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
without annotations (?), we cannot hope for a better complexity upper bound.
However, if we restrict ourselves to ontologies expressed in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
, we can obtain a result similar to Corollary
6.12
for annotated BCQ entailment.

Theorem 6.20
.

If
\mathcal{O}
is a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
ontology, then for every BCQ
q
that does not contain any individual name from
{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and monomial
m
,

\mathcal{O}\models(q,m)
is decidable in
NP
w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|+|q|
(with
|m|
fixed), and in exponential time in
|m|
.

A matching
NP
lower bound comes from the combined complexity of standard BCQ entailment over databases.
Note that for the specific cases of
DL-Lite
R
and
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
, ? (?) and ? (?) proposed algorithms for annotated BCQ entailment with some
annotations based on monomials, respectively by adapting classical query rewriting algorithm for
DL-Lite
R
(?) and the combined approach based on the computation of a compact canonical model for
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
(?).

7
Provenance in the
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
Semirings

In this section, we investigate provenance in the
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
semirings, which correspond to well-known notions of provenance in the database setting.
Similarly to the preceding section, we focus on ontologies annotated with variables (or
1
) rather than any element from
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
or
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
. A
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
(resp.
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
)-annotated ontology thus means
\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
with
\lambda_{\sf X}:\mathcal{O}\mapsto{\sf X}\cup\{1\}
and we often omit the superscripts and identify
\mathcal{O}
and
\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
.
Recall from Section
2.3
that
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]=(\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}],\vee,\wedge,0,1)
is the semiring of positive Boolean functions over
{\sf X}
and that
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]=(\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}],\cup,\cup^{*},\emptyset^{*},\emptyset)
where
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
is the set of all subsets of
{\sf X}
extended with
\emptyset^{*}
and for every
S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq{\sf X}
,
S_{1}\cup^{*}S_{2}=S_{1}\cup S_{2}
.
Also recall that we often represent elements of
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
or
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
as polynomials: elements of
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
are identified with their irredundant disjunctive normal form and represented by a sum of monomials, and elements of
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
are represented by monomials.

Given a satisfiable
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}
annotated by elements from
{\sf X}\cup\{1\}
, if we denote by
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]}
this ontology interpreted as a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-,
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
- and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontology respectively, it is easy to verify that the three annotated ontologies share the same canonical model (if the annotations are represented by monomials). The following proposition thus follows from Theorems
4.5
and
4.9
(since the statement holds trivially in the cases where
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable or
\alpha
is an RI with an unsatisfiable left-hand side by Remarks
3.1
and
3.2
).

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology,
\lambda_{\sf X}:\mathcal{O}\mapsto{\sf X}\cup\{1\}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]}
defined by
\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
. For every BCQ, assertion, or RI
\alpha
and monomial
m
,
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}\models(\alpha,m)
iff
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}\models(\alpha,m)
iff
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]}\models(\alpha,m)
.

Remark 7.2
.

Example
5.3
shows that the proposition does not hold if
\alpha
is a GCI because
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
is not
\otimes
-idempotent: if
\mathcal{O}=\{(A\sqsubseteq B_{1},x_{1}),(A\sqsubseteq B_{2},x_{2}),(B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq C,x_{3})\}
, since
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
are
\otimes
-idempotent,
{\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}}\models(A\sqsubseteq C,x_{1}x_{2}x_{3})
and
{\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]}}\models(A\sqsubseteq C,x_{1}x_{2}x_{3})
but
{\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}}\not\models(A\sqsubseteq C,x_{1}x_{2}x_{3})
.

However, the
provenance
differs depending on the semiring considered, since the additions have different properties. Since
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
is absorptive (intuitively,
x+xy=x
since the Boolean function
x\vee(x\wedge y)
is equivalent to
x
), the
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-provenance of a BCQ, assertion or RI
\alpha
corresponds to the sum of
minimal
monomials that occur in its
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance. Regarding
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
, for all monomials
m
and
n
,
m+n=m\times n
so the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-provenance of
\alpha
corresponds to the product of all variables that occur in its
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance. These relationships are well-known in the database setting.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]}
be as in Proposition
7.1
and
\alpha
be a BCQ, assertion, or RI.

•

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]})=\Sigma_{m\in M}m
where
M
is the set of monomials
m
such that
m
occurs in
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]})
and for every
n\neq m
that occurs in
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]})
, there exists a variable that occurs in
n
and not in
m
.

•

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]})=\Pi_{v\in V}v
where
V=\{v\mid v\in{\sf X}\text{ occurs in }\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]})\}
.

7.1
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-Provenance

7.1.1
Relationship to Axiom Pinpointing

Provenance over the
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
semiring is closely related to what has been thoroughly studied in the DL literature under the name of

axiom pinpointing

(originally coined by ?). In this context, the goal is
to find one or all the
minimal
(w.r.t. set inclusion) subsets of axioms that entail a given consequence. These
sets are called the
justifications
for the consequence (?).
This definition of justifications can be straightforwardly extended to justifications for BCQs.
Proposition
7.4
establishes the connection between
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-provenance and justifications. The Boolean formula
\bigvee_{\mathcal{J}\in{\sf Just_{\mathcal{O}}}(\alpha)}\ \bigwedge_{\beta\in\mathcal{J}}\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)
in the proposition statement is a
pinpointing formula
, defined in the DL literature as a monotone Boolean formula whose satisfying valuations correspond exactly to subsets of the ontology that entail the consequence (?). Indeed, since description logics are monotone, every superset of a justification also entails the consequence so the formula is equivalent to
\bigvee_{\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}\models\alpha}\ \bigwedge_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\langle\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
be a satisfiable
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology. If (i)
\alpha
is a BCQ, an assertion, or an RI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
, or (ii)
\alpha
is a GCI between basic concepts whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, then

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O})=\bigvee_{\mathcal{J}\in{\sf Just_{\mathcal{O}}}(\alpha)}\ \bigwedge_{\beta\in\mathcal{J}}\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)

where
{\sf Just_{\mathcal{O}}}(\alpha)
denotes the set of all justifications for
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

Remark 7.5
.

One can check that the conditions imposed on
\alpha
and
\mathcal{O}
are necessary with the usual examples. Example
5.2
shows that Proposition
7.4
does not apply to GCIs with conjunctions in the left.
Regarding the satisfiability of the left-hand side of
\alpha
, if
\mathcal{O}=\{(A\sqsubseteq B,v_{1}),(A\sqsubseteq C,v_{2}),(B\sqcap C\sqsubseteq\bot,v_{3})\}
, then
\mathcal{O}\models A\sqsubseteq D
for every
D\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\{A\}
and the only justification for
\mathcal{O}\models A\sqsubseteq D
is
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
itself. However,
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq D,\mathcal{O})
is the sum over all elements of
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
so is equal to 1. Finally, if
\mathcal{O}=\{(\top\sqsubseteq D,x)\}
, for every
C\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\{D\}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is a justification for
C\sqsubseteq D
but
\mathcal{P}(C\sqsubseteq D,\mathcal{O})=0
(cf. Example
3.14
).

In the context of axiom pinpointing, it is common to allow for static axioms to capture cases where, for instance, the assertions or the GCIs and RIs are
considered to be immutable, among many other situations that appear in different formalisms
(?, ?, ?).
Formally, we consider that a DL ontology
\mathcal{O}
is partitioned into two subsets
\mathcal{O}:=\mathcal{O}_{s}\cup\mathcal{O}_{r}
where

\mathcal{O}_{s}
is the class of
static
axioms, which are assumed to always hold, and
\mathcal{O}_{r}
is the class of

refutable
axioms which take part in the justifications.
In particular, to the best of our knowledge, the only systematic study on
the computation of BCQ justifications considers justifications to be sets of assertions, i.e. GCIs and RIs to be static (?).
To handle this case, we may simply annotate the axioms of
\mathcal{O}_{s}
with
1
and those of
\mathcal{O}_{r}
with distinct variables.

It is worth noting that the problem of axiom pinpointing has been studied, under different names, in many other
communities like—among many others—propositional satisfiability (?), process modelling (?), and
answer set programming (?).

7.1.2
Computing
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-Provenance

There exist two main approaches for finding and enumerating the justifications for a given consequence of an
ontology. The
black-box
approach simply calls an existing (classical) reasoner repeatedly to prune out
superfluous axioms (?); the
glass-box
approach, on the other hand,
modifies the reasoner to compute the justifications directly. The specific glass-box technique needed obviously depends
on the characteristics of the underlying reasoner, but general frameworks have been developed for tableaux
(?), automata (?), and consequence-based
(?) methods. Within the context of
{\cal E\!LHI}_{\bot}
, it has also been proposed to reduce the
problem to an enumeration in SAT (?, ?, ?) or ASP (?, ?).

Since
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-provenance can be obtained from
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance by removing the non-minimal monomials, the algorithms we gave in Section
6
provide a glass-box approach to compute the set of all justifications for an assertion or a BCQ in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
. This is, in particular, interesting for BCQs for which the problem of finding all justifications that take into account axioms beyond assertions has not been considered yet, while GCIs and RIs are usually crucial to explain why a BCQ is entailed from a DL ontology to a user that may not have in mind all the semantics relationships between concepts and roles expressed in the ontology.
Glass-box approaches have the advantage of being streamlined: contrary to black-box methods, a glass-box algorithm needs
to be executed only once to find one or all justifications, making them much more efficient. For instance, the only method
capable of enumerating all justifications for all consequences of the very large
{\cal E\!LH}
ontology
Snomed
is the
glass-box based PuLi (?). The cost of this efficiency is that implementations and optimizations need to be
developed anew.

A direct consequence of the black-box methods is that one justification can be computed (or verified)
with a polynomial number of calls to a classical reasoner. This means that a justification in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
can be found
in exponential time, and this bound reduces to polynomial time in any sublogic of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
which allows for
polynomial-time reasoning. On the other hand, it is known that a single consequence may have exponentially many
justifications (?). This means that enumerating all justifications (that is, finding the full

\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-provenance polynomial in extended form) necessarily requires exponential time. Through a more fine-grained complexity
analysis, ? (?) showed that, unless
\textsc{PTime}=\textsc{NP}
,
there exists no algorithm that can compute all justifications w.r.t. an
{\cal E\!L}
ontology
in polynomial time
even if only polynomially many justifications exist
.

7.1.3
Applications of
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-provenance

Recall that
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
specializes correctly to every commutative,
\otimes
-idempotent and absorptive semiring. Since it is idempotent for both operations, by Theorem
5.12
, it follows that for every commutative semiring
\mathbb{K}
that is
\otimes
-idempotent and absorptive, every satisfiable
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}

\mathbb{K}
-annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
, and every
\alpha
, if (i)
\alpha
is a BCQ, assertion or RI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
or (ii)
\alpha
is a GCI between basic concepts whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, then the provenance
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
can be computed as follows. First, compute
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]})
where
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
with
\lambda_{\sf X}
an injective function from the axioms in
\mathcal{O}
to the set of variables
{\sf X}
. Then evaluate the obtained polynomial through the unique semiring homomorphism
h
from
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
to
\mathbb{K}
such that
h(\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta))=\lambda(\beta)
for every
\beta\in\mathcal{O}
and
h(x)=\mathbb{0}
for every
x\in{\sf X}\setminus\{\lambda_{X}(\beta)\mid\beta\in\mathcal{O}\}
. Many useful semirings are
\otimes
-idempotent and absorptive, so
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-provenance is particularly interesting.
For example, we have shown in Section
3.3
that provenance in the fuzzy semiring captures the semantics of fuzzy or possibilistic DLs, and that provenance in the semiring that corresponds to a bounded distributive lattice allows to compute access right in the setting defined by ? (?).

The set of justifications of a query has also proven to be useful to characterize other semantics for annotated databases or DL ontologies. This is in particular the case for
probabilistic
databases (?) and probabilistic DLs (?, ?, ?). In this context, axioms are annotated either directly with probability values or with some Boolean events associated with a probability of being true, and the probability of a query is the sum of the probabilities of the worlds in which the query is true. It has been shown that the query probability is equal to the probability of the Boolean formula that corresponds to the
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-provenance.

7.2
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-Provenance

7.2.1
Computing
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-Provenance of Axioms

Since the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-provenance of an assertion can be obtained from its
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance by taking the product of all variables that occur in the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance (Proposition
7.3
), it can be computed in exponential time using the techniques from Section
6.1
. However, we show that if we modify the completion algorithm from Section
6.1
to combine all monomials that annotate an axiom instead of storing them separately, one can compute the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-
provenance of all assertions in
polynomial time
if the ontology belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
.

As in Section
6.1
,
the algorithm assumes normal form and keeps as data structure
a set
\mathcal{S}
of annotated axioms
(\alpha,m)
,
where
\alpha
uses the vocabulary of
\mathcal{O}
(extended with
a_{\top}
and possibly
\top
,
\bot
), and
m\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
.

\mathcal{S}
is initialised as in Section
6.1

and extended by exhaustively applying the rules in Table
3
, where rule applications are modified by replacing
\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}\cup\{\Phi\}
by
\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}\Cup\{\Phi\}
with

\mathcal{S}\Cup\{(\alpha,m)\}:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{S}\cup\{(\alpha,m)\}\text{ if there is no $(\alpha,n)\in\mathcal{S}$}\\
\mathcal{S}\setminus\{(\alpha,n)\}\cup\{(\alpha,m\times n)\}\text{ if $(\alpha,n)\in\mathcal{S}$;}\end{cases}

i.e., add the
axiom
\alpha
with an associated monomial if it does not yet appear in
\mathcal{S}
, and modify the monomial
associated to
\alpha
to include new variables otherwise. Note that since no axiom occurs several times with different annotations when
\mathcal{S}
is initialized, there is always at most one
(\alpha,n)\in\mathcal{S}
for a given
\alpha
. To ensure termination, a rule is applicable only if its conclusion is not in
\mathcal{S}
(note that each rule application either adds an annotated axiom or adds some variables to some monomial, and the monomial size is bounded by the number of axioms in
\mathcal{O}
).
The rules are applied until no new rule is applicable; i.e.,
\mathcal{S}
is
saturated
.

Example 7.6
.

For the ontology of Example
6.8
, we obtain the saturated set

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=

\displaystyle\{(A(a),x\times m),(B(a),x\times m),(A\sqsubseteq A,m),(B\sqsubseteq B,m),(A\sqsubseteq B,m),(B\sqsubseteq A,m)\}\ \cup

\displaystyle\{(A_{i}\sqsubseteq B,m),(B\sqsubseteq A_{i},m),(A_{i}\sqsubseteq A,m),(A\sqsubseteq A_{i},m)\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\ \cup

\displaystyle\{(A_{i}\sqsubseteq A_{j},m)\mid 1\leq i,j\leq n\}\cup\{(\top(a),1),(\top(a_{\top}),1)\}

with
m=u\times\Pi_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}
.
∎

Theorem 7.7
.

If
\mathcal{O}
is a satisfiable
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology,
\alpha
is an assertion s.t.
\mathcal{O}\models\alpha
, and

\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
is the result of saturating
\mathcal{O}
:

1.

if
\alpha
is of the form
A(a)
or
R(a,b)
with
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, then

(\alpha,\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}))\in\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
and there is no other
m
such that
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
;

2.

if
\alpha=A(c)
for
c\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, then
\mathcal{P}(A(c),\mathcal{O})\!=\!\mathcal{P}(A(a_{\top}),\mathcal{O})
,

(A(a_{\top}),\mathcal{P}(A(a_{\top}),\mathcal{O}))\!\in\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
and there is no other
m
such that
(A(a_{\top}),m)\in\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
.

We show that if
\mathcal{O}
belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
,
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
can be computed in polynomial time using the completion rules modified for this case as in Theorem
6.11
. Indeed, we have seen in Section
6.1.2
that these rules can build only polynomially many different (non-annotated) axioms and since a rule application that modifies a monomial adds at least one variable, each annotated axiom can be modified only a linear number of times.
Moreover, since
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
is multiplicatively idempotent, by Theorems
4.10
and
4.11
, the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-provenance of GCIs between basic concepts and positive RIs can computed via a reduction to the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-provenance of assertions (with the usual condition for the GCI case).

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a satisfiable
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology. If (i)
\alpha
is an assertion
or a positive RI or (ii)
\alpha
is a GCI between basic concepts and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, then the following hold (note that
|\alpha|\leq 3
).

•

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O})
can be computed in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.

•

If
\mathcal{O}
belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
,
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O})
can be computed in polynomial time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.

7.2.2
Relevant Axioms

Given a satisfiable
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}
,
we say that a
variable
v\in{\sf X}
is relevant
to entail an axiom
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
if it occurs in
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O})
. By definition of the addition of the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
semiring, this is equivalent to
the existence of a monomial
m
such that
v
occurs in
m
and
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
.
If the annotation function
\lambda_{\sf X}
is injective and does not map any axiom to
1
, we say that an
axiom
\beta
is relevant
to entail
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
if
\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)
is relevant.
We relate this notion to
usable facts
that have been defined in the context of Datalog provenance as the database facts that occur in some derivation tree for the query (?).
An equivalent definition of usable facts independent of the notion of derivation tree has also been proposed (see “adornment-usable facts”, ?, Section B.3). Adapting this definition to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
characterizes relevant axioms as follows.

Definition 7.9
.

Given an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
axiom
\gamma
, the
adornment by

\gamma
of a concept (resp. role) name
A
(resp.
R
)
is (the fresh name)
A^{\gamma}
(resp.
R^{\gamma}
).

For an assertion
\beta
, let
\beta^{\gamma}
be the assertion obtained by replacing the predicate in
\beta
by its adornment by
\gamma
, and for an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
GCI or RI
\beta
, let
\beta^{\gamma}
be the axiom obtained by replacing the (unique) predicate in the right-hand side of
\beta
by its adornment by
\gamma
.

For an assertion
\beta
, let
f^{\gamma}(\beta)=\{\beta\}
, and for an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
GCI or RI
\beta
, let
f^{\gamma}(\beta)
be the set of all GCIs or RIs obtained from
\beta
by applying the two following steps: (i) replace the predicate in the right-hand side of
\beta
by its adornment by
\gamma
, and (ii) replace a single predicate in the left-hand side of
\beta
by its adornment by
\gamma
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a satisfiable
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology. Given an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
axiom
\alpha
and an axiom
\gamma\in\mathcal{O}
, we say that
\gamma
is
usable to derive

\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
if
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cup\{\gamma^{\gamma}\}\models\alpha^{\gamma}
where
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}=\bigcup_{\beta\in\mathcal{O}}f^{\gamma}(\beta)
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\langle\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
be a satisfiable
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology such that
\lambda_{\sf X}
maps all axioms to distinct variables. If (i)
\alpha
is an assertion or a positive RI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
, or (ii)
\alpha
is a GCI between basic concepts whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, then
\gamma\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is relevant to entail
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
iff it is usable to derive
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

It follows from Theorem
7.8
(or alternatively from Proposition
7.10
, since
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
,
\gamma^{\gamma}
and
\alpha^{\gamma}
can be built in polynomial time) that if we only need to know which axioms of an ontology
\mathcal{O}
are relevant, or usable, to entail an axiom
\alpha
(where
\mathcal{O}
is a satisfiable
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
or
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
ontology and
\alpha
is as in Proposition
7.10
), the complexity is the same as classical reasoning in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
or
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
.
This contrasts with the axiom pinpointing setting in which
deciding whether an axiom belongs to a justification is

NP
-hard for Horn-
{\cal E\!L}
(?).
This is because axiom pinpointing requires that justifications are
minimal
: if

\mathcal{O}=\{(A\sqsubseteq B,v_{1}),(B\sqsubseteq C,v_{2}),(C\sqsubseteq B,v_{3})\}
, the only justification for
A\sqsubseteq B
is
\{A\sqsubseteq B\}
but
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq B,\mathcal{O})=v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}
(since
\mathcal{O}\models(A\sqsubseteq B,v_{1})
and
\mathcal{O}\models(A\sqsubseteq B,v_{1}v_{2}v_{3})
) and, in particular,
v_{2}
and
v_{3}

are relevant to entail
A\sqsubseteq B
(i.e.
B\sqsubseteq C
and
C\sqsubseteq B
are relevant).

7.2.3
Relationship with Lean Kernels

Provenance in the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
semiring is related to
lean kernels
(?),
which approximate the union of justifications.
? (?) define lean kernels w.r.t. a
consequence-based method
, defined as an algorithm that works on a set of axioms and uses rules to extend this set, until the set becomes saturated and consequences can be read from the saturated set.
The lean kernel of a consequence
\alpha
(being an assertion or subsumption between two concept names) is the set of axioms appearing in at least one proof of
\alpha

in a given consequence-based method.
This generalizes the notion from propositional logic,
where a lean kernel is the set of
clauses appearing in a resolution proof for unsatisfiability.
The monomials, or sets of variables, computed by the completion algorithm for
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontologies described in this section correspond to
the sets of axioms used in the derivations by the completion algorithm that ignores the annotations,
which is a consequence-based method for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
, thus correspond to lean kernels w.r.t. this algorithm.

8
Related Work on Semiring Provenance for Description Logics

In this section, we review and discuss other frameworks that use some form of semiring provenance for description logics or very close settings within the semantic web. We refer to Section
3.3
for a discussion about the relationship between our framework and DLs annotated with specific kinds of annotations, to Section
5
for a comparison with the semiring provenance framework for relational databases and Datalog, and to Section
9
for
a discussion of our results and possible future work in light of the literature.

Provenance for lightweight DLs.

The closest works to ours are those by ? (?) and ? (?), who considered
DL-Lite
R
and
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
respectively, which are fragments of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
(in particular, ? (?) imposed the same syntactic restriction on
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
as the one we use in this paper).
In these papers, ontology axioms are annotated by
monomials
(actually
variables
or
1
in the latter paper).
The semantics is defined using annotated interpretations of the form
\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},\Delta_{\text{m}}^{\mathcal{I}},\cdot^{\mathcal{I}})
which interpret monomials by elements of the
domain of monomials

\Delta_{\text{m}}^{\mathcal{I}}
with the constraint that two monomials that are mathematically equal are mapped to the same element.
Such an interpretation satisfies, e.g., an assertion
(A(a),x)
if
(a^{\mathcal{I}},x^{\mathcal{I}})\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
and a GCI
(C\sqsubseteq D,y)
if
(e,m^{\mathcal{I}})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
implies that
(e,(m\times y)^{\mathcal{I}})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
(thus ignoring any
(e,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
\kappa\in\Delta_{\text{m}}^{\mathcal{I}}
is not equal to
m^{\mathcal{I}}
for some monomial
m
).
To avoid counter-intuitive behaviors with conjunction in
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
, ? (?) additionally assumed that
\times
is idempotent (i.e. that
(x\times x)^{\mathcal{I}}=x^{\mathcal{I}}
).
If we interpret a
DL-Lite
R
or
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
ontology
\mathcal{O}
annotated with
monomials as a
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
- or
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{{\sf X}}
in our framework, then for every assertion, GCI, or RI
\alpha
and monomial
m
, it holds that
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
under this semantics iff
\mathcal{O}^{{\sf X}}\models(\alpha,m)
under our semantics.
In the case where
\mathcal{O}^{{\sf X}}
is interpreted as a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontology, this result holds if
\alpha
is an assertion (cf. Proposition
7.1
and Remark
7.2
).
However, ? (?) and ? (?) did not define the semantics of an ontology annotated with elements of an arbitrary commutative semiring.
In contrast, we defined a general semantics, more in line with the database semiring provenance framework where the semantics of annotated databases is defined independently from the specific semiring, and showed how it captures (or does not capture) the semantics of several annotated DLs such as fuzzy or possibilistic DLs (Section
3.3
).
We also studied the properties of the semantics, and, in particular, showed the relationship between our semantics and the classical semiring provenance of relational or Datalog queries (Section
5
), a question that was not considered by ? (?) and ? (?).

From an algorithmic perspective, ? (?) showed how to normalize the ontology, reduced annotated GCIs and RIs entailment to annotated assertions entailment, and provided a completion algorithm for computing annotated assertions entailed by an
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
ontology. We straightforwardly adapted the normalization rules to the case where the annotations can be elements of an arbitrary commutative semiring. To adapt the reductions between different entailment tasks to our semantics, we had to modify them slightly and
restrict our attention to GCIs between basic concepts because the reduction provided by ? (?) relies on the fact that axioms are annotated with variables rather than by the elements of an arbitrary commutative semiring.
Our completion algorithm follows the same idea as the one for
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
but its rules are different to handle
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
. Finally, ? (?) proposed the adaptation of the completion algorithm to compute “relevant variables”, which we reused to compute the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-provenance.

Regarding complexity results, ? (?) showed that in
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
, deciding entailment of an axiom annotated by a monomial is in polynomial time w.r.t. the ontology size (if the monomial size is fixed),
and in polynomial space w.r.t. the whole input size.
Since
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
iff
\mathcal{O}^{{\sf X}}\models(\alpha,m)
where
\mathcal{O}^{{\sf X}}
is
\mathcal{O}
interpreted as a
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
- or
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-ontology and these semirings are fully idempotent,
one can use the reductions from annotated GCI or RI entailment to annotated assertion entailment from Theorems
4.10
and
4.11
and
the complexity results of Corollary
6.12
and Theorem
6.14
to generalize these results to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
(with the restriction that our reduction is for GCIs between basic concepts and ontologies that do not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, but the reduction given by ? (?) without these restrictions could be used for
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontologies).
They also showed that deciding whether a variable is relevant to an entailment (i.e. whether it occurs in a monomial
m
such that
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
) can be decided in polynomial time. Theorem
7.8
generalizes this result to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
(with the same restriction as before).

Annotated BCQ entailment was considered by ? (?) and
? (?). Intuitively, given a BCQ
q
and a
sum of monomials

p
, they define
\mathcal{O}\models(q,p)
iff
p
is included in the provenance of
q
in each model of
\mathcal{O}
. If we restrict the comparison to
p
being a monomial, we obtain the same annotated query entailments under our semantics. However, under the semantics of ? (?) and
? (?), one can also get
\{(R(a,b),v_{1}),(R(b,a),v_{2})\}\models(\exists xyR(x,y)\wedge R(y,x),v_{1}\times v_{2}+v_{1}\times v_{2})
(?, Section 2.4).
? (?) proposed a rewriting algorithm for
DL-Lite
R
to compute all monomials such that
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
, i.e. the provenance of the query as we defined it, and implemented it.
For
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
, ? (?) described an algorithm based on the computation of a model and query rewriting. ? (?) showed that in
DL-Lite
R
, deciding
\mathcal{O}\models(q,p)
is
NP
-complete (even if
\mathcal{O}
is specified by an ontology-based data access instance consisting of an ontology, a set of mappings and a relational database) and ? (?) showed that
\mathcal{O}\models(q,p)
is decidable in exponential time in
{\cal E\!LH}^{r}
. Since
p
is a sum of monomials, this problem differs from the problem of entailment of BCQ annotated with a monomial that we considered for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
in Theorem
6.20
, for which we obtained an
NP
upper bound if the monomial size is fixed, and an exponential time one w.r.t. the monomial size.

Provenance semantics for attributed DL-Lite.

A provenance semantics was considered in the context of
attributed DL-Lite
(?).
Attributed DLs allow for annotating assertions with an arbitrary number of
attribute-value pairs of individual names
(?). For example some assertions may be annotated with source, clearance level and multiplicity, such as
A(a)@[\mathsf{src}:x_{1},\mathsf{classif}:P,\mathsf{mult}:3]
, while some are not annotated at all. GCIs and RIs are used to express constraints on annotations (for example by requiring that a premise of a GCI has a given source to use this GCI).
If we consider the case where (i) each assertion is annotated by a single attribute-value pair of the form
[\mathsf{attr}:x]
with the same attribute
\mathsf{attr}
, and (ii) RIs and GCIs only propagate annotations (in attributed DL syntax:
C@X\sqsubseteq D@X
), then we can see an attributed DL-Lite ontology
\mathcal{O}^{a}
in our setting as an
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
-annotated DL-Lite ontology
