\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]}=\{(\beta,x)\mid\beta@[\mathsf{attr}:x]\in\mathcal{O}^{a},\beta\text{ assertion}\}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq D,1)\mid C@X\sqsubseteq D@X\in\mathcal{O}^{a}\}\cup\{(P\sqsubseteq Q,1)\mid P@X\sqsubseteq Q@X\in\mathcal{O}^{a}\}

with
{\sf X}=\{x\mid\beta@[\mathsf{attr}:x]\in\mathcal{O}^{a}\}
,
keeping the unique common attribute implicit.
The
provenance-interpretations
for attributed DL-Lite defined by ? (?) are required to satisfy a property of closure under sum, which amounts in our simplified context to requiring that, e.g., if
(e,s_{1})
and
(e,s_{2})
are in
A^{\mathcal{I}}
and neither
s_{1}
nor
s_{2}
can be obtained as the sum of
s
and
s^{\prime}
such that
(e,s)
and
(e,s^{\prime})
are in
A^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
(e,s_{1}+s_{2})
is in
A^{\mathcal{I}}
. Since GCIs in DL-Lite cannot have conjunction or qualified existential restriction in the left-hand side, if
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable and
\alpha
is an assertion such that
\mathcal{O}\models\alpha
, the attributed ontology
\mathcal{O}^{a}

entails
\alpha@[\mathsf{attr}:\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]})]
.

Provenance for expressive DLs.

Another notion of provenance was defined by ? (?) for the expressive DL
{\cal ALC}
and arbitrary commutative semirings.
They considered GCIs without annotation and (potentially complex) assertions such that each assertion is associated with a single expression of the form
=\kappa
,
>\kappa
or
\geq\kappa
for some
\kappa\in K
.
In this setting, the semantics is defined by interpretations that map every (negated) simple assertion
(\neg)\alpha
built from a
finite
interpretation domain
\Delta\subseteq{\sf N_{I}}
,
{\sf N_{C}}
, and
{\sf N_{R}}
, to an element of the semiring. Such an interpretation
\pi
is required to be such that
\pi(\alpha)\otimes\pi(\neg\alpha)=\mathbb{0}
for every
\alpha
built from
\Delta
,
{\sf N_{C}}
and
{\sf N_{R}}
. It is extended to interpret complex assertions with, e.g.,
\pi((C\sqcap D)(e))=\pi(C(e))\otimes\pi(D(e))
and
\pi((\exists R.C)(e))=\bigoplus_{d\in\Delta}\pi(R(e,d))\otimes\pi(C(d))
.

A (strong) model of an ontology is an interpretation that satisfies all its assertions and such that for each of its GCIs
C\sqsubseteq D
, for every
e\in\Delta
,
\pi(C(e))\leq\pi(D(e))
(where
a\leq b
iff there exists
c\in K
with
a\oplus c=b
).
If we translate our annotated assertions as “
=\kappa
” assertions in this framework, their semantics is not comparable with ours when we consider the intersection of the two settings (i.e.,
{\cal E\!L}_{\bot}
ontology that complies with our syntactic restriction, with GCIs annotated with
\mathbb{1}
). Indeed, the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}=\{(A(a),x_{1}),(B(a),x_{2}),(A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq A,1)\}
translates into the ontology
\{\pi(A(a))\!=\!x_{1},\ \pi(B(a))\!=\!x_{2},\ A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq A\}
which is not satisfiable according to this semantics (since there is no
\pi
such that
\pi(A(a))=x_{1}
and
\pi(A(a))\geq x_{1}x_{2}
).
If we instead translate our annotated assertions as “
\geq\kappa
” assertions, then given a satisfiable ontology
\mathcal{O}
and an assertion
\alpha
such that
\mathcal{O}\models\alpha
, the possible provenance values of
\alpha
in the framework of ? (?) are all semiring elements
\eta
such that
\eta\geq\kappa
for every
\kappa
such that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
under our semantics.

A notion of weak model was also introduced for ontologies put in some specific form. Intuitively, in this case,
C\sqsubseteq D
requires that
\pi(C(e))\otimes\pi(\neg D(e))=\mathbb{0}
and
C\equiv D
that
\pi(C(e))=\pi(D(e))
. This alternative semantics is still not comparable with ours: the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}=\{(A(a),x),(C(a),y),(A\sqsubseteq B,1)\}
translates into
\{\pi(A(a))\!=\!x,\ \pi(C(a))\!=\!y,\ A\sqsubseteq B\}
or
\{\pi(A(a))\!\geq\!x,\ \pi(C(a))\!\geq\!y,\ A\sqsubseteq B\}
so the possible provenance values for
B(a)
in weak models are all elements of
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
but
0
(in particular,
y
is a possible provenance value for
B(a)
).

From an algorithmic point of view, ? (?) defined tableaux rules that can be applied if (i) the semiring is
absorptive and such that
\geq
is a total order
, and (ii) the ontology does not contain any equality statement (“
=\kappa
”).

Finally, ? (?) proposed
provenance tracking interpretations
. In such interpretation, for each assertion
C(a)
,
\pi(C(a))
is a polynomial whose variables represent literals of the form
\alpha
or
\neg\alpha
where
\alpha
is an assertion
which does not occur in the ontology
.

A recent work by ? (?) considered GCI entailment from expressive ontologies (with a focus on
{\cal ALC}
) annotated with elements of a
\oplus
- and
\otimes
-idempotent commutative semiring. To handle
{\cal ALC}
constructors, and, in particular, negation, the author defined a provenance semantics based on interpretations that do not satisfy the consequence. For each such interpretation, it takes the sum of the labels of the ontology axioms violated by the interpretation, then the provenance is defined as the product of these sums.
When the semiring is not absorptive, the semantics differs from the one defined in this paper. For example, if we consider the ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(A\sqsubseteq B,\kappa),(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa^{\prime})\}
, the provenance of
A\sqsubseteq B
defined by ? is
\kappa\otimes(\kappa\oplus\kappa^{\prime})=\kappa\oplus\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime}
, while
\mathcal{P}(A\sqsubseteq B,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\kappa
.
When the semiring is absorptive, however, the provenance of a consequence
\alpha
defined by ? is equal to
\bigoplus_{\mathcal{J}\in{\sf Just_{\mathcal{O}}}(\alpha)}\ \bigotimes_{\beta\in\mathcal{J}}\lambda(\beta)
(?, Theorem 5), hence corresponds to our semantics if
\mathcal{O}
and
\alpha
are as required by Proposition
7.4
. Indeed, since
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
specializes correctly to every commutative semiring that is
\otimes
-idempotent and absorptive, there exists a unique semiring homomorphism
h
from
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
to
\mathbb{K}
such that
h(\lambda_{\sf X}(\alpha))=\lambda(\alpha)
for every
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}
and
h(x)=\mathbb{0}
for every
x\in{\sf X}\setminus\{\lambda_{\sf X}(\alpha)\mid\alpha\in\mathcal{O}\}
, i.e.
\lambda=h\circ\lambda_{\sf X}
.
By Theorem
5.12
,
h(\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}))=\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
and by Proposition
7.4
,

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]})=\bigvee_{\mathcal{J}\in{\sf Just_{\mathcal{O}}}(\alpha)}\bigwedge_{\beta\in\mathcal{J}}\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)
, so
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\bigoplus_{\mathcal{J}\in{\sf Just_{\mathcal{O}}}(\alpha)}\ \bigotimes_{\beta\in\mathcal{J}}\lambda(\beta)
.

Provenance for the semantic web.

Several works focussed on querying annotated RDF data with SPARQL (?, ?).
In particular, ? (?) considered SPARQL queries on RDF data annotated with values from some arbitrary annotation domain
K
equipped with three binary operations (with
\ominus
to cover SPARQL difference operator) and showed that
(K,\oplus,\otimes,\ominus,0,1)
must be an extension of semiring they called spm-semiring and further studied.
As far as reasoning is concerned,
? (?) defined an algebraic deductive system for RDFS annotated with elements of a
\oplus
-
idempotent semiring (not necessarily commutative), and
? (?) considered RDFS annotated with elements of a
\oplus
-
idempotent commutative semiring and defined a semantics based on annotated interpretations and a deductive system.
These two deductive systems are similar in spirit to our completion algorithm for the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
semiring, the main difference being the existence of a generalization rule that deduces
(\alpha,\kappa\oplus\kappa^{\prime})
from
(\alpha,\kappa)
and
(\alpha,\kappa^{\prime})
, while our completion algorithm only computes the monomials that then need to be added to obtain the full
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance, hence avoiding another blowup of the saturated ontology.

9
Conclusions and Future Work

In this paper, we defined a semiring provenance semantics for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontologies, more in line with the classical semiring provenance for relational databases than previous proposals (cf. discussion of the work by ? (?) and
? (?) in Section
8
). After studying its properties in details, we provided algorithms and complexity results for computing the provenance of assertions and conjunctive queries in the case of
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontologies. We also investigated in more details
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-provenance and
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-provenance and discussed connections with notions related to explanations in description logics. Besides
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
, we also considered
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
, a fragment of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
that we introduced and which has the same good computational complexity as DL-Lite and
{\cal E\!L}
.

9.1
Discussion

Note that if we restrict further the language to ontologies whose GCIs have only a concept name on the right-hand side and whose RIs are positive, then, by Theorem
5.8
, for commutative
\oplus
-
idempotent
\omega
-
continuous semirings, the provenance of any BCQ in our framework can be computed using the tools developed for computing the provenance of Datalog queries over databases. This is via the translation of the ontology and query into a Datalog program presented in Theorem
5.8
, and this is applicable, in particular, for the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance and
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
-provenance which have attracted a lot of interest in the Datalog literature.
Datalog provenance has been considered since the seminal work on provenance by ? (?) who gave an algorithm for computing the coefficient of a particular monomial in the provenance series of the query.
? (?) studied the data complexity of deciding whether a monomial is part of the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance for Datalog queries and showed that while the problem is in general intractable (
NP
-complete w.r.t. data complexity), it is tractable for non-recursive Datalog.
? (?) proposed circuit-based provenance representation as an efficient way to compute provenance with absorptive (hence
\oplus
-
idempotent) semirings (
\sf{Sorp}[{\sf X}]
-provenance) as well as
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance.
Several approaches have also been investigated to approximate Datalog provenance. For example, ? (?) proposed to compute a compact representation of the top-k derivation trees, ranking the trees using tree patterns and facts and rules they use.
The implemented approaches either use these kinds of approximations, restrict the language and/or constrain the semiring:
? (?) considered only minimal depth proof trees computed through semi-naïve evaluation;
? (?) used SQL to compute provenance of non-recursive Datalog queries;
? (?) used a hypergraph that represents all derivation steps of the Datalog program to compute the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance either via a system of equations or via a translation to an extension of Datalog with sets;
? (?) translated a Datalog program into a weighted hypergraph and characterized the semirings where the best-weight derivation in the hypergraph corresponds to the provenance for the initial Datalog program, and used this translation to develop a practical approach to compute Datalog provenance in absorptive semirings that are totally ordered;
finally, ? (?) proposed a practical SAT-based approach for computing the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-provenance based on a restricted class of proof-trees.

By Theorem
6.17
(point 3), the provenance of a BCQ w.r.t. a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology can be obtained by computing the saturation of the ontology
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, rewriting the query w.r.t. the saturation, and evaluating the rewritings over the set of annotated assertions in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, seen as a database.
This last step could be performed by using a system for relational provenance management,
such as
GProM (?), which supports
\mathbb{N}[{\sf X}]
-provenance as well as some other types of annotations, or
ProvSQL (?) which supports all provenance semirings
as well as arbitrary user-defined semirings, and has been used by ? (?) to implement a provenance-aware ontology-based data access system (cf. Section
8
).

? (?) proposed a completion algorithm for a fuzzy version of
{\cal E\!L}^{++}
. If we adapted the completion algorithms for
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
and
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
ontologies presented in Section
6.1
to
\mathbb{F}
-annotated ontologies (by using degrees from
[0,1]
instead of monomials and the
\min
operator instead of
\times
), to compute the provenance of all assertions w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}
, we would not need to store in the saturation all elements
n\in[0,1]
generated by the completion rules for a given axiom, but only the
maximal
one (as we did in the case of
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
-annotated ontologies in Section
7.2
). We would thus avoid the exponential blow-up due to the monomials, hence retain polynomial complexity in the case of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
.

9.2
Future Work

For semirings that are not
\otimes
-idempotent, Theorem
5.1
does not hold for GCIs and some concept subsumptions entailed by the ontology may have an unexpected provenance
\mathbb{0}
. It will nevertheless be interesting to investigate the case where the TBox is not annotated, in particular in the case of
\oplus
-
idempotent, absorptive semirings (
\sf{Sorp}[{\sf X}]
provenance) since it covers useful semirings such as the Tropical semiring (costs), the Viterbi semiring (confidence) or the Łukasiewicz semiring (truth values).
Possible directions for future work also include considering other query answering methods for
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontologies, for example based on Datalog rewritings (?).

We could also try to extend the framework in several directions. First, we focussed on
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
but the semantics can be easily defined for other constructors that have been considered in the
{\cal E\!L}
family, such as complex role inclusions (
R_{1}\circ\dots\circ R_{n}\sqsubseteq R
), nominals (
\{a\}
), concrete domains, or concept products (
C\times D\sqsubseteq R
) (?, ?), or for Horn versions of expressive DLs, such as Horn
\mathcal{SHIQ}
for which a completion algorithm has been proposed (?). However, adapting our provenance-aware completion algorithm for such an extension of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
(with suitable restrictions to ensure decidability, cf. ?) will not be trivial. Indeed, we have seen that existing algorithms cannot be straightforwardly adapted to handle provenance annotations (cf. Example
6.2
), and algorithms that allow to deal properly with such constructs are often already complex without annotation (see, e.g., ?, for nominals). Note that even if there exist consequence-based procedures for non Horn DLs (?, ?), extending our semantics to such DLs remains a challenge, since it is not clear how to handle full negation in our framework (cf. Section
3.1.4
).
A recent line of research developed semiring provenance for very general logical languages, such as first-order logic and fixed-point logic (?, ?, ?). In this context, interpretations associate a semiring element to literals (ground facts and their negation) and are extended to more complex formula as expected (in the spirit of the work on
\mathcal{ALC}
by ? (?) discussed in Section
8
). This could be a track to explore, even if this notion–defined for model checking over finite interpretations rather than entailment from possibly infinite models–seems difficult to adapt to our purposes.

Another way to extend the framework would be to consider more expressive query languages.
There have been several attempts at extending semiring provenance for relational databases to more expressive query languages, for example featuring aggregation (?). In particular, non-monotone queries with difference attracted interest: ? (?) proposed to use a monus operator on the underlying semiring, and call m
-
semirings the class of semirings with such an operator, but ? (?) showed limitations of the approach. The case of Boolean provenance (
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
) of non-monotone queries is however well-understood (?).
Similar questions will certainly arise if we try to extend our framework to DLs or queries featuring negation. The recent work by ? (?) that explores three ways of
adding a difference operator to a semiring may provide some leads on these questions.

Proofs for Section
3

Proof.

The proof is made by structural induction. In the base case,
C\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
and
\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)=\exists t_{0}\,C(x,t_{0})
:

\displaystyle\{\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)}\pi(t)\mid\pi\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)),\pi(x)=d\}=\{\pi(t_{0})\mid\pi\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(C(d,t_{0}))\}=\{\kappa\mid(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}.

Induction step.
If
C=C_{1}\sqcap C_{2}
, then
\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)=\boldsymbol{q_{1}}(x)\wedge\boldsymbol{q_{2}}(x)
where
\boldsymbol{q_{1}}(x)
and
\boldsymbol{q_{2}}(x)
are the extended versions of the queries that retrieve the instances of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
respectively.

\displaystyle\{\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)}\pi(t)\mid\pi\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)),\pi(x)=d\}=

\displaystyle\{\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{1}}(x)}\pi_{1}(t)\otimes\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{2}}(x)}\pi_{2}(t)\mid

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q_{1}}

\displaystyle(x)),\pi_{2}\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q_{2}}(x)),\pi_{1}(x)=\pi_{2}(x)=d\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\mid(d,\kappa_{1})\in C_{1}^{\mathcal{I}},(d,\kappa_{2})\in C_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\kappa\mid(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}

If
C=\exists R.C_{1}
, then
\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)=\exists yt_{0}\,R(x,y,t_{0})\wedge\boldsymbol{q_{1}}(y)
where
\boldsymbol{q_{1}}(y)
is the extended version of the query that retrieves the instances of
C_{1}
.

\displaystyle\{\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)}\pi(t)\mid\pi\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)),\pi(x)=d\}=

\displaystyle\{\pi_{R}(t_{0})\otimes\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{1}}(x)}\pi_{1}(t)\mid

\displaystyle\pi_{R}\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(R(x,y,t_{0})),

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q_{1}}(y)),\pi_{R}(x)=d,\pi_{1}(y)=\pi_{R}(y)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\kappa_{R}\otimes\kappa_{1}\mid(d,e,\kappa_{R})\in R^{\mathcal{I}},(e,\kappa_{1})\in C_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\kappa\mid(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}

The case
C=\exists R^{-}.C_{1}
, where
\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(x)=\exists yt_{0}\,R(y,x,t_{0})\wedge\boldsymbol{q_{1}}(y)
, is similar.∎

See
3.11

Proof.

By Lemma
3.10
, for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, it holds that

{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}}({\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(a)})=\{\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(a)}\pi(t)\mid\pi\in\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(a))\}=\{\kappa\mid(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}.

Moreover,
(i) for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}
,
C^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq A_{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
and the annotated interpretation
\mathcal{J}
obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by setting
A_{C}^{\mathcal{J}}=C^{\mathcal{I}}
is also a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}
, so
\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}}\{\chi\mid(a^{\mathcal{I}},\chi)\in A_{C}^{\mathcal{I}}\}=\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}}\{\chi\mid(a^{\mathcal{I}},\chi)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}
, and
(ii) models of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}
are models of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, the interpretation
\mathcal{J}
that extends
\mathcal{I}
by setting
A_{C}^{\mathcal{J}}=C^{\mathcal{I}}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}
so
\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\{\chi\mid(a^{\mathcal{I}},\chi)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}=\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}}\{\chi\mid(a^{\mathcal{I}},\chi)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(A_{C}(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\})=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}\models(A_{C}(a),\kappa)}\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\kappa\in\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}}\{\chi\mid(a^{\mathcal{I}},\chi)\in A_{C}^{\mathcal{I}}\}}\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\kappa\in\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(C\sqsubseteq A_{C},\mathbb{1})\}}\{\chi\mid(a^{\mathcal{I}},\chi)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}}\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\kappa\in\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\{\chi\mid(a^{\mathcal{I}},\chi)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\}}\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\kappa\in\bigcap_{\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}}({\boldsymbol{q_{C}}(a)})}\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathcal{P}(q_{C}(a),\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}).\qed

A.1
Relationship with Semantics for Specific Annotations

The proofs of Propositions
3.12
and
3.15
are based on Proposition
7.4
and Theorem
5.12
.
This is not an issue since Propositions
3.12
and
3.15
are not used to prove any other results in this paper.

See
3.12

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda\rangle
be a satisfiable
\mathbb{F}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology and let
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}=\langle\mathcal{O},\lambda_{\sf X}\rangle
be such that
\lambda_{\sf X}
is an injective function from
\mathcal{O}
to the set of variables
{\sf X}
(assuming that
|\mathcal{O}|\leq|{\sf X}|
). Recall that
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
specializes correctly to every commutative semiring that is
\otimes
-idempotent and absorptive, hence in particular to
\mathbb{F}
. It follows that there exists a unique semiring homomorphism
h
from
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
to
\mathbb{F}
such that
h(\lambda_{\sf X}(\alpha))=\lambda(\alpha)
for every
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}
and
h(x)=0
for every
x\in{\sf X}\setminus\{\lambda_{\sf X}(\alpha)\mid\alpha\in\mathcal{O}\}
, i.e.
\lambda=h\circ\lambda_{\sf X}
.
By Theorem
5.12
, since
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
and
\mathbb{F}
are commutative
\omega
-complete semirings (recall that
{\sf X}
is finite) that are
\oplus
-
idempotent and
\otimes
-idempotent, and
h
is an
\omega
-complete semiring homomorphism from
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
to
\mathbb{F}
:
h(\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}))=\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})
holds if:

1.

\alpha
is a BCQ, an assertion, or an RI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
; or

2.

\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, and
\alpha
is a GCI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
.

In both cases, by Proposition
7.4
(and absorptivity of
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
which implies that considering also non-minimal
\mathcal{M}
in the disjunction does not change the provenance value),

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]})=\bigvee_{\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O},\mathcal{M}\models\alpha}\bigwedge_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)
. Therefore,

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})=h(\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}))=\max\{\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}(\lambda(\beta))\mid\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O},\mathcal{M}\models\alpha\}.

It is then easy to check that
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{F}})\geq n
iff there exists
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
, such that
\mathcal{M}\models\alpha
and all axioms in
\mathcal{M}
are annotated with degrees greater or equal to
n
, i.e. iff
\mathcal{O}_{\geq n}\models\alpha
.
∎

See
3.15

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Proposition
3.12
, but using
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}}
instead of
\mathbb{F}
. Indeed, note that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}}
is also
\otimes
-idempotent and absorptive. Hence, we obtain

\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}}})=h(\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}^{\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]}))=\sup\{\inf_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}(\lambda(\beta))\mid\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O},\mathcal{M}\models\alpha\}
.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for Section
4

B.1
Normal Form

See
4.1

Proof.

For the first point, we show by induction that for every annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
if
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
is obtained from
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
by applying
i
normalization steps, then every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
is also a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

Base case:
i=1
.
Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
be an annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology and assume that
\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
is obtained from
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
by applying a single normalization rule
\mathsf{NF}
. We have four cases.

\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{1}
:

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\{(C\sqcap\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa_{0})\}\cup\{(\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),\,(C\sqcap A\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa_{0})\}
and let
(d,\kappa)\in(C\sqcap\widehat{D})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
There exist
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in K
such that
(d,\kappa_{1})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(d,\kappa_{2})\in\widehat{D}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
.
Since
\mathcal{I}\models(\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq A,\mathbb{1})
, then
(d,\kappa_{2})\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Hence
(d,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in(C\sqcap A)^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}\models(C\sqcap A\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa_{0})
, it follows that

(d,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes\kappa_{0})\in E^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.,
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa_{0})\in E^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Thus
\mathcal{I}\models(C\sqcap\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa_{0})
, and
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{2}
:

This case is analogous to the case
\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{1}
.

\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{3}
:

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of

\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\{(\exists P.\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa_{0})\}\cup\{(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),\,(\exists P.A\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa_{0})\}
and let
(d,\kappa)\in(\exists P.\widehat{C})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
There exist
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in K
such that
(d,e,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(e,\kappa_{2})\in\widehat{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
. Since
\mathcal{I}\models(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1})
, then
(e,\kappa_{2})\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
. Hence
(d,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in(\exists P.A)^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}\models(\exists P.A\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa_{0})
, it follows that
(d,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes\kappa_{0})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa_{0})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
. Thus
\mathcal{I}\models(\exists P.\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa_{0})
, and
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{4}
:

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of

\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\{(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa_{0})\}\cup\{(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),\,(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa_{0})\}
and let
(d,\kappa)\in\widehat{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Since
\mathcal{I}\models(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1})
, then
(d,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
. Since
\mathcal{I}\models(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa_{0})
, it follows that
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa_{0})\in(\exists P)^{\mathcal{I}}
. Thus

\mathcal{I}\models(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa_{0})
, and
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

Induction step.
Assume that the property is true for some
i\geq 1
and let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
be an annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology,
\mathsf{NF}^{i+1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
be the result of applying
i+1
normalization steps to
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
that of applying the first
i
steps to
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
By applying the induction hypothesis on
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
, we obtain that every model of
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
Then, since
\mathsf{NF}^{i+1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
results from applying one normalization step to
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
, we obtain that every model of
\mathsf{NF}^{i+1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
is a model of
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
, and thus also a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

Conversely, we show by induction that for every
i
and for every annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
if
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
can be obtained from
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
by applying
i
normalization rules, then if
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})\models(\alpha,\kappa)
and every concept name occurring in
\alpha
occurs in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, it holds that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
.

Base case:
i=1
.
Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
be an annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology,
\alpha
an axiom such that every concept name occurring in
\alpha
occurs in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\chi\in K
. Assume that
\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
is obtained from
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
by applying a normalization rule
\mathsf{NF}
and that
\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})\models(\alpha,\chi)
. We have four cases.

\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{1}
:

\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\{(C\sqcap\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa_{0})\}\cup\{(\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),\,(C\sqcap A\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa_{0})\}
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathcal{J}
be the interpretation that extends
\mathcal{I}
with
A^{\mathcal{J}}=\widehat{D}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Clearly,
\mathcal{J}\models(\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1})
. Let
(d,\kappa)\in(C\sqcap A)^{\mathcal{J}}
. There exist
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in K
such that

(d,\kappa_{1})\in C^{\mathcal{J}}=C^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(d,\kappa_{2})\in A^{\mathcal{J}}=\widehat{D}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
.
It follows that
(d,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in(C\sqcap\widehat{D})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}\models(C\sqcap\widehat{D}\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa_{0})
, it follows that
(d,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes\kappa_{0})\in E^{\mathcal{I}}=E^{\mathcal{J}}
, i.e.
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa_{0})\in E^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Hence
\mathcal{J}\models(C\sqcap A\sqsubseteq E,\,\kappa_{0})
and
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
.
It follows that
\mathcal{J}\models(\alpha,\chi)
. Since
\alpha
does not contain
A
, then
\mathcal{I}\models(\alpha,\chi)
. Hence
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\chi)
.

\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{2}
:

This case is analogous to the case
\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{1}
.

\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{3}
:

\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\{(\exists P.\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa_{0})\}\cup\{(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),\,(\exists P.A\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa_{0})\}
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathcal{J}
be the interpretation that extends
\mathcal{I}
with
A^{\mathcal{J}}=\widehat{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Clearly,
\mathcal{J}\models(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1})
. Let
(d,\kappa)\in(\exists P.A)^{\mathcal{J}}
. There exist
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in K
such that
(d,e,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{J}}=P^{\mathcal{I}}
,
(e,\kappa_{2})\in A^{\mathcal{J}}=\widehat{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
. It follows that
(d,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in(\exists P.\widehat{C})^{\mathcal{I}}
. Since
\mathcal{I}\models(\exists P.\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa_{0})
, it follows that
(d,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes\kappa_{0})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}=D^{\mathcal{J}}
, i.e.
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa_{0})\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Hence
\mathcal{J}\models(\exists P.A\sqsubseteq D,\,\kappa_{0})
and
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
.
It follows that
\mathcal{J}\models(\alpha,\chi)
. Since
\alpha
does not contain
A
, then
\mathcal{I}\models(\alpha,\chi)
. Hence
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\chi)
.

\mathsf{NF}=\mathsf{NF}_{4}
:

\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\{(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa_{0})\}\cup\{(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1}),\,(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa_{0})\}
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathcal{J}
be the interpretation that extends
\mathcal{I}
with
A^{\mathcal{J}}=\widehat{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Clearly,
\mathcal{J}\models(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq A,\,\mathbb{1})
. Let
(d,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{J}}=\widehat{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Since
\mathcal{I}\models(\widehat{C}\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa_{0})
, it follows that
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa_{0})\in(\exists P)^{\mathcal{I}}=(\exists P)^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Hence
\mathcal{J}\models(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,\,\kappa_{0})
and
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathsf{NF}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
.
It follows that
\mathcal{J}\models(\alpha,\chi)
. Since
\alpha
does not contain
A
, then
\mathcal{I}\models(\alpha,\chi)
. Hence
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\chi)
.

Induction step.
Assume that the property is true for some
i
and let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
be an annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology,
\alpha
an axiom such that every concept name occurring in
\alpha
occurs in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\kappa\in K
.
Let
\mathsf{NF}^{i+1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})

be obtained by applying
i+1
normalization rules to
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})

be obtained by applying the first
i
normalization rules to
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
(so that
\mathsf{NF}^{i+1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
is obtained by applying a normalization rule
\mathsf{NF}
to
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
).
Assume that
\mathsf{NF}^{i+1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})\models(\alpha,\kappa)
.
Since the normalization rules can only introduce new concept names, the concept names occurring in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
are a subset of those occurring in
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
, so every concept name occurring in
\alpha
occurs in
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
.
Since we have that
\mathsf{NF}^{i+1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})\models(\alpha,\kappa)
,
that
\mathsf{NF}^{i+1}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
results from the application of a single normalization rule to
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
, and that all concept names in
\alpha
occur in
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})
,
the base case applies and we obtain that
\mathsf{NF}^{i}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}})\models(\alpha,\kappa)
. Hence by the induction hypothesis,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(\alpha,\kappa)
.
∎

B.2
Canonical Model

We start by proving two lemmas.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},K,\cdot^{\mathcal{I}})
and
\mathcal{J}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}},K,\cdot^{\mathcal{J}})
be
\mathbb{K}
-annotated interpretations such that there is a homomorphism

g:\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
.
For every
\kappa\in K
and every
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept expression
C
,
if
(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
then
(g(d),\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
.

Proof.

The proof is by structural induction. In the base case,

C
is a concept name
A
and the lemma holds by definition of
g
. For
C=\top
the lemma holds trivially. Now, suppose that the lemma holds
for
D,D_{1},D_{2}
. We make a case distinction:

•

C=D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}
: if
(d,\kappa)\in(D_{1}\sqcap D_{2})^{\mathcal{I}}
then, by the semantics of
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
, there exist
(d,\kappa_{1})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}_{1}

and
(d,\kappa_{2})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}_{2}
such that
\kappa=\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}
.
By the inductive hypothesis,
(g(d),\kappa_{1})\in D^{\mathcal{J}}_{1}

and
(g(d),\kappa_{2})\in D^{\mathcal{J}}_{2}
.
Then, by the semantics of
\mathbb{K}
-annotated

{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
, we have that
(g(d),\kappa)\in(D_{1}\sqcap D_{2})^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

C=\exists P.D
(where
P
can be a role name or an inverse role): if
(d,\kappa)\in(\exists P.D)^{\mathcal{I}}
then, by the semantics of
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
,
there exist
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
with
(d,e,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}

and
(e,\kappa_{2})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}

such that
\kappa=\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}
.
By definition of
g
,
we have that
(d,e,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}

implies
(g(d),g(e),\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{J}}

and, by the inductive hypothesis,
(g(e),\kappa_{2})\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Then, by the semantics of
\mathbb{K}
-annotated

{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
, we have that
(g(d),\kappa)\in(\exists P.D)^{\mathcal{J}}
.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},K,\cdot^{\mathcal{I}})
and
\mathcal{J}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}},K,\cdot^{\mathcal{J}})
be
\mathbb{K}
-annotated interpretations such that there is a homomorphism

g:\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
.
For every
\kappa\in K
and every BCQ
q
,
if
\mathcal{I}\models(q,\kappa)
then
\mathcal{J}\models(q,\kappa)
.

Proof.

Assume
\mathcal{I}\models(q,\kappa)
and let
\boldsymbol{q}
be the extended version of
q
. Denote by
\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q})
the set of all matches of
\boldsymbol{q}
in
\mathcal{I}
. By semantics of
(q,\kappa)
, we have that
\mathcal{I}\models(q,\kappa)
iff
there is a match
\pi:\mathsf{terms}(\boldsymbol{q})\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\cup K
in
\nu_{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{q})
such that

\kappa=\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi(t)
.
To prove this lemma, we need to construct a match
\pi^{\prime}:\mathsf{terms}(\boldsymbol{q})\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\cup K
such that

\kappa=\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi^{\prime}(t)
.

For every
t\in\mathsf{terms}(\boldsymbol{q})
, we define
\pi^{\prime}(t):=g(\pi(t))
if
\pi(t)\notin K
and
\pi^{\prime}(t):=\pi(t)
otherwise.
It is clear that
\kappa=\bigotimes_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi^{\prime}(t)
from the definition of
\pi^{\prime}
. We argue that
\pi^{\prime}
is a match of
\boldsymbol{q}
in
\mathcal{J}
.
For every
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
,
\pi(a)=a^{\mathcal{I}}
because
\pi
is a match, and
g(a^{\mathcal{I}})=a^{\mathcal{J}}
because
g
is a homomorphism. Hence
\pi^{\prime}(a)=g(\pi(a))=a^{\mathcal{J}}
for every
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
.

It remains to show that

\pi^{\prime}(\vec{t},t)\in P^{\mathcal{J}}
for every
P(\vec{t},t)\in\mathsf{atoms}(\boldsymbol{q})
.
That is,
(i)
(\pi^{\prime}(t_{1}),\pi^{\prime}(t))\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
for every
A(t_{1},t)\in\mathsf{atoms}(\boldsymbol{q})

and (ii)
(\pi^{\prime}(t_{1}),\pi^{\prime}(t_{2}),\pi^{\prime}(t))\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
for every
R(t_{1},t_{2},t)\in\mathsf{atoms}(\boldsymbol{q})
.
(i) If
A(t_{1},t)\in\mathsf{atoms}(\boldsymbol{q})
then, since
\pi
is a match,

(\pi(t_{1}),\pi(t))\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, where
\pi(t)
is necessarily equal to some
\kappa\in K
by definition of
\mathcal{I}
. Since

(\pi(t_{1}),\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
then, by definition of
g
, we have that
(g(\pi(t_{1})),\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
,
i.e.
(\pi^{\prime}(t_{1}),\pi^{\prime}(t))\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
, by definition of
\pi^{\prime}
.
The proof of (ii) is similar.
∎

See
4.4

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
The proof is by induction on the sequence of interpretations of the canonical model

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
.
We define
g:=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}g_{n}
and show that for every
n
,
g_{n}:\mathcal{I}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
is a homomorphism. Set
g_{0}:\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

with
g_{0}(a)=a^{\mathcal{I}}
for all
a\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}

(recall that
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}={\sf N_{I}}
and
a^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}=a
for all
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
).
By definition of
\mathcal{I}_{0}
,
(a,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
iff
(A(a),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, if
(A(a),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
then

(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
.
So
(a,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}

implies
(g_{0}(a),\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Similarly, if
(a,b,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
then

(R(a,b),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, so

(a^{\mathcal{I}},b^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Then,
(a,b,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
implies
(g_{0}(a),g_{0}(b),\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Thus,
g_{0}:\mathcal{I}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
is a homomorphism.

Suppose it was proven that
g_{n}:\mathcal{I}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
is a
homomorphism.
We want to show that there is a homomorphism

g_{n+1}:\mathcal{I}_{n+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
that extends
g_{n}
.
By definition of the canonical model,
given
\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
the interpretation
\mathcal{I}_{n+1}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}_{n}
by applying the chase rule with some
(\alpha,\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
(\vec{d},\kappa^{\prime})\in E^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
with
E
the left-hand side of
\alpha
, and we are in one of the following cases:

(1)

if
(\alpha,\kappa)=({P\sqsubseteq Q},{\kappa})
and
(\vec{d},\kappa^{\prime})=(d,d^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
, then
Q^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}=Q^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\cup\{(d,d^{\prime},\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\}
;

(2)

if
(\alpha,\kappa)=({C\sqsubseteq A},{\kappa})
, and
(\vec{d},\kappa^{\prime})=(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
, then
A^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\cup\{(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\}
;

(3)

if
(\alpha,\kappa)=({C\sqsubseteq\exists P},{\kappa})
, and
(\vec{d},\kappa^{\prime})=(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
, then
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}:=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\cup\{d_{f}\}
with
d_{f}
a fresh element, and

P^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}=P^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\cup\{(d,d_{f},\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\}
.

In the first two cases, we define
g_{n+1}:=g_{n}
. In the third case, we need to map
d_{f}
to an element of
\mathcal{I}
.
Since
g_{n}:\mathcal{I}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
is a homomorphism, by Lemma
B.1
,

(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
implies that
(g_{n}(d),\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
.
As
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
if
({C\sqsubseteq\exists P},{\kappa})\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
(g_{n}(d),\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
then there is

e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that

(g_{n}(d),e,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
.
We take a fixed but arbitrary such
e

and define
g_{n+1}
in the same way
as
g_{n}
except that
g_{n+1}(d_{f}):=e
.

We now show that in each case
g_{n+1}:\mathcal{I}_{n+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
is a homomorphism.

(1)

The difference between
\mathcal{I}_{n}

and
\mathcal{I}_{n+1}
is that
now we have
(d,e,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in Q^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}
.
By assumption
g_{n}
is a homomorphism,
so
(d,e,\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
implies that

(g_{n}(d),g_{n}(e),\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since in this case
g_{n+1}=g_{n}
,
we also have that

(g_{n+1}(d),g_{n+1}(e),\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
. As
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
if

({P\sqsubseteq Q},{\kappa})\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
then

(g_{n+1}(d),g_{n+1}(e),\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in Q^{\mathcal{I}}
,
which means that
g_{n+1}:\mathcal{I}_{n+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
is a homomorphism, as required.

(2)

In this case, the difference between
\mathcal{I}_{n}

and
\mathcal{I}_{n+1}
is that
now we have
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}
.
By assumption
g_{n}
is a homomorphism,
so (by Lemma
B.1
)
(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
implies that

(g_{n}(d),\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since in this case
g_{n+1}=g_{n}
,
we also have that

(g_{n+1}(d),\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
. As
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
if

({C\sqsubseteq A},{\kappa})\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
then

(g_{n+1}(d),\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
,
which means that
g_{n+1}:\mathcal{I}_{n+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
is a homomorphism, as required.

(3)

Finally, here the difference between
\mathcal{I}_{n}

and
\mathcal{I}_{n+1}
is that

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}:=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\cup\{d_{f}\}

and

(d,d_{f},\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}
,
where
d_{f}
is a fresh element.
In this case
g_{n+1}
is the same as
g_{n}
except for
g_{n+1}(d_{f})
,
which is mapped to an element in
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

such that
(g_{n+1}(d),e,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
(recall that
g_{n+1}(d)=g_{n}(d)
).
So
(d,d_{f},\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}

implies
(g_{n+1}(d),g_{n+1}(d_{f}),\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
, as required.

Since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
there exists a homomorphism
g:\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
.
∎

See
4.5

Proof.

Since annotated BCQs subsume annotated assertions, the two first points are consequences of the last one.
Assume
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(q,\kappa)
. By Proposition
4.3
,

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. So

\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(q,\kappa)
implies

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(q,\kappa)
.
Conversely, assume that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(q,\kappa)
and let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
By Lemma
4.4
,
there exists a homomorphism

g:\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
.
By Lemma
B.2
, we thus have that
\mathcal{I}\models(q,\kappa)
.
It follows that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(q,\kappa)
. ∎

Given an annotation
\kappa\in K
, we define
\mathcal{I}_{C,\kappa,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
in the same way as
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
except that we use
\kappa
instead of
\mathbb{1}
in the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{C}
(cf. paragraph before Theorem
4.7
).

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
with
(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
, where
C
is a
basic
concept.
Then there exists a homomorphism

g:\mathcal{I}_{C,\kappa,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
such that
g(d_{C})=d
.

Proof.

Similar to the case of Lemma
4.4
, the proof is by induction on the sequence of interpretations of the canonical model, which now is

\mathcal{I}_{C,\kappa,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
with
C
a basic concept.
We define
g:=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}g_{n}
and set
g_{0}:\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

with
g_{0}(d_{C})=d
and
g_{0}(a)=a^{\mathcal{I}}
for all
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
.
By definition of
\mathcal{I}_{0}
, we have that
(d_{C},\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
and
by assumption
(d,\kappa)\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since
C
is a basic concept, it is either a concept name
A
or of the form
\exists P
, with
P
a (possibly inverse) role. For
C=A
setting
g_{0}(d_{A})=d
clearly
satisfies the homomorphism property required by this lemma.
For
C=\exists P
, we know that
(d,\kappa)\in(\exists P)^{\mathcal{I}}

holds iff there is
d^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(d,d^{\prime},\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
.
We map
d_{f}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
(see
d_{f}
in the definition of the canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{C}
for a basic concept
C
)
to such
d^{\prime}
, that is,
g_{0}(d_{f})=d^{\prime}
. Then we have that

(d_{C},d_{f},\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
implies
(g_{0}(d_{C}),g_{0}(d_{f}),\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
, which satisfies the homomorphism property required by this lemma. The argument for the assertions is as in Lemma
4.4
:
if
(a,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
, then
(A(a),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
so
(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.
(a,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}

implies
(g_{0}(a),\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, and similarly for role assertions.
We have shown that
g_{0}:\mathcal{I}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
is a homomorphism. The argument for the inductive step is as in Lemma
4.4
.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

If
(d_{C},\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
, where
D
is a basic concept,
then
(d_{C},\kappa^{\prime}\otimes\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\kappa^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
.

Proof.

By construction of
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{I}_{n}
, one can show by induction on
n
such that
(d_{C},\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
that there exists a sequence of GCIs and role inclusions from
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
with annotation
\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{k}
which forms a subset of the axioms used in the
n
chase rule applications that go from
\mathcal{I}_{0}
to
\mathcal{I}_{n}
and is such that
\kappa=\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\kappa_{k}
. This can be shown thanks to
\otimes
-idempotency (for example, if we apply the chase rule with
(A_{1}\sqcap A_{2}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa_{4})
and some
(e,\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{3})\in(A_{1}\sqcap A_{2})^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
that comes from
(e,\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in A_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
and
(e,\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{3})\in A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
, we obtain
(e,\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes\kappa_{3}\otimes\kappa_{4})\in B^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}
because
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{1}
).

Recall that
\mathcal{I}_{C,\kappa^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{n}
is defined in the same way as
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
except that we use
\kappa^{\prime}
instead of
\mathbb{1}
in the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{C}
.
Since
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, and
d_{C}
only occurs in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{0}
in
(d_{C},\kappa^{\prime})
in
C^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{0}}
and in
(d_{C},\mathbb{1})
in
\top^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{0}}
, when applying the same rules starting with
(d_{C},\kappa^{\prime})
in
C^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{0}}
instead of
(d_{C},\mathbb{1})
, one can show that we obtain
\kappa^{\prime}\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\kappa_{k}=\kappa^{\prime}\otimes\kappa
instead of
\kappa
(again, thanks to
\otimes
-idempotency).
∎

See
4.7

Proof.

Assume
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
, where
C,D
are basic concepts (that is, either a concept name
A
or of the form
\exists P
) and
C
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. This means that, for every interpretation

\mathcal{I}
such that
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, if
(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
then
(d,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
.
In particular, this holds for the canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}

of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
C
.
Since
(d_{C},\mathbb{1})\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
and
\kappa\otimes\mathbb{1}=\kappa
we have that

(d_{C},\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
.

Conversely, assume
(d_{C},\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. If
(d,\kappa^{\prime})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
,
then by Lemma
B.3

there exists a homomorphism

g:\mathcal{I}_{C,\kappa^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
such that

g(d_{C})=d
.
By Lemma
B.4
,
(d_{C},\kappa^{\prime}\otimes\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\kappa^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
,
so by Lemma
B.1
,
(d,\kappa^{\prime}\otimes\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
. Hence
\mathcal{I}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
.
Since
\mathcal{I}
was an arbitrary model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
we obtain
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
. ∎

See
4.9

Proof.

Assume
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(P\sqsubseteq Q,\kappa)
. This means that, for every interpretation

\mathcal{I}
such that
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
if
(d,e,\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
then
(d,e,\kappa\otimes\kappa^{\prime})\in Q^{\mathcal{I}}
.
In particular, this holds for the canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{P,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}

of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
P
.
Since
(d_{1},d_{2},\mathbb{1})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{P,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
and
\kappa\otimes\mathbb{1}=\kappa
we have that

(d_{1},d_{2},\kappa)\in Q^{\mathcal{I}_{P,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
.

Conversely, assume
(d_{1},d_{2},\kappa)\in Q^{\mathcal{I}_{P,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
. By construction of
\mathcal{I}_{P,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{I}_{n}
, since
(d_{1},d_{2},\mathbb{1})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
and
d_{1},d_{2}
do not occur anywhere else in
\mathcal{I}_{0}
, one can show by induction on
n
such that
(d_{1},d_{2},\kappa)\in Q^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
that there exists a sequence of role inclusions
(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{1}),\dots,(P_{k-1}\sqsubseteq P_{k},\kappa_{k})
from
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
which forms a subset of the axioms used in the
n
chase rule applications that go from
\mathcal{I}_{0}
to
\mathcal{I}_{n}
and is such that
\kappa=\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\kappa_{k}
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. Since
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{1}),\dots,(P_{k-1}\sqsubseteq P_{k},\kappa_{k})
, one can show that
(d,e,\kappa^{\prime})\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
implies that
(d,e,\kappa^{\prime}\otimes\kappa)\in Q^{\mathcal{I}}
. Hence
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(P\sqsubseteq Q,\kappa)
.

∎

B.3
Reduction Between Assertion and GCI or RI Entailment

We start with two lemmas that will be used to prove the second point of Theorem
4.10
.

Lemma B.5
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative semiring,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
a
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology such that
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side,
B(a_{0})
a concept assertion and
\kappa_{0}\in K
. Let
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
be defined as in the second point of Theorem
4.10
. Assume that
C_{a_{0}}
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
and let
\mathcal{J}:=\mathcal{I}_{C_{a_{0}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}}
be the canonical model of
C_{a_{0}}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{0}
be first interpretation built in the construction of
\mathcal{J}
, i.e.

•

\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}:={\sf N_{I}}\cup\{d_{C_{a_{0}}}\}
;

•

a^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}:=a
,
for all
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
;

•

C_{a_{0}}^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}=\{(d_{C_{a_{0}}},\mathbb{1})\}
;

•

(a,\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
iff

(A(a),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
;

•

(a,b,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
iff

(R(a,b),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

The following properties hold.

1.

For every
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
, for every
b,c\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
,
(b,c,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
implies that for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exists
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
such that
(e,d,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
.

2.

For every
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
D
, for every
b\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
,
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
implies that for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

Proof.

For point (1), assume that
(b,c,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
. By definition of
\mathcal{J}_{0}
,
(R(b,c),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. Hence the following axioms belong to
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
:
(R_{bc}\sqsubseteq R,\kappa)
,
(C_{b}\equiv\exists R_{bc},\mathbb{1})
,
(C_{c}\equiv\exists R_{bc}^{-},\mathbb{1})
. Let
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
. Since
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
, there exists
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
such that
(e,d,\mathbb{1})\in R_{bc}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(e,d,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
and
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

We now show point (2) by structural induction.

•

Base case:
D\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
. Let
b\in{\sf N_{I}}
, and
\kappa\in K
be such that
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
.

–

If
D=\top
,
(b,\kappa)\in\top^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
implies that
\kappa=\mathbb{1}
and for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(e,\mathbb{1})\in\top^{\mathcal{J}}
.

–

If
D\in{\sf N_{C}}
,
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
implies that
(D(b),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, so that
(C_{b}\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
. Hence, since
\mathcal{J}\models\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
, for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
, it holds that
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

Induction step:

–

Let
D=C_{1}\sqcap C_{2}
with
C_{1}
and
C_{2}

{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concepts such that the property holds. Let
b\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
be such that
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
. There exist
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
such that
(b,\kappa_{1})\in C_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
,
(b,\kappa_{2})\in C_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
and
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
. By induction hypothesis, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
(b,\kappa_{i})\in C_{i}^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
implies that for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(e,\kappa_{i})\in C_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}
, so
(e,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in(C_{1}\sqcap C_{2})^{\mathcal{J}}
, i.e.
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

–

Let
D=\exists P.C
with
P
a role name or an inverse role and
C
an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept such that the property holds. Let
b\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
be such that
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
. There exist
c\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
,
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
such that
(b,c,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
,
(c,\kappa_{2})\in C^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
and
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
.
Let
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
First note that
c\in{\sf N_{I}}
. Indeed, it cannot be the case that
c=d_{C_{a_{0}}}
and
(b,c,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
by definition of
\mathcal{J}_{0}
.
Hence, by point (1),
(b,c,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
and
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
implies that there exists
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
such that
(e,d,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{J}}
. Moreover, by induction hypothesis,
(c,\kappa_{2})\in C^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
and
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
implies
(d,\kappa_{2})\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
. It follows that
(e,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in(\exists P.C)^{\mathcal{J}}
, i.e.
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
. ∎

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative semiring,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
a
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology such that
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side,
B(a_{0})
a concept assertion and
\kappa_{0}\in K
. Let
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
be defined as in the second point of Theorem
4.10
. Assume that
C_{a_{0}}
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
and let
\mathcal{J}:=\mathcal{I}_{C_{a_{0}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}}
be the canonical model of
C_{a_{0}}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
The following properties hold.

1.

For every
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
, for every
b,c\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
,
(b,c,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
implies that for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exists
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
such that
(e,d,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
.

2.

For every
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
D
, for every
b\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
,
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
implies that for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

Proof.

We denote by
\mathcal{J}_{0},\mathcal{J}_{1},\dots
the annotated interpretations built in the construction of
\mathcal{J}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{J}_{n}
(cf. Section
4.2
).

We show point (1) by proving by induction on
n
that for every
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
, for every
b,c\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
,
(b,c,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
implies that for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exists
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
such that
(e,d,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

The base case (
n=0
) follows from point (1) of Lemma
B.5
.

•

Assume now that the property is true for some
n\geq 0
.
Let
(b,c,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
.
If
(b,c,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
, we obtain the result by induction hypothesis. Otherwise, the rule applied to obtain
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}
from
\mathcal{J}_{n}
added
(b,c,\kappa)
to
R^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
using some
(P\sqsubseteq R,\kappa_{1})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
(with
P
a role name or an inverse role) such that
(b,c,\kappa_{2})\in P^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
with
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
.
Let
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
. By induction hypothesis, there exists
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
s.t.
(e,d,\kappa_{2})\in P^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Since
\mathcal{J}\models\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
, it follows that
(e,d,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
, i.e.
(e,d,\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
.

We now show point (2) by proving by induction on
n
that for every
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
D
, for every
b\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
,
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
implies that for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

The base case (
n=0
) follows from point (2) of Lemma
B.5
.

•

Assume that for every
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
D
, for every
b\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
,
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
implies that for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.
We prove by structural induction that for every
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
D
, for every
b\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\kappa\in K
,
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
implies that for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

–

Base case:
D\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
. If
D=\top
,
\kappa=\mathbb{1}
and for every
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(e,\mathbb{1})\in\top^{\mathcal{J}}
. If
D\in{\sf N_{C}}
, let
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
. If
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
, we obtain
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
by induction. Otherwise, the rule applied to obtain
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}
from
\mathcal{J}_{n}
added
(b,\kappa)
to
D^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
using some
(E\sqsubseteq D,\kappa_{1})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
(with
E
an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept) such that
(b,\kappa_{2})\in E^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
with
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
. Since
(b,\kappa_{2})\in E^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}
, by induction hypothesis,
(e,\kappa_{2})\in E^{\mathcal{J}}
. Hence, since
\mathcal{J}\models\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
(e,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2})\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
, i.e.
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

–

Induction step:

*

Assume that
D=C_{1}\sqcap C_{2}
with
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that for every
c\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\chi\in K
,
(c,\chi)\in C_{i}^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
implies that for every
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(d,\chi)\in C_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Let
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Since
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
, there exist
(b,\kappa_{1})\in C_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
and
(b,\kappa_{2})\in C_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
such that
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
.
Hence,
(e,\kappa_{1})\in C_{1}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
(e,\kappa_{2})\in C_{2}^{\mathcal{J}}
, so
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

*

Assume that
D=\exists P.C
with
C
such that for every
c\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\chi\in K
,
(c,\chi)\in C^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
implies that for every
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
(d,\chi)\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
, and
let
(e,\mathbb{1})\in C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Since
(b,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
, there must exist
(b,c,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
and
(c,\kappa_{2})\in C^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
such that
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}=\kappa
.
By point (1), there exists
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
such that
(e,d,\kappa_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Since
(d,\mathbb{1})\in C_{c}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
(c,\kappa_{2})\in C^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}
, we get that
(d,\kappa_{2})\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Hence
(e,\kappa)\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
. ∎

See
4.10

Proof.

For the first point we show that
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa_{0})
iff
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}\models(E(a_{0}),\kappa_{0})
. Recall that
C
and
D
are
basic concepts
(concept names or of the form
\exists P
).
First note that if
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is unsatisfiable, so is
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}
, and both annotated ontologies entail every annotated axiom.
In the same way, if
C
is unsatisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa)
for every
\kappa\in K
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}
is unsatisfiable so entails
(E(a_{0}),\kappa)
for every
\kappa\in K
.
Hence, we next focus on the case where
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is satisfiable and
C
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
, so that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}
is satisfiable.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}
. By construction of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}
,
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
. Since
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa_{0})
and
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, it follows that
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa_{0})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa_{0})\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
. Hence, since
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}
,
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa_{0})\in E^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa_{0})\in E^{\mathcal{I}}
.

(
\Leftarrow
) We show the other direction by contrapositive: We assume that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa_{0})
and show that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}\not\models(E(a_{0}),\kappa_{0})
.
Let
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
be the canonical model of
C
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. Recall that
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that its domain element
d_{C}
is such that
(d_{C},\mathbb{1})\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
(cf. Section
4.2
).
Since
\mathbb{K}
is
\otimes
-idempotent and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, by Theorem
4.7
,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\kappa_{0})
implies that
(d_{C},\kappa_{0})\notin D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
. We obtain a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C}
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models(E(a_{0}),\kappa_{0})
as follows:
a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}}=d_{C}
(recall that
a_{0}
does not occur in
\mathcal{O}
),
E^{\mathcal{I}}=D^{\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
and for all other individual names, concept names and role names,
\mathcal{I}
coincides with
\mathcal{I}_{C,\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
.

We now show the second point:
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(B(a_{0}),\kappa_{0})
iff
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa_{0})
.
Again, note that if
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is unsatisfiable, so is
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\bigcup_{a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{C_{a}}
, and both annotated ontologies entail every annotated axiom. Hence, we next focus on the case where
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is satisfiable.

(
\Rightarrow
) Assume that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa_{0})
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{C_{a_{0}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}}
be the canonical model of
C_{a_{0}}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
(note that
C_{a_{0}}
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
, otherwise we would have
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa)
for any
\kappa\in K
). Since
\mathbb{K}
is
\otimes
-idempotent and
\mathcal{O}
(hence also
\mathcal{T}
) does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side, by Theorem
4.7
,
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa_{0})
implies that
(d_{C_{a_{0}}},\kappa_{0})\notin B^{\mathcal{I}_{C_{a_{0}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
.
By Lemma
B.6
, and since
(d_{C_{a_{0}}},\mathbb{1})\in C_{a_{0}}^{\mathcal{I}_{C_{a_{0}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
(by definition of
\mathcal{I}_{C_{a_{0}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}}
),
(d_{C_{a_{0}}},\kappa_{0})\notin B^{\mathcal{I}_{C_{a_{0}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
implies that
(a_{0},\kappa_{0})\notin B^{\mathcal{I}_{C_{a_{0}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
. Since
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
,
\mathcal{I}_{C_{a_{0}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. Hence,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(B(a_{0}),\kappa_{0})
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
For the converse, assume that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(B(a_{0}),\kappa_{0})
.
We show that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa_{0})
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models(B(a_{0}),\kappa_{0})
, i.e.
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa_{0})\notin B^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Let
\mathcal{J}
be the interpretation that extends
\mathcal{I}
with
C_{a}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
for every
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, and
R_{ab}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},b^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
for all
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and every
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
.
Since
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\in C_{a_{0}}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa_{0})\notin B^{\mathcal{J}}
, then
\mathcal{J}\not\models(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa_{0})
.
We show that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
, so that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa_{0})
.
It is clear that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
since interpretations of individuals, concepts and roles that occur in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
are not modified.
We now consider the different kinds of RIs and GCIs in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.

•

Let
(R_{ab}\sqsubseteq R,\kappa)\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
. By construction of
\mathcal{J}
,
R_{ab}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},b^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
and since
(R(a,b),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, then
(a^{\mathcal{I}},b^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
. Thus we have
\mathcal{J}\models(R_{ab}\sqsubseteq R,\kappa)
.

•

Let
(C_{a}\sqsubseteq A,\kappa)\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
with
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
.
Since
(A(a),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
and
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, then
(a^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Thus, since
C_{a}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
, it follows that
\mathcal{J}\models(C_{a}\sqsubseteq A,\kappa)
.

•

Let
(C_{a}\equiv\exists R_{ab},\mathbb{1})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
By construction
C_{a}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
and
R_{ab}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},b^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
so
(\exists R_{ab})^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
. Hence
\mathcal{J}\models(C_{a}\equiv\exists R_{ab},\mathbb{1})
.

•

Let
(C_{b}\equiv\exists R_{ab}^{-},\mathbb{1})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
.
By construction
C_{b}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(b^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
and
R_{ab}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(a^{\mathcal{I}},b^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
so
(\exists R_{ab}^{-})^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(b^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
. Hence
\mathcal{J}\models(C_{b}\sqsubseteq\exists R_{ab}^{-},\mathbb{1})
.

We conclude that
\mathcal{J}\models\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}
, so
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(C_{a_{0}}\sqsubseteq B,\kappa_{0})
.

∎

See
4.11

Proof.

We start with the first point and show that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}{\models}(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{0})
if and only if
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(P_{1}(a_{0},b_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}{\models}(P_{2}(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})
.
First note that if the ontology
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is unsatisfiable, so is
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(P_{1}(a_{0},b_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}
, and both annotated ontologies entail every annotated axiom. Moreover, if
P_{1}
is unsatisfiable w.r.t.
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\models(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa)
for every
\kappa\in K
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(P_{1}(a_{0},b_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}
is unsatisfiable so
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(P_{1}(a_{0},b_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}\models(P_{2}(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa)
for every
\kappa\in K
.
We next focus on the case where
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is satisfiable and
P_{1}
is satisfiable w.r.t.
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
.

(
\Rightarrow
) Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(P_{1}(a_{0},b_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}
. By construction,
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},b_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\in P_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Since
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{0})
and
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, it follows that
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},b_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1}\otimes\kappa_{0})\in P_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},b_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa_{0})\in P_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
We show the other direction by contrapositive: We assume that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{0})
and show that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(P_{1}(a_{0},b_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}\not\models(P_{2}(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})
.
Let
\mathcal{I}_{P_{1},\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
be the canonical model of
P_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. Recall that
\mathcal{I}_{P_{1},\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
such that its domain elements
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are such that
(d_{1},d_{2},\mathbb{1})\in P_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{P_{1},\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
(cf. Section
4.2
).
By Theorem
4.9
,
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{0})
implies that
(d_{1},d_{2},\kappa_{0})\notin P_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{P_{1},\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}}
. We obtain a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\cup\{(P_{1}(a_{0},b_{0}),\mathbb{1})\}
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models(P_{2}(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})
as follows:
a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}}=d_{1}
,
b_{0}^{\mathcal{I}}=d_{2}
(recall that
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
do not occur in
\mathcal{O}
), and for all other individual names, concept names and role names,
\mathcal{I}
coincides with
\mathcal{I}_{P_{1},\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
.

We now show the second point:
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}\models(R(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})
iff
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}\models(S\sqsubseteq R,\kappa_{0})
.
Again, note that if
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is unsatisfiable, so is
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
, and both annotated ontologies entail every annotated axiom. Hence, we next focus on the case where
{\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}}
is satisfiable.

(
\Rightarrow
) Assume that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}\not\models(S\sqsubseteq R,\kappa_{0})
. Let
\mathcal{J}:=\mathcal{I}_{S,\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}}
be the canonical model of
S
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
(note that
S
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
, otherwise we would have
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}\models(S\sqsubseteq R,\kappa)
for any
\kappa\in K
). By Theorem
4.9
,
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}\not\models(S\sqsubseteq R,\kappa_{0})
implies that
(d_{1},d_{2},\kappa_{0})\notin R^{\mathcal{J}}
. Since
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
,
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
. To obtain that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(R(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})
, we show that for every role
P
and
\kappa\in K
,
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{J}},b_{0}^{\mathcal{J}},\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{J}}
implies that
(d_{1},d_{2},\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Indeed, by construction of
\mathcal{J}
,
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{J}},b_{0}^{\mathcal{J}},\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{J}}
means that there exist a role assertion
(R^{\prime}(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{1})
or
(R^{\prime}(b_{0},a_{0}),\kappa_{1})
in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
(since
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}\setminus\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
does not contain any assertion) and a sequence of role inclusions
(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{2}),\dots,(P_{n-1}\sqsubseteq P_{n},\kappa_{n})
in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
such that applying the chase rules corresponding to
(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{2}),\dots,(P_{n-1}\sqsubseteq P_{n},\kappa_{n})
to
(a_{0},b_{0},\kappa_{1})\in R^{\prime\mathcal{J}}_{0}
or
(b_{0},a_{0},\kappa_{1})\in R^{\prime\mathcal{J}}_{0}
respectively leads to
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{J}},b_{0}^{\mathcal{J}},\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Since
(d_{1},d_{2},\mathbb{1})\in S^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}
, and
(R^{\prime}(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{1})\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
implies that
(S\sqsubseteq R^{\prime},\kappa_{1})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
(resp.
(R^{\prime}(b_{0},a_{0}),\kappa_{1})\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
implies that
(S\sqsubseteq R^{\prime-},\kappa_{1})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
), it holds that
(d_{1},d_{2},\kappa_{1})\in R^{\prime\mathcal{J}}
(resp.
(d_{2},d_{1},\kappa_{1})\in R^{\prime\mathcal{J}}
) and applying the chase rules corresponding to
(P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2},\kappa_{2}),\dots,(P_{n-1}\sqsubseteq P_{n},\kappa_{n})
yields
(d_{1},d_{2},\kappa)\in P^{\mathcal{J}}
.
We conclude that
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{J}},b_{0}^{\mathcal{J}},\kappa_{0})\notin R^{\mathcal{J}}
, so that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(R(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Assume that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}\not\models(R(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})
and let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
s.t.
\mathcal{I}\not\models(R(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa_{0})
. Let
\mathcal{J}
the interpretation that extends
\mathcal{I}
with
S^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},b_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathbb{1})\}
. Since
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
(in particular, for every
(S\sqsubseteq R^{\prime},\kappa)\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
,
(R^{\prime}(a_{0},b_{0}),\kappa)\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
so
(a_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},b_{0}^{\mathcal{I}},\kappa)\in R^{\prime\mathcal{J}}
, and similarly for
(S\sqsubseteq R^{\prime-},\kappa)\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}
).
Moreover,
\mathcal{J}\not\models(S\sqsubseteq R,\kappa_{0})
so
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{K}}_{S_{a_{0},b_{0}}}\not\models(S\sqsubseteq R,\kappa_{0})
.

∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Section
5

The following lemma will be useful to prove Theorem
5.1
.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative semiring and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{K}}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology. If either

(i)

\alpha
is an assertion, a BCQ, or an RI, or

(ii)

\alpha
is a GCI between basic concepts,
\mathbb{K}
is
\otimes
-idempotent and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side,

then
if
S^{\mathbb{K}}=\{(\alpha_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(\alpha_{n},\kappa_{n})\}
is a minimal subset of
