(\mathsf{Conj}(B^{0}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{0}_{k_{0}}\sqcap B^{\prime 0}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime 0}_{k_{0}^{\prime}}\sqcap E_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap E_{k})\sqsubseteq A,r_{z}\times p_{0})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
By applying successively
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
, we obtain
(\mathsf{Conj}(\bigsqcap_{j=0}^{k}(B^{j}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{j}_{k_{j}}\sqcap B^{\prime j}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime j}_{k_{j}^{\prime}}))\sqsubseteq A,p)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, i.e.
(\bigsqcap_{j=0}^{k}(B^{j}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{j}_{k_{j}}\sqcap B^{\prime j}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime j}_{k_{j}^{\prime}})\sqsubseteq A,p)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, with
p=r_{z}\times\Pi_{j=0}^{k}p_{j}
.

If we let
K=\Sigma_{j=0}^{k}k_{j}
,
K^{\prime}=\Sigma_{j=1}^{k}k^{\prime}_{j}
, and rename the
P_{i}^{j}
,
A_{i}^{j}
,
B_{i}^{j}
,
{B^{\prime}_{i}}^{j}
and
s^{j}_{\ell}
, we obtain items 1 and 2 of the property. We now show item 3 of the property from (
\dagger
).

In the case where
K\neq 0
:

\displaystyle o^{\prime}=

\displaystyle s\times m_{0}

\displaystyle\times\Pi_{j=0}^{k}\Pi_{\ell=1}^{k_{j}}s^{j}_{\ell}\times\Pi_{j=0}^{k}\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{j}).A^{j}_{i}\sqsubseteq B^{j}_{i}})\times\Pi_{j=0}^{k}\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{j}_{i}}

\displaystyle\times\Pi_{j=0}^{k}p_{j}\times r_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{j=0}^{k}\Pi_{\ell=1}^{k_{j}}s^{j}_{\ell}\times\Pi_{j=0}^{k}\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{j}).A^{j}_{i}\sqsubseteq B^{j}_{i}})\times\Pi_{j=0}^{k}\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{j}_{i}}\times p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{\ell=1}^{K}s_{\ell}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{K}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{K^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p.

In the case
K=0
:

\displaystyle o^{\prime}={}

\displaystyle\Pi_{j=0}^{k}\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{j}_{i}}\times p_{j}\times r_{z}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle\Pi_{j=0}^{k}\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{j}_{i}}\times p

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle\Pi_{i=1}^{K^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p.

This shows the property in case (ii) and finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma D.7
.

For all
i\geq 0
,
x,y\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
,
R
role name or inverse role, and
A\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
, if
(x,y,o)\in R^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
,
(y,o^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
,
(\exists R.A\sqsubseteq B,n)\in\mathcal{O}
, and
y\notin{\sf N_{I}}
has been introduced between
\mathcal{I}_{j-1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{j}
(
j\leq i
), to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
by applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
to some
(x,s)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{j-1}}
,
then

•

there exists
(C\sqcap A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}\sqsubseteq B,r)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
for some
k\geq 0
, such that for every
1\leq\ell\leq k
,
(x,s_{\ell})\in A_{\ell}^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
,

•

and
s\times\Pi_{\ell=1}^{k}s_{\ell}\times r=o\times o^{\prime}\times n
.

Proof.

By Lemma
D.3
, there exists
(S\sqsubseteq R,m_{S\sqsubseteq R})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
such that

s\times m_{0}\times m_{S\sqsubseteq R}=o,

and
by Lemma
D.6
,
the following statements hold.

1.

There exist
k\geq 0
and
k^{\prime}\geq 0
such that:

•

(S\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}})
and
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k
,

•

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
,

•

(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq A,p)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

and
\mathsf{Conj}(C,A_{1},\dots,A_{k})=C\sqcap A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}
(i.e. there is no concept name that occurs more than
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
times in
\{C,A_{1},\dots,A_{k}\}
).

Moreover, if
A=\top
, then
k=k^{\prime}=0
and
p=1
.

2.

For every
1\leq\ell\leq k
, there exists
(x,s_{\ell})\in A_{\ell}^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
.

3.

The monomials are related as follows:

•

If
k\neq 0
,
s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{\ell=1}^{k}s_{\ell}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p=o^{\prime}
.

•

If
k=0
,

\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p=o^{\prime}
.

Hence,
if
(\exists R.A\sqsubseteq B,n)\in\mathcal{O}
, since
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
, it follows by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
that
(\mathsf{Conj}(C\sqcap A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k})\sqsubseteq B,r)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, i.e.
(C\sqcap A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}\sqsubseteq B,r)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
with

r=m_{0}\times n\times m_{S\sqsubseteq R}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p,

so that

\displaystyle s\times\Pi_{\ell=1}^{k}s_{\ell}\times r

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle s\times\Pi_{\ell=1}^{k}s_{\ell}\times m_{0}\times n\times m_{S\sqsubseteq R}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle m_{S\sqsubseteq R}\times s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{\ell=1}^{k}s_{\ell}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p\times n

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle o\times o^{\prime}\times n.\qed

We are now ready to prove our theorem.

See
6.4

Proof.

(1) If
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable,
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
for every
(\alpha,m)
. If
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable, by point (1) of Lemma
D.1
,
\mathcal{I}\models(\alpha,m)
for every model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}
, so
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
. Moreover, if
\alpha=A(a_{\top})
, since
a_{\top}\notin\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, it is easy to check that
\mathcal{O}\models(A(a_{\top}),m)
implies
\mathcal{O}\models(A(c),m)
for every
c\in{\sf N_{I}}
(for example by considering the canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
of
\mathcal{O}
).

(2.a) Assume that
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable and let
\alpha
be an assertion of the form
A(a)
or
R(a,b)
with
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
m
a monomial. We show that if
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
then
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})

by contrapositive:
assuming that
(\alpha,{m})\notin\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, we show that the canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\mathcal{I}_{i}
of
\mathcal{O}
(cf. Figure
3
) is such that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\not\models(\alpha,m)
.
We show by induction that for every
i
, for every assertion
\beta
of the form
S(a,b)
or
B(a)
with
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and every monomial
m
, if
(\beta,{m})\notin\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, then
\mathcal{I}_{i}\not\models(\beta,m)
.

For
i=0
, for every assertion
\beta
,
(\beta,{m})\notin\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
implies
(\beta,{m})\notin\mathcal{O}
, so
\mathcal{I}_{0}\not\models(\beta,m)
by construction of
\mathcal{I}_{0}
.

Assume that the property holds for
i\geq 0
and let
(\beta,m)
be an assertion of the form
S(a,b)
or
B(a)
with
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
such that
(\beta,{m})\notin\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. Assume for a contradiction that
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}\models(\beta,m)
.
Since
\mathcal{I}_{i}\not\models(\beta,m)
by the induction hypothesis, it follows that
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
has been obtained from
\mathcal{I}_{i}
by applying the chase rule in one of the cases
\mathsf{R}_{1}
to
\mathsf{R}_{6}
(since the tuples added by applying the chase rule in cases
\mathsf{R}_{7}
and
\mathsf{R}_{8}
involve at least one domain element
x\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus{\sf N_{I}}
).
We next show that in every case,
(\beta,{m})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

\mathsf{R}_{1}
:
(\beta,m)=(S(a,b),o\times n)
and it holds that
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(R(a,b),o)
and
(R\sqsubseteq S,n)\in\mathcal{O}
.
By induction hypothesis, since
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(R(a,b),o)
, then
(R(a,b),o)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
Hence, it follows from the construction of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{4}
) that
(\beta,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

\mathsf{R}_{2}
:
(\beta,m)=(S(a,b),o\times n)
and it holds that
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(R(b,a),o)
and
(R\sqsubseteq S^{-},n)\in\mathcal{O}
.
By induction hypothesis, since
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(R(b,a),o)
, then
(R(b,a),o)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
Thus, it follows from the construction of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{5}
) that
(\beta,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

\mathsf{R}_{3}
:
(\beta,m)=(B(a),o\times n)
and it holds that
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(A(a),o)
and
(A\sqsubseteq B,n)\in\mathcal{O}
.
By induction hypothesis, since
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(A(a),o)
, then
(A(a),o)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
Hence, it follows from the construction of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
) that
(\beta,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

\mathsf{R}_{4}
:
(\beta,m)=(B(a),o_{1}\times o_{2}\times n)
and it holds that
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(A_{1}(a),o_{1})
,
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(A_{2}(a),o_{2})
and
(A_{1}\sqcap A_{2}\sqsubseteq B,n)\in\mathcal{O}
.
By induction hypothesis, since
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(A_{j}(a),o_{j})
for
1\leq j\leq 2
, then
(A_{j}(a),o_{j})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
Hence, it follows from the construction of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
) that
(\beta,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

\mathsf{R}_{5}
:
(\beta,m)=(B(a),o\times o^{\prime}\times n)
and it holds that
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(R(a,x),o)
,
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(A(x),o^{\prime})
, and
(\exists R.A\sqsubseteq B,n)\in\mathcal{O}
.

If
x\in{\sf N_{I}}
, since
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, it must be the case that
x\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
by construction of
\mathcal{I}_{i}
(since the chase rule never adds two individuals in a role interpretation if they are not already related together in some role interpretation). Hence, by induction hypothesis, since
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(R(a,x),o)
and
\mathcal{I}_{i}\models(A(x),o^{\prime})
, then
(R(a,x),o)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and
(A(x),o^{\prime})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
Hence, it follows from the construction of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{2}
) that
(\beta,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

Otherwise, if
x\notin{\sf N_{I}}
,
x
has been introduced during the construction of
\mathcal{I}_{i}
, let us say between
\mathcal{I}_{j-1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{j}
(with
j\leq i
), to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})
that belongs to
\mathcal{O}
, when applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
(
S
can be a role name or an inverse role).
In this case, there exists a monomial
s
such that
(a,s)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{j-1}}
to which the chase rule has been applied to add
(a,x,s\times m_{0})
to
S^{\mathcal{I}_{j-1}}
, so
(C(a),s)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
by induction. Moreover, by Lemma
D.7
:

–

there exists
(C\sqcap A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}\sqsubseteq B,r)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
for some
k\geq 0
, such that for every
1\leq\ell\leq k
,
(a,s_{\ell})\in A_{\ell}^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
, so by induction
(A_{\ell}(a),s_{\ell})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,

–

and
s\times\Pi_{\ell=1}^{k}s_{\ell}\times r=o\times o^{\prime}\times n=m
.

Hence,
(\beta,{m})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
by
\mathsf{CR}_{1}^{A}
.

•

The case
\mathsf{R}_{6}
is similar to the case
\mathsf{R}_{5}
, using
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{3}
instead of
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{2}
in the case
x\in{\sf N_{I}}
.

We have thus shown that
(\beta,{m})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
regardless the form of the rule applied between
\mathcal{I}_{i}
and
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
, which contradicts our original assumption.
Hence
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}\not\models(\beta,m)
.
We conclude by induction that for every
(\beta,m)
with
\beta
of the form
S(a,b)
or
B(a)
with
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
such that
(\beta,{m})\notin\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
\mathcal{I}_{i}\not\models(\beta,m)
for every
i\geq 0
, so that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\not\models(\beta,m)
.
In particular,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\not\models(\alpha,m)
so
\mathcal{O}\not\models(\alpha,m)
.

(2.b) Assume that
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable and that
\mathcal{O}\models(A(c),m)
with
c\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
. It is easy to check that this implies that
\mathcal{O}\models(A(d),m)
for every
d\in{\sf N_{I}}
, in particular,
\mathcal{O}\models(A(a_{\top}),m)
. Consider
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\cup\{(\mathit{Top}(a_{\top}),1)\}
where
\mathit{Top}
is a fresh concept name. Clearly,
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
entail the same annotated assertions, except for
(\mathit{Top}(a_{\top}),1)
which is entailed by
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
but not by
\mathcal{O}
. In particular,
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\models(A(a_{\top}),m)
. Hence, by point (2.a),
(A(a_{\top}),m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
. Moreover, it is easy to see that
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})=\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})\cup\mathcal{B}_{\top}
where
\mathcal{B}_{\top}
contains only assertions on the fresh element
b_{\top}\notin\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
introduced by the initialization of the completion algorithm for
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
(which is different from
a_{\top}\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
). It follows that
(A(a_{\top}),m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

(3) Assume that
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable. We show that there exists
(\bot(a),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
where
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})\cup\{a_{\top}\}
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
be the set of annotated axioms obtained from
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
as follows: remove all assertions on
a_{\top}
; replace
\bot
by a fresh concept name
\mathit{Bot}
in the assertions and GCIs; remove all negative RIs.
Since
\bot
does not occur in
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is satisfiable, and we have
\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})=\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
.
We can show that
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
is as follows, assuming w.l.o.g. that the same fresh individual
a_{\top}
is used when initializing the saturation algorithm for
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
(note that
\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})=\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})\cup\{\mathit{Bot}\}
and that
(\mathit{Bot}\sqsubseteq\mathit{Bot},1)\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
because
(\bot\sqsubseteq\bot,1)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
).

\displaystyle(\dagger)\ \mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\cup\{(\exists R.\bot\sqsubseteq\bot,1),(\exists R^{-}.\bot\sqsubseteq\bot,1)\mid R\in{\sf N_{R}}\cap\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})\}\cup\{(\top(a_{\top}),1)\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(A(a_{\top}),m)\mid(A(a_{\top}),m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}),A\in{\sf N_{C}}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(\mathit{Bot}(a_{\top}),m)\mid(\bot(a_{\top}),m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})\}.

Indeed, the saturation rules from Table
3
treat
\bot
in the same way as a concept name when it occurs in a GCI that appears as a premise of the rule, and
(\bot(a_{\top}),m)
can be added to
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
only by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{1}
-
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{3}
(and not by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{6}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{7}
) since the completion rules will never add
a_{\top}
in a role assertion.

Let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
be the canonical model of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
(we extend straightforwardly the definition of the canonical model of an annotated ontology to the canonical model of a set of annotated axioms since the unicity of axiom annotation does not play any role for the canonical model).
Since
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable,
\mathcal{O}
does not have any model so
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is not a model of
\mathcal{O}
. Since
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
contains all axioms of
\mathcal{O}
except those with
\bot
as right-hand side, it follows that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
does not satisfy (i) a GCI of
\mathcal{O}
with
\bot
as right-hand side or (ii) a negative RI of
\mathcal{O}
.

•

Case (i):
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
does not satisfy a GCI
(D\sqsubseteq\bot,v)\in\mathcal{O}
, i.e. there exists
(d,m)\in D^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. By construction of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
,
(D\sqsubseteq\mathit{Bot},v)\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
so by construction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
(d,m\times v)\in\mathit{Bot}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

First note that
if
d\notin{\sf N_{I}}
, then

d
has been introduced during the construction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, let us say between
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{j-1}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{j}
, to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(C\sqsubseteq\exists P,m_{0})
that belongs to
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, hence to
\mathcal{O}
(since completion rules do not introduce axioms of this form), when applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
with some
(c,s)\in C^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

It is easy to check by induction that there exist
c_{0}=a\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\notin{\sf N_{I}}
such that
k\geq 0
,
d=c_{k}
and each
c_{i}
has been introduced during the construction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(C_{i}\sqsubseteq\exists P_{i},m_{i})
that belongs to
\mathcal{O}
, when applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
with
(c_{i-1},s_{i-1})\in C_{i-1}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, so that
(c_{i-1},c_{i},s_{i-1}\times m_{i})\in P_{i}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
We show by induction on
k
that
(d,m\times v)\in\mathit{Bot}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
implies that there exists
(\mathit{Bot}(a),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
if
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
(\mathit{Bot}(a_{\top}),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
if
a\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
. By
(\dagger)
and by definition of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, it will follow that
(\bot(a),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
if
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
(\bot(a_{\top}),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
otherwise.

–

Base case:
k=0
, i.e.
d=a\in{\sf N_{I}}
. Since
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\models(\mathit{Bot}(a),m\times v)
, it follows by Theorem
4.5
(straightforwardly extended to use a set of annotated axioms instead of an annotated ontology) that
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\models(\mathit{Bot}(a),m\times v)
.

*

If
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})=\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
, since
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is satisfiable, by point (2.a), we obtain that
(\mathit{Bot}(a),m\times v)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
.

*

Otherwise, if
a\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})={\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
, since
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is satisfiable, by point (2.b), we obtain that
(\mathit{Bot}(a_{\top}),m\times v)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
.

–

Induction step: Assume that the property is true for
k
and consider
(d,m\times v)\in\mathit{Bot}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
such that there exists
c_{0}=a\in{\sf N_{I}}
and
c_{1},\dots,c_{k+1}\notin{\sf N_{I}}
with
d=c_{k+1}
and each
c_{i}
has been introduced during the construction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
to satisfy some
(C_{i}\sqsubseteq\exists P_{i},m_{i})\in\mathcal{O}
, when applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
with
(c_{i-1},s_{i-1})\in C_{i-1}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, so that
(c_{i-1},c_{i},s_{i-1}\times m_{i})\in P_{i}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
Since
(c_{k},d,s_{k}\times m_{k+1})\in P_{k+1}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
,
(d,m\times v)\in\mathit{Bot}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, and
(\exists P_{k+1}.\bot\sqsubseteq\bot,1)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(by initialization of
\mathcal{S}
), so that
(\exists P_{k+1}.\mathit{Bot}\sqsubseteq\mathit{Bot},1)\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, it follows that
(c_{k},s_{k}\times m_{k+1}\times m\times v)\in\mathit{Bot}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
Hence, by induction hypothesis, there exists
(\mathit{Bot}(a),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
if
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
(\mathit{Bot}(a_{\top}),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
otherwise.

•

Case (ii):
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
does not satisfy a RI
(P\sqcap Q\sqsubseteq\bot,v)\in\mathcal{O}
, i.e. there exists
(c,d,m_{1})\in P^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and
(c,d,m_{2})\in Q^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

–

If
c,d\in{\sf N_{I}}
, then by the construction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, it must be the case that
c,d\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
(since the chase rule never adds two individuals in a role interpretation if they are not already related together in some role interpretation). Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\models(P(c,d),m_{1})
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\models(Q(c,d),m_{2})
, then by Theorem
4.5

\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
satisfies
(P(c,d),m_{1})
and
(Q(c,d),m_{2})
, and by point (2.a) of the theorem, since
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is satisfiable,
(P(c,d),m_{1})
and
(Q(c,d),m_{2})
are in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
hence in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
by
(\dagger)
and construction of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.
Hence, since
(P\sqcap Q\sqsubseteq\bot,v)\in\mathcal{O}
, by
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{6}
or
\mathsf{CR}^{A}_{7}
,
(\bot(c),v\times m_{1}\times m_{2})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

–

If
c
or
d
is not in
{\sf N_{I}}
, assume that
d\notin{\sf N_{I}}
has been introduced after
c
in the construction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
(the case where
c
has been introduced after
d
is similar).
Hence
d
has been introduced during the construction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, let us say between
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{j-1}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{j}
, to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})
or
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S^{-},m_{0})
that belongs to
\mathcal{O}
, when applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
respectively.
Let us consider the case
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})
(the case
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S^{-},m_{0})
is similar).
We can show that there exist

*

(c,s)\in C^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
,

*

(S\sqsubseteq P,o_{1})\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
hence in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
such that
s\times m_{0}\times o_{1}=m_{1}
, and

*

(S\sqsubseteq Q,o_{2})\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
hence in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
such that
s\times m_{0}\times o_{2}=m_{2}
.

By
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{0}
, since
(P\sqcap Q\sqsubseteq\bot,v)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, it follows that
(C\sqsubseteq\bot,m_{0}\times o_{1}\times o_{2}\times v)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
Hence
(C\sqsubseteq\mathit{Bot},m_{0}\times o_{1}\times o_{2}\times v)\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
and by the construction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
(c,s\times m_{0}\times o_{1}\times o_{2}\times v)\in\mathit{Bot}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, i.e.
(c,m_{1}\times m_{2}\times v)\in\mathit{Bot}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. We can now use the argument of case (i) to conclude that there exists
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})\cup\{a_{\top}\}
such that
(\bot(a),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
for some
n
.

It follows that in all cases, there exists
(\bot(a),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
where
a\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})\cup\{a_{\top}\}
.

(4)
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
can be computed in exponential time w.r.t. the size of
\mathcal{O}
. Indeed, every annotated axiom
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})\setminus\mathcal{O}
is such that
m
has at most
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
variables
(since they represent sets of variables by definition of
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
),
so the number of possible
m
is exponential in the size of
\mathcal{O}
(more precisely, there are
2^{\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})}
monomials since each monomial is obtained by choosing for each variable whether it is in the product or not), and
\alpha
is of one of the following form:

•

a concept or role assertion built from individual names and predicates that occurs in
\mathcal{O}
(plus
a_{\top}
,
\top
,
\bot
), and there are polynomially many such assertions,

•

a RI
P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2}
where
P_{1},P_{2}
or their inverses occur in
\mathcal{O}
, and there are polynomially many such RIs,

•

a negative RI
P_{1}\sqcap P_{2}\sqsubseteq\bot
or a GCI of the form
\exists P.\bot\sqsubseteq\bot
where
P_{1},P_{2}
or their inverses occur in
\mathcal{O}
, and there are polynomially many such axioms,

•

a GCI
A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}\sqsubseteq B
, where
A_{1},\dots,A_{k}
are concept names that occur in
\mathcal{O}
(or
k=1
and
A_{1}=\top
) and
B
is a concept name that occurs in
\mathcal{O}
or
\bot
. Moreover, each concept name can occurs at most
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
times in
A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}
(since conjunctions are treated as multisets with maximal multiplicity
\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
). Hence the number of possible left-hand sides
A_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap A_{k}
is bounded by
K^{N}
where
K
is the biggest possible
k
and
N
is the number of concept names that occur in
\mathcal{O}
plus 1, since each such left-hand side can be obtained by choosing for each
1\leq i\leq K
one concept name or
\top
.
The number of such GCIs is thus bounded by
N*K^{N}
(since there are
N
possibilities for the right-hand side
B
).
Since
N\leq|\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})|+1\leq|\mathcal{O}|
and
K\leq|\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})|*\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})\leq|\mathcal{O}|^{2}
, then
N*K^{N}\leq|\mathcal{O}|^{2|\mathcal{O}|+1}
.

Hence, in the worst case,
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
adds to
\mathcal{O}

O(|\mathcal{O}|^{2|\mathcal{O}|+1})
axioms where each axiom may be annotated with
2^{\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})}\leq 2^{|\mathcal{O}|}
monomials. The total number of annotated axioms added is thus at most exponential in the size of
\mathcal{O}
(there are
O(exp(|\mathcal{O}|^{2}))
possible annotated axioms).
Since each rule application adds an annotated axiom, it follows that the number of rule applications is exponential in
|\mathcal{O}|
. Moreover, each rule application can be done in polynomial time w.r.t. the number of axioms already added, hence in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.
It follows that the algorithm runs in exponential time.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.11

Recall that
\mathcal{O}
belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
if
\mathcal{O}
is an
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
ontology in normal form such that if
C\sqsubseteq\exists P_{1}\in\mathcal{O}
,
P_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{O}}P_{2}
, and
\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{2}).A\sqsubseteq B\in\mathcal{O}
, then
A=\top
.
In this case, we modify the completion algorithm by restricting the rules from Table
3
that may introduce exponentially many axioms, and add two additional rules
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{5}
(see Figure
2
).
We show the following lemmas, which are the counterparts of Lemmas
D.6
and
D.7
for the
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
case.
We consider a
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-annotated
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
ontology
\mathcal{O}
, its canonical model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\mathcal{I}_{i}
(cf. Figure
3
) and the set
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
obtained from
\mathcal{O}
using the modified completion rules of Figure
2
.

Lemma D.8
.

For all
i\geq 0
,
x,y\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
and
A\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
if
(y,o^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
and
y\notin{\sf N_{I}}
has been introduced between
\mathcal{I}_{j-1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{j}
(
j\leq i
) to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
by applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
to some
(x,s)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{j-1}}
,
then the following holds.

1.

There exist
k\geq 0
and
k^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
and:

•

(S\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}})
and
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k
,

•

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
,

•

(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq A,p)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(note that the conjunction contains at most two concept names since
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
).

Moreover, if
A=\top
, then
k=k^{\prime}=0
and
p=1
(recall that the empty conjunction is
\top
and
(\top\sqsubseteq\top,1)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
).

2.

The monomials are related as follows:

•

If
k\neq 0
,
s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p=o^{\prime}
.

•

If
k=0
,

\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p=o^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
l=i-j
. In the case where
A=\top
, the property trivially holds with
k=k^{\prime}=0
for every
l\geq 0
.

Base case.

In the case where
A\neq\top
, the base case is
l=1
and is exactly as in the proof of Lemma
D.6
.

Induction step.

Assume that the property is true for every integer up to
l
and consider the case where
i-j=l+1
.
Let
x,y\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
, and assume that
(y,o^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
, and
y\notin{\sf N_{I}}
has been introduced between
\mathcal{I}_{j-1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{j}
, to satisfy
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
by applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
to
(x,s)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{j-1}}
(thus adding
(x,y,s\times m_{0})\in S^{\mathcal{I}_{j}}
).
We make a case analysis on the last chase rule applied to add
(y,o^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
.

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}_{3}}
:
\boldsymbol{(y,o^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}}
added by applying the chase rule with

\boldsymbol{(D\sqsubseteq A,m_{D\sqsubseteq A})\in\mathcal{O}}.

There are
(y,o)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
and
o^{\prime}=m_{D\sqsubseteq A}\times o
.
Since
(y,o)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
, by induction hypothesis:

•

There exist
k\geq 0
and
k^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
and:

–

(S\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}})
,
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k
,

–

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
,

–

(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k_{j}}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq D,p^{\prime})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

The monomials are related as follows

–

If
k\neq 0
,
s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p^{\prime}=o
.

–

If
k=0
,
\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p^{\prime}=o
.

It follows that:

•

If
k\geq 1
:
since
(S\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, then by Lemma
D.2
, we have that
(\mathsf{inv}(S)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}),m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
Thus, since
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})
,
(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}})
, and
(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq D,p^{\prime})
are in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(recall that
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
), then by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
,
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D,m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
where
m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D}=\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p^{\prime}
.

•

If
k=0
: since
(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}})
,
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
, and
(B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k_{j}^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq D,p^{\prime})
are in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(recall that
k^{\prime}\leq 2
),
then by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{5}
,
(\top\sqsubseteq D,m_{\top\sqsubseteq D})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
where
m_{\top\sqsubseteq D}=\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p
.

This shows item 1 of the property: by taking
K=1
and
K^{\prime}=0
if
k\geq 1
, and
K=0
,
K^{\prime}=1
if
k=0
, it holds that:

•

(S\sqsubseteq S,1)
and
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D,m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq K
, (take
P_{i}=S
,
m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}=1
, and
B_{i}=D
)

•

(\top\sqsubseteq D,m_{\top\sqsubseteq D})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq K^{\prime}
, (take
B^{\prime}_{i}=D
)

•

(D\sqsubseteq A,p)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
with
p=m_{D\sqsubseteq A}
.

To show item 2 of the property, we observe that if
K\neq 0
(i.e.
k\neq 0
), then

\displaystyle o^{\prime}=

\displaystyle m_{D\sqsubseteq A}\times o=p\times o

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\times s\times m_{0}\times(\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\times m_{0}\times p\times\Pi_{i=1}^{K}(1\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{K^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq D},

and if
K=0
(i.e.
k=0
and
K^{\prime}=1
)

\displaystyle o^{\prime}=m_{D\sqsubseteq A}\times o=p\times o=p\times(\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p^{\prime})=p\times\Pi_{i=1}^{K^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq D}.

This shows the property in this case.

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}_{4}}
:
\boldsymbol{(y,o^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}}
added by applying the chase rule with

\boldsymbol{(D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}\sqsubseteq A,m_{D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}\sqsubseteq A})\in\mathcal{O}}.

There are
(y,o_{1})\in D_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
and
(y,o_{2})\in D_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
and
o^{\prime}=m_{D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}\sqsubseteq A}\times o_{1}\times o_{2}
.
For
j\in\{1,2\}
, since
(y,o_{j})\in D_{j}^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
, by induction hypothesis:

•

There exist
k_{j}\geq 0
and
k_{j}^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
k_{j}+k^{\prime}_{j}\leq 2
and:

–

(S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i},m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i}})
,
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{j}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{j}_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{j}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{j}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k_{j}
,

–

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k_{j}^{\prime}
,

–

(B^{j}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{j}_{k_{j}}\sqcap B^{\prime j}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime j}_{k_{j}^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq D_{j},p_{j})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

The monomials are related as follows

–

If
k_{j}\neq 0
,
s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{j}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{j}_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i}}\times p_{j}=o_{j}
.

–

If
k_{j}=0
,
\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i}}\times p_{j}=o_{j}
.

It follows that for
j\in\{1,2\}
:

•

If
k_{j}\geq 1
:
since
(S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i},m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, then by Lemma
D.2
, we have that
(\mathsf{inv}(S)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P^{j}_{i}),m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.
Since
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{j}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{j}_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{j}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{j}_{i}})
,
(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i}})
, and
(B^{j}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{j}_{k_{j}}\sqcap B^{\prime j}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime j}_{k_{j}^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq D_{j},p_{j})
are in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(recall that
k_{j}+k^{\prime}_{j}\leq 2
), then by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
,
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D_{j},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D_{j}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
where
m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D_{j}}=\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{j}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{j}_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}_{j}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i}}\times p_{j}
.

•

If
k_{j}=0
: since
(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i}})
,
1\leq i\leq k_{j}^{\prime}
, and
(B^{\prime j}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime j}_{k_{j}^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq D_{j},p_{j})
are in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(recall that
k^{\prime}_{j}\leq 2
),
then by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{5}
,
(\top\sqsubseteq D_{j},m_{\top\sqsubseteq D_{j}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
where
m_{\top\sqsubseteq D_{j}}=\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}_{j}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i}}\times p_{j}
.

Let
K=0
if
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
,
K=2
if
k_{1}\neq 0
and
k_{2}\neq 0
, and
K=1
otherwise,
and let
K^{\prime}=2-K
. Note that
K,K^{\prime}\geq 0
and
K+K^{\prime}\leq 2
.

Rename
D_{1}
by
D^{\prime}_{1}
if
k_{1}=0
, and if
D_{2}\neq\top
, rename
D_{2}
by:
D_{1}
if
k_{1}=0
and
k_{2}\neq 0
,
D^{\prime}_{1}
if
k_{1}\neq 0
and
k_{2}=0
, and
D^{\prime}_{2}
if
k_{1}=0
and
k_{2}=0
. This shows item 1 of the property:

•

(S\sqsubseteq S,1)
and
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq K
, (take
P_{i}=S
,
m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}=1
, and
B_{i}=D_{i}
)

•

(\top\sqsubseteq D^{\prime}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq D^{\prime}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq K^{\prime}
, (take
B^{\prime}_{i}=D^{\prime}_{i}
)

•

(D_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap D_{K}\sqcap D^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap D^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq A,p)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
where
D_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap D_{K}\sqcap D^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap D^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}=D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}
and
p=m_{D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}\sqsubseteq A}
.

To show item 2 of the property, we observe that if
K\neq 0
(i.e.
k_{1}\neq 0
or
k_{2}\neq\emptyset
), then

\displaystyle o^{\prime}={}

\displaystyle m_{D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}\sqsubseteq A}\times o_{1}\times o_{2}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle p\times o_{1}\times o_{2}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle p\times s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{j=1}^{2}(\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{j}_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{j}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{j}_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i}}\times p_{j})

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle s\times m_{0}\times p\times\Pi_{i=1}^{K}(1\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(S).\top\sqsubseteq D_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{K^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq D^{\prime}_{i}},

and if
K=0
(i.e.
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
and
K^{\prime}=2
)

\displaystyle o^{\prime}=m_{D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}\sqsubseteq A}\times o_{1}\times o_{2}=p\times o_{1}\times o_{2}=p\times\Pi_{j=1}^{2}(\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{j}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime j}_{i}}\times p_{j})=p\times\Pi_{i=1}^{K^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq D^{\prime}_{i}}.

This shows the property in this case.

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}_{5}}
or
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}_{6}}
:
\boldsymbol{(y,o^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}}
added by applying the chase rule with

\boldsymbol{(\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A,m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A})\in\mathcal{O}}.

There exist
(y,z,o_{yz})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
and
(z,o_{z})\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
such that
o^{\prime}=o_{yz}\times o_{z}\times m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A}
. We distinguish two subcases:

(i)

either
z=x
,

(ii)

or
z\neq x
, which implies that
z
has been introduced between
j^{\prime}-1
and
j^{\prime}
(
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq i
) to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(E_{0}\sqsubseteq\exists S_{z},m_{z})\in\mathcal{O}
by applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
.

We start with case (i). It holds that

(y,x,o_{yx})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
,
(x,o_{x})\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
and
o^{\prime}=o_{yx}\times o_{x}\times m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A}
.
Since
(y,x,o_{yx})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
, i.e.
(x,y,o_{yx})\in\mathsf{inv}(P)^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
,
(y,1)\in\top^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
and
y
has been introduced to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
by applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
to
(x,s)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{j-1}}
, by Lemma
D.3
(whose proof only uses the completion rule
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{1}
which is not modified for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
, cf. Figure
2
), there exists
(S\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P),m_{S\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P)})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
such that
s\times m_{0}\times m_{S\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P)}=o_{yx}
.

By Lemma
D.2
,
(S\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P),m_{S\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P)})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
implies
S\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{O}}\mathsf{inv}(P)
.
Since
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
,
S\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{O}}\mathsf{inv}(P)
and
(\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A,m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, it follows that
D=\top
: Indeed, if
(\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A,m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A})
has been added by the completion algorithm, i.e. by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
,
D=\top
by the definition of
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
, and otherwise,
(\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A,m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A})\in\mathcal{O}
so since
\mathcal{O}
belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
, it must be the case that
D=\top
.
Since
D=\top
and
(x,o_{x})\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
, it must be the case that
o_{x}=1
.
This shows the property in case (i), indeed:

1.

If we take
k=1
and
k^{\prime}=0
, it holds that:

•

(S\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P),m_{S\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P)})
and
(\exists P.\top\sqsubseteq A,m_{\exists P.\top\sqsubseteq A})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(take
P_{1}=\mathsf{inv}(P)
,
B_{1}=A
),

•

(A\sqsubseteq A,1)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(take
B_{1}=A
and
p=1
).

2.

o^{\prime}=o_{yx}\times o_{x}\times m_{\exists P.\top\sqsubseteq A}=o_{yx}\times 1\times m_{\exists P.\top\sqsubseteq A}=s\times m_{0}\times m_{S\sqsubseteq\mathsf{inv}(P)}\times m_{\exists P.\top\sqsubseteq A}\times p.

We now consider case (ii):
(y,z,o_{yz})\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
,
(z,o_{z})\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
,
o^{\prime}=o_{yz}\times o_{z}\times m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A}
and
z
has been introduced between
j^{\prime}-1
and
j^{\prime}
(
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq i
) by applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(E_{0}\sqsubseteq\exists S_{z},m_{z})\in\mathcal{O}
.
There must exist
(y,s_{0})\in E_{0}^{\mathcal{I}_{j^{\prime}-1}}
to which the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
has been applied. Hence by Lemma
D.3
there exists
(S_{z}\sqsubseteq P,m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
such that
s_{0}\times m_{z}\times m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P}=o_{yz}
, and
by induction hypothesis, the following statements hold.

•

There exist
k\geq 0
and
k^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
and:

–

(S_{z}\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P_{i}})
and
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k
,

–

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
,

–

(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq D,p)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

Since
(\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A,m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A})\in\mathcal{O}
and
(E_{0}\sqsubseteq\exists S_{z},m_{z})\in\mathcal{O}
, it follows by the modified version of
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
(cf. Figure
2
) that
(E_{0}\sqsubseteq A,r_{z})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})

with

r_{z}=m_{z}\times m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A}\times m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p.

•

The monomials are related as follows

–

If
k\neq 0
,
s_{0}\times m_{z}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p=o_{z}
.

–

If
k=0
,
\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p=o_{z}
.

Moreover since
(y,s_{0})\in E_{0}^{\mathcal{I}_{i-1}}
, by induction hypothesis:

•

There exist
k_{0}\geq 0
and
k_{0}^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
k_{0}+k_{0}^{\prime}\leq 2
and:

–

(S\sqsubseteq P^{0}_{i},m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{0}_{i}})
,
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{0}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{0}_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{0}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{0}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k_{0}
,

–

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime 0}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime 0}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k_{0}^{\prime}
,

–

(B^{0}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{0}_{k_{0}}\sqcap B^{\prime 0}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime 0}_{k_{0}^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq E_{0},p_{0})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

The monomials are related as follows

–

If
k_{0}\neq 0
,
s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{0}}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{0}_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{0}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{0}_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{0}_{i}}\times p_{0}=s_{0}
.

–

If
k_{0}=0
,
\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{0}_{i}}\times p_{0}=s_{0}
.

Since
(B^{0}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{0}_{k_{0}}\sqcap B^{\prime 0}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime 0}_{k_{0}^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq E_{0},p_{0})
(with
k_{0}+k_{0}^{\prime}\leq 2
) and
(E_{0}\sqsubseteq A,r_{z})
are in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
(cf. Figure
2
),

(B^{0}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{0}_{k_{0}}\sqcap B^{\prime 0}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime 0}_{k_{0}^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq A,p_{0}\times r_{z})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and this shows item 1 of the property.
To get item 2 of the property, observe that

\displaystyle o^{\prime}={}

\displaystyle o_{yz}\times o_{z}\times m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle s_{0}\times m_{z}\times m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p\times m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A}

so that if
k_{0}\neq 0
, then

\displaystyle o^{\prime}={}

\displaystyle s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{0}}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{0}_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{0}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{0}_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{0}_{i}}\times p_{0}

\displaystyle\times m_{z}\times m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p\times m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{0}}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P^{0}_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}^{0}).\top\sqsubseteq B^{0}_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{0}_{i}}\times p_{0}\times r_{z}

and if
k_{0}=0
, then

\displaystyle o^{\prime}={}

\displaystyle\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{0}_{i}}\times p_{0}

\displaystyle\times m_{z}\times m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S_{z}\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p\times m_{\exists P.D\sqsubseteq A}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle\Pi_{i=1}^{k_{0}^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{0}_{i}}\times p_{0}\times r_{z}

This shows the property in case (ii) and finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma D.9
.

For all
i\geq 0
,
x,y\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
,
R
role name or inverse role, and
A\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
, if
(x,y,o)\in R^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
,
(y,o^{\prime})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
,
(\exists R.A\sqsubseteq B,n)\in\mathcal{O}
, and
y\notin{\sf N_{I}}
has been introduced between
\mathcal{I}_{j-1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{j}
(
j\leq i
), to satisfy an inclusion of the form
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
by applying the chase rule in case
\mathsf{R}_{7}
or
\mathsf{R}_{8}
to some
(x,s)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{j-1}}
,
then

•

there exists
(C\sqsubseteq B,r)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,

•

and
s\times r=o\times o^{\prime}\times n
.

Proof.

By Lemma
D.3
(whose proof only uses the completion rule
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{1}
which is not modified for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
, cf. Figure
2
), there exists
(S\sqsubseteq R,m_{S\sqsubseteq R})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
such that

s\times m_{0}\times m_{S\sqsubseteq R}=o,

and
by Lemma
D.8
,
the following statements hold.

1.

There exist
k\geq 0
and
k^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
and:

•

(S\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}})
and
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k
,

•

(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i},m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
,

•

(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{k}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq A,p)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(note that the conjunction contains at most two concept names since
k+k^{\prime}\leq 2
).

Moreover, if
A=\top
, then
k=k^{\prime}=0
and
p=1
.

2.

The monomials are related as follows:

•

If
k\neq 0
,
s\times m_{0}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p=o^{\prime}
.

•

If
k=0
,

\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p=o^{\prime}
.

Hence, if
(\exists R.A\sqsubseteq B,n)\in\mathcal{O}
, since
(C\sqsubseteq\exists S,m_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
, it follows by the modified version of
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
(cf. Figure
2
) that
(C\sqsubseteq B,r)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
with

r=m_{0}\times n\times m_{S\sqsubseteq R}\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(m_{S\sqsubseteq P_{i}}\times m_{\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).\top\sqsubseteq B_{i}})\times\Pi_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}m_{\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}_{i}}\times p,

so that
s\times r=o\times o^{\prime}\times n
.
∎

Recall that
\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
is the
k
-saturation of
\mathcal{O}
, i.e. the set of annotated axioms obtained from
\mathcal{O}
through the completion algorithm restricted to monomials of size at most
k
.

See
6.11

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
6.4
so we focus here on what differs.

(1) To show that every annotated assertion
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
is entailed by
\mathcal{O}
, since the modified version of
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
are special cases of the original
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
, we only need to show that adding
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{5}
preserves the soundness of the completion algorithm. This is easy to check (similarly as we do in the proof of Lemma
D.1
).

(2.a) To show that if
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable and
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
with
\alpha
an assertion of the form
A(a)
or
R(a,b)
with
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
|m|\leq k
, then
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
, we show (i) that if
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable and
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
, then
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
obtained with the modified rules and (ii) that if
|m|\leq k
and
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, then
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
.

For point (i), we can use exactly the same proof as for point (2.a) of Theorem
6.4
except that we use Lemma
D.9
instead of Lemma
D.7
(note that
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
are not used in the proof for point (2) of Theorem
6.4
, except for proving Lemma
D.7
, so that our modifications do not affect the rest of the proof).

Point (ii) follows from the fact that every annotated axiom added by a rule application has at least as many variables as the premises of the rule.

(2.b) The proof of point (2.b) is exactly the same as in the proof of Theorem
6.4
.

(3) Every annotated axiom
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})\setminus\mathcal{O}
is such that
m
has at most
k
variables, so the number of possible
m
is exponential in
k
and polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
(more precisely, bounded by
|\mathcal{O}|^{k}
), and
\alpha
is of one of the following form:

•

a concept or role assertion built from individual names and predicates that occurs in
\mathcal{O}
(plus
a_{\top}
,
\top
,
\bot
), and there are polynomially many such assertions,

•

a RI
P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2}
where
P_{1},P_{2}
or their inverses occur in
\mathcal{O}
, and there are polynomially many such RIs,

•

a negative RI
P_{1}\sqcap P_{2}\sqsubseteq\bot
or a GCI of the form
\exists P.\bot\sqsubseteq\bot
where
P_{1},P_{2}
or their inverses occur in
\mathcal{O}
, and there are polynomially many such axioms,

•

a GCI of the form
B\sqsubseteq D
or
B_{1}\sqcap B_{2}\sqsubseteq D
with
B_{(i)}\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\top\}
and
D\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\bot\}
occurs in
\mathcal{O}
(thanks to the modifications done to
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{2}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{3}
), and there are polynomially many such GCIs, or

•

a GCI of the form
\exists P.\top\sqsubseteq B
(introduced by
\mathsf{CR}^{T}_{4}
) where
P
is a role name or inverse role and
B\in{\sf N_{C}}\cup\{\bot\}
, and there are polynomially many such GCIs.

Hence, in the worst case,
\mathsf{saturate}^{k}(\mathcal{O})
adds to
\mathcal{O}
a polynomial number of axioms where each axiom may be annotated with
|\mathcal{O}|^{k}
monomials: the total number of annotated axiom is thus polynomial in the size of
\mathcal{O}
and exponential in
k
.
∎

See
6.12

Proof.

We show that the algorithm can be modified by ignoring the monomials in the annotated axioms to check in polynomial time whether
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable. It follows that we can decide whether
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
by first checking that
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable in polynomial time, then, by Theorem
6.11
, computing
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
in polynomial time w.r.t. the size of
\mathcal{O}
and exponential time w.r.t.
|m|
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
be the result of applying the completion algorithm using the rules modified by Figure
2
while ignoring the monomial part of the annotated axioms.
It is easy to check that
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\{\alpha\mid(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})\}
.
Moreover, it follows from the proof of point (3) of Theorem
6.11
that the algorithm adds a polynomial number of axioms, hence it terminates in polynomial time.
Finally, we can show as in the proof of point (3) of Theorem
6.4
that
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
contains some
\bot(a)
iff
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable.
∎

D.2
Conjunctive Queries over
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
Ontologies

1
5
5

5

ALGORITHM 3

Lemma D.10
.

Algorithm
1
computes
\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
.

Proof.

Let
(q^{*},m)\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
. There exists a rewriting sequence
(q_{0},m_{0})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{1},m_{1})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}\dots\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{k},m_{k})
where
(q,1)=(q_{0},m_{0})
and
(q^{*},m)=(q_{k},m_{k})
. We show by induction that
(q_{i},m_{i})
is produced by Algorithm
1
for every
0\leq i\leq k
.

Base case,
i=0
.
Since Algorithm
1
initializes
Result
with
\{(q,1)\}
and only adds elements to
Result
, it holds that Algorithm
1
produces
(q_{0},m_{0})
.

Induction step.
Assume that Algorithm
1
produces
(q_{i},m_{i})
and that
(q_{i},m_{i})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
, i.e.
(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
is obtained from
(q_{i},m_{i})
by applying steps (S1) to (S6) of Definition
6.16
.
After Algorithm
1
adds
(q_{i},m_{i})
to
Result
,
Rew\neq Result
so the while-loop is entered. Then since
(q_{i},m_{i})\in Result
, it is considered in the foreach-loop in line
1
of Algorithm
1
.

•

Let
x_{0}
be the existentially quantified variable of
q_{i}
selected in step (S1):
x_{0}
fulfills the conditions to be
considered in the foreach-loop in line
1
.

•

Step (S2) corresponds to lines
1
and
1
.

•

Let
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)\in\mathcal{O}
be the GCI selected by step (S3):
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)
is considered in the foreach-loop in line
1
. We show that
At(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
and
mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
constructed in step (S3) are such that there are
mon_{r}\in\mathsf{RoleAtRew}(q^{*}_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
and
(At,mon_{c})\in\mathsf{ConceptAtRew}(q^{*}_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
s.t.
At=At(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\setminus\{A\}
and
v\times mon_{r}\times mon_{c}=mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
.
This follows from the fact that :

1.

Algorithm
2
returns the set of all monomials that can be obtained as a product of the form
\prod_{Q(y,x_{0})\in q^{*}_{i}}m_{Q}
where for each
Q(y,x_{0})\in q^{*}_{i}
,
m_{Q}\in MonSetQ
and
MonSetQ=\{m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}\mid(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})\}
. In particular, it contains the product
mon_{r}
of the
m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}
selected by step (S3)(a).

2.

Algorithm
3
returns the set of all pairs
(At,mon_{c})
that can be obtained as the union and product of pairs of sets of concept names
At_{C}
and monomials
n_{C}\times m_{C}
for all
C(x_{0})\in q^{*}_{i}
, such that there is

(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{p}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and for every
1\leq i\leq p
either there is a pair
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i},v_{i})\in\mathcal{O}
and
(P\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
or there is
(\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},o_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
such that
At_{C}
is the set of the
A_{i}
and
m_{C}
is the product of the
v_{i}\times m_{i}
or
o_{i}
.
In particular, some
(At,mon_{c})
corresponds to the GCIs and RIs selected by step (S3)(b).

•

Finally, step (S4) corresponds to line
1
, step (S5) corresponds to lines
1
and
1
and step (S6) corresponds to line
1
.

Hence,
(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
is in the set
Result
returned by Algorithm
1
.

In the other direction, assume that
(q^{*},m)
is in the set
Result
returned by Algorithm
1
. Since Algorithm
1
only adds elements to
Result
, we can prove that there exists a rewriting sequence
(q_{0},m_{0})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{1},m_{1})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}\dots\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{k},m_{k})
where
(q,1)=(q_{0},m_{0})
and
(q^{*},m)=(q_{k},m_{k})
by induction on the number of iterations of the while-loop of Algorithm
1
before it produces
(q^{*},m)
.

Base case.
If
(q^{*},m)
is in the set returned by Algorithm
1
when the while-loop has not been entered,
(q^{*},m)=(q,1)
and there exists a rewriting sequence as required (with
k=0
).

Induction step.
Assume that every
(q_{i},m_{i})
produced by Algorithm
1
after
n
iterations of the while-loop are such that there exists a rewriting sequence as required.
Let
(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
be added to
Result
by Algorithm
1
in the
n+1
iteration of the while-loop and
(q_{i},m_{i})\in Rew
be the query that corresponds to the for-loop (line
1
) in which
(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
is added. It is sufficient to show that
(q_{i},m_{i})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
to conclude by induction.

•

Let
x_{0}
,
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)
,
mon_{r}
,
(At,mon_{c})
correspond to the foreach-loops (lines
1
,
1
,
1
, and
1
respectively) in which
(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
is added: it is clear that
x_{0}
fulfills the conditions to be selected in step (S1) and we show that
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)
fulfills those to be selected in step (S3) and that step (S3) can choose
At(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
and
mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
such that
At(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\setminus\{A\}=At
and
mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)=v\times mon_{r}\times mon_{c}
.

1.

Since
mon_{r}\in\mathsf{RoleAtRew}(q^{*}_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
, Algorithm
2
outputs a non-empty set. Hence for every
Q(y,x_{0})\in q^{*}_{i}
,
MonSetQ\neq\emptyset
, i.e. there exists
(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. Moreover,
mon_{r}
is one of the products built by selecting one such
m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}
per
Q(y,x_{0})\in q^{*}_{i}
.

2.

Since
(At,mon_{c})\in\mathsf{ConceptAtRew}(q^{*}_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
, Algorithm
3
outputs a non-empty set. Hence for every
C(x_{0})\in q_{i}
,
PairSetC\neq\emptyset
, i.e. there exists
(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{p}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
such that the corresponding
PairSet
is non-empty when added to
PairSetC
. It follows that for every
1\leq i\leq p
,
PairSB_{i}\neq\emptyset
(since otherwise
PairSet
is replaced by
\emptyset
in line
3
). Finally,
PairSB_{i}\neq\emptyset
implies the existence of either
(P\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i},v_{i})\in\mathcal{O}
or of
(\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},o_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. Moreover, each
PairSetC
contains all possible pairs
(At_{C},n_{C}\times m_{C})
that correspond to the choice of some
(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{p}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})
and for
1\leq i\leq p
of either
(P\sqsubseteq P_{i},m_{i})
and
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P_{i}).A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i},v_{i})
or of
(\top\sqsubseteq B_{i},o_{i})
, so
(At,mon_{c})
is one of the pairs built by selecting one such pair per
C(x_{0})\in q_{i}
and aggregating them.

•

It is then easy to check that step (S2) is performed by lines
1
and
1
, step (S4) by line
1
, step (S5) by lines
1
and
1
and step (S6) by line
1
.

It follows that we can obtain
(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
from
(q_{i},m_{i})
by applying steps (S1) to (S6), i.e.
(q_{i},m_{i})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
.

∎

Lemma D.11
.

For every
(q^{*},m)\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
, the size of
(q^{*},m)
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.
Hence the cardinality of
\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
is exponential in
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.

Proof.

For every
(q^{*},m)\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
, since
\mathsf{terms}(q^{*})\subseteq\mathsf{terms}(q)
and the atoms of
q^{*}
use concept and role names that occur in
\mathcal{O}
, the size of
q^{*}
is bounded by
|\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})|\times|\mathsf{terms}(q)|^{2}
. Hence the size of
q^{*}
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
. Moreover, since
m
is a product of variables that occur in
\mathcal{O}
, the size of
m
is linear w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.

It follows that the cardinality of
\{q^{*}\mid(q^{*},m)\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})\}
is exponential in
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
and the cardinality of
\{m\mid(q^{*},m)\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})\}
is exponential in
|\mathcal{O}|
.
Hence the cardinality of
\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
is exponential in
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.
∎

Lemma D.12
.

Algorithm
2
runs in exponential time w.r.t.
|q|
and
|\mathcal{O}|
.

Proof.

Every foreach-loop of Algorithm
2
iterates over a set whose size is exponentially bounded w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.
In line
2
, by Lemma
D.11
, the number of atoms in
q^{*}_{i}
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.
In line
2
, by Theorem
6.4
,
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
can be computed in exponential time hence is of exponential size w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.
In line
2
,
ListMonSets
contains a polynomial number (bounded by the size of
q^{*}_{i}
) of sets
MonSetQ
.
In line
2
, each set
MonSetQ
constructed by the algorithm is a set of monomials from
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
hence is of exponential size w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.
Finally, in line
2
,
Result
is bounded by
\prod_{Q(y,x_{0})\in q^{*}_{i}}|MonSetQ|
where
|q^{*}_{i}|
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
and
|MonSetQ|
is exponential w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
, hence
|Result|
is exponential w.r.t.
|q|
and
|\mathcal{O}|
. ∎

Lemma D.13
.

Algorithm
3
runs in exponential time w.r.t.
|q|
and
|\mathcal{O}|
.

Proof.

Every foreach-loop of Algorithm
3
iterates over a set whose size is exponentially bounded w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.
In line
3
, by Lemma
D.11
, the number of atoms in
q^{*}_{i}
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.
In lines
3
,
3
,
3
,
3
, by Theorem
6.4
,
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
can be computed in exponential time hence is of exponential size w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.
In line
3
, for every
(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{p}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,
p
is bounded by
|\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})|*\mathsf{Card}(\mathcal{O})
, hence is polynomial in
|\mathcal{O}|
.
In line
3
, each
ListPairSBs
constructed by the algorithm is a set of
p
sets for some
(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{p}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, so contains a polynomial number of sets.
In line
3
, for each
PairSB_{i}
constructed by the algorithm,
PairSB_{i}
is a set of pairs that consists of a set of 0 or 1 concept name that occurs in
\mathcal{O}
and a product of one or two monomials that occur in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, so

is exponential w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.
In line

, for each

is bounded by
\prod_{i=1}^{p}|PairSetB_{i}|

is polynomial w.r.t.

and

is exponential w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
, hence
|PairSet|
is exponential w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.
In line

,

is bounded by the number of atoms
C(x_{0})
in

so is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.
In line

, for each
