is bounded by the number of
(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{p}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
so is exponentially bounded w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.
In line
3
,
|Result|
is bounded by
\prod_{C(x_{0})\in q^{*}_{i}}|PairSetC|
where
|q^{*}_{i}|
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
and
|PairSetC|
is exponential w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
, hence
|Result|
is exponential w.r.t.
|q|
and
|\mathcal{O}|
. ∎

Lemma D.14
.

Algorithm
1
runs in exponential time w.r.t.
|q|
and
|\mathcal{O}|
.

Proof.

By Lemma
D.11
,
|\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})|
is exponential in
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
so Algorithm
1
runs the while-loop an exponential number of times w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
. Moreover, at each iteration of the while-loop, every foreach-loop iterates over a set whose size is exponentially bounded w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
and each iteration takes at most exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
:

•

|Rew|
is bounded by
|\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})|
so exponential w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.

•

For each
(q_{i},m_{i})
, by Lemma
D.11

(q_{i},m_{i})
has polynomial size w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
so both the number of variables in
q_{i}
and
|\mathsf{atoms}(q^{*}_{i})|
are polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.

•

By Lemmas
D.12
and
D.13
,
\mathsf{RoleAtRew}(q^{*}_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
and
\mathsf{ConceptAtRew}(q^{*}_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
are executed in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
, hence their results
RoleRew
and
ConceptRew
are of exponential size w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.∎

See
6.17

Proof.

Claim (4) of the theorem follows from Lemmas
D.10
and
D.14
:
\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
can be computed in exponential time in
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
using Algorithm
1
.
Moreover, claim (3) implies claims (1) and (2):

•

Since
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
is positive, by Theorem
5.1
,
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\models q(\vec{a})
iff
\mathcal{P}(q(\vec{a}),\mathcal{O})\neq 0
, and claim (3) implies that
\mathcal{P}(q(\vec{a}),\mathcal{O})\neq 0
iff there exists
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
such that there is a match for the extended version of
q^{*}(\vec{a})
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}
(note that all monomials that occur in
\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
are different from 0 since by construction they are products of variables that annotate
\mathcal{O}
), so iff there exists
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
such that there is a match for
q^{*}(\vec{a})
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

•

\mathcal{P}(q(\vec{a}),\mathcal{O})=\sum_{\mathcal{O}\models(q(\vec{a}),m)}m
so claim (3) implies that
\mathcal{O}\models(q(\vec{a}),m)
iff
m
occurs in
\sum_{(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})}m^{*}\times\Sigma_{o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}(\vec{a})}})}o=\sum_{(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})}\sum_{o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}(\vec{a})}})}m^{*}\times o
.

We thus prove claim (3), i.e. that
\mathcal{P}(q(\vec{a}),\mathcal{O})=\sum_{(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})}m^{*}\times\Sigma_{o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}(\vec{a})}})}o
.
By definition of the provenance of a query and by Theorem
4.5
, it holds that:

\mathcal{P}(q(\vec{a}),\mathcal{O})=\sum_{n\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}({\boldsymbol{q}(\vec{a})})}n
where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
is the canonical model of
\mathcal{O}
and
\boldsymbol{q}(\vec{a})
is the extended version of
q(\vec{a})
.
We thus need to show that

{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}({\boldsymbol{q}(\vec{a})})=\{m^{*}\times o\mid(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O}),o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}(\vec{a})})\}.

[“
\supseteq
”]
Let
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
and let
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}(\vec{a})})
.

•

There is a match
\pi
of
\boldsymbol{q^{*}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}
such that
\pi(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
and
o=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi(t)
.
By definition of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}
, it holds that for every
P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}
:

–

(P(\pi(\vec{t})),\pi(t))\in\mathcal{D}
so
(P(\pi(\vec{t})),\pi(t))\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
;

–

so
\mathcal{O}\models(P(\pi(\vec{t})),\pi(t))
by Theorem
6.4
;

–

so
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(P(\pi(\vec{t})),\pi(t))
by Theorem
4.5
.

It follows that
\pi
is a match of
\boldsymbol{q^{*}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\pi(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
and
o=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi(t)
.

•

Since
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
, there is a rewriting sequence
(q_{0},m_{0})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{1},m_{1})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}\dots\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{k},m_{k})
such that
(q_{0},m_{0})=(q,1)
and
(q_{k},m_{k})=(q^{*},m^{*})
.
Moreover, for every
0\leq i\leq k-1
, there is
m_{i,i+1}
such that
m_{i+1}=m_{i}\times m_{i,i+1}
.

We show that for every
0\leq j\leq k
that there is a match
\pi_{j}
of the extended version
\boldsymbol{q_{j}}
of
q_{j}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\pi_{j}(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
and
\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j}}}\pi_{j}(t)=\prod_{i=j}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}\times o
. The proof is by descending induction on
j
.

Base case:

j=k
,
(q_{k},m_{k})=(q^{*},m^{*})
and
\prod_{i=k}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}\times o=o
. We have shown that there is a match
\pi
of
\boldsymbol{q^{*}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\pi(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
and
o=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi(t)
so we just need to take
\pi_{k}=\pi
.

Induction step.
Assume that the property is true for some
1\leq j\leq k
: There is a match
\pi_{j}
of
\boldsymbol{q_{j}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\pi_{j}(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
and
\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j}}}\pi_{j}(t)=\prod_{i=j}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}\times o
.

We show that the property still holds for
j-1
. Since
(q_{j-1},m_{j-1})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{j},m_{j})
, there is a variable
x_{0}
existentially quantified in
q_{j-1}
such that no atoms of the form
R(x_{0},x_{0})
occur in
q_{j-1}
and there is
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)\in\mathcal{O}
such that
q_{j}
has been obtained from
q_{j-1}
by dropping every atom that contains
x_{0}
, selecting a variable
y_{0}\in V_{p}
and replacing every occurrence
y^{\prime}\in V_{p}
in
q_{j-1}
by
y_{0}
and adding the atoms
\{D(y_{0})\mid D\in At(q_{j-1},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\}
where:

(a)

for every
Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{j-1}
, there exists
(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, and

(b)

for every
C(x_{0})\in q_{j-1}
, there are
p^{C},p^{\prime C}\geq 0
such that
(B^{C}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{C}_{p^{C}}\sqcap B^{\prime C}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime C}_{p^{\prime C}}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, for every
1\leq i\leq p^{C}
, there exist
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P^{C}_{i}).A^{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq B^{C}_{i},v^{C}_{i})\in\mathcal{O}
and
(P\sqsubseteq P^{C}_{i},m^{C}_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, and for every
1\leq i\leq p^{\prime C}
there exists
(\top\sqsubseteq B^{C}_{i},o^{C}_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, and if we let
m_{C}=\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}(v^{C}_{i}\times m^{C}_{i})\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{\prime C}}o^{C}_{i}
,

m_{j-1,j}=mon(q_{j-1},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)=v\times\prod_{Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{j-1}}m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}\times\prod_{C(x_{0})\in q_{j-1}}m_{C}\times n_{C}.

First note that since
\pi_{j}
is a match of
\boldsymbol{q_{j}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
and for every
D\in At(q_{j-1},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
,
D(y_{0},t_{D})\in\boldsymbol{q_{j}}
(where
t_{D}
is an existentially quantified variable that does not occur anywhere else), it holds that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(D(\pi_{j}(y_{0})),\pi_{j}(t_{D}))
for every
D\in At(q_{j-1},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
.
Since
A\in At(q_{j-1},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
and
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)\in\mathcal{O}
it follows that there exists
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
such that
(\pi_{j}(y_{0}),e,\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v)\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
.

Let
P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j-1}}
.

–

If
P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j}}
,
\pi_{j}(\vec{t},t)\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
by definition of
\pi_{j}
.

–

Otherwise, if
P(\vec{t},t)\notin\boldsymbol{q_{j}}
, either
P(\vec{t},t)=Q(y,x_{0},t)
for some
Q\in{\sf N_{R}}
and
y\in V_{p}
, or
P(\vec{t},t)=C(x_{0},t)
for some
C\in{\sf N_{C}}
.

*

In the first case,
Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{j-1}
so there is
(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

By Lemma
D.1
, since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}
and
(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})
is an RI,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})
.

Hence, since
(\pi_{j}(y_{0}),e,\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v)\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
, it holds that
(\pi_{j}(y_{0}),e,\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v\times m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})\in Q^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
.

*

In the second case,
C(x_{0})\in q_{j-1}
so there are
p^{C},p^{\prime C}\geq 0
such that
(B^{C}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{C}_{p^{C}}\sqcap B^{\prime C}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime C}_{p^{\prime C}}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, for every
1\leq i\leq p^{C}
, there exist
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P^{C}_{i}).A^{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq B^{C}_{i},v^{C}_{i})\in\mathcal{O}
and
(P\sqsubseteq P^{C}_{i},m^{C}_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, and for every
1\leq i\leq p^{\prime C}
there exists
(\top\sqsubseteq B^{C}_{i},o^{C}_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
.

By Lemma
D.1
, since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
is a model of
\mathcal{O}
and
all annotations of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
are monomials
, it holds that,

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(B^{C}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{C}_{p^{C}}\sqcap B^{\prime C}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime C}_{p^{\prime C}}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})
, for
1\leq i\leq p^{C}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P^{C}_{i}).A^{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq B^{C}_{i},v^{C}_{i})
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(P\sqsubseteq P^{C}_{i},m^{C}_{i})
, and for every
1\leq i\leq p^{\prime C}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(\top\sqsubseteq B^{C}_{i},o^{C}_{i})
.

·

For
1\leq i\leq p^{C}
,
(\pi_{j}(y_{0}),e,\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v\times m^{C}_{i})\in(P^{C}_{i})^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
and since
A^{C}_{i}\in At(q_{j-1},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
,
A^{C}_{i}(y_{0},t_{A^{C}_{i}})\in\boldsymbol{q_{j}}
so
(\pi_{j}(y_{0}),\pi_{j}(t_{A^{C}_{i}}))\in(A^{C}_{i})^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
, thus
(e,\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v\times m^{C}_{i}\times v^{C}_{i}\times\pi_{j}(t_{A^{C}_{i}}))\in(B^{C}_{i})^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
.

·

For
1\leq i\leq p^{\prime C}
,
(e,o^{C}_{i})\in(B^{C}_{i})^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
.

It follows that
(e,\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}(m^{C}_{i}\times v^{C}_{i}\times\pi_{j}(t_{A^{C}_{i}}))\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{\prime C}}o^{C}_{i}\times n_{C})\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
, i.e.
(e,\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v\times m_{C}\times n_{C}\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}\pi_{j}(t_{A^{C}_{i}}))\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
(with
m_{C}=\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}(m^{C}_{i}\times v^{C}_{i})\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{\prime C}}o^{C}_{i}
) .

Define
\pi_{j-1}
as follows:

*

\pi_{j-1}(x_{0})=e
,

*

\pi_{j-1}(y)=\pi_{j}(y_{0})
for every
y\in V_{p}
,

*

\pi_{j-1}(x)=\pi_{j}(x)
for every other variable
x
of
q_{j-1}
,

*

\pi_{j-1}(t)=\pi_{j}(t)
for every
P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j}}\cap\boldsymbol{q_{j-1}}
,

*

\pi_{j-1}(t)=\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v\times m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}
for
t
such that
Q(y,x_{0},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j-1}}
and

*

\pi_{j-1}(t)=\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v\times m_{C}\times n_{C}\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}\pi_{j}(t_{A^{C}_{i}})
for
t
such that
C(x_{0},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j-1}}
.

\pi_{j-1}
is a match of
\boldsymbol{q_{j-1}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\pi_{j-1}(\vec{x})=\pi_{j}(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
(since
x_{0}\notin\vec{x}
as it is an existentially quantified variable, and for every
y\in\vec{x}\cap V_{p}
,
y
is replaced by
y_{0}
in
q_{j}
) and

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j-1}}}\pi_{j-1}(t)=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j-1}}\cap\boldsymbol{q_{j}}}\pi_{j}(t)\times\prod_{Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{j-1}}\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v\times m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{C(x_{0})\in q_{j-1}}\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times v\times m_{C}\times n_{C}\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}\pi_{j}(t_{A^{C}_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j-1}}\cap\boldsymbol{q_{j}}}\pi_{j}(t)\times\pi_{j}(t_{A})\times\prod_{C(x_{0})\in q_{j-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}\pi_{j}(t_{A^{C}_{i}})

\displaystyle\times v\times\prod_{Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{j-1}}m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}\times\prod_{C(x_{0})\in q_{j-1}}m_{C}\times n_{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j}}}\pi_{j}(t)\times v\times\prod_{Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{j-1}}m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}\times\prod_{C(x_{0})\in q_{j-1}}m_{C}\times n_{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{j}}}\pi_{j}(t)\times m_{j-1,j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=j}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}\times o\times m_{j-1,j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=j-1}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}\times o.

We conclude that there exists a match
\pi_{0}
of
\boldsymbol{q_{0}}=\boldsymbol{q}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\pi_{0}(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
and
\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi_{0}(t)=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}\times o
. Since
m_{0}=1
,
\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}=m_{0}\times\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}
so since
m_{i+1}=m_{i}\times m_{i,i+1}
for every
0\leq i\leq k-1
,
\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}=m_{k}=m^{*}
.
Hence
\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi_{0}(t)=m^{*}\times o
.

We have thus shown that for every
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
and
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}(\vec{a})})
,
m^{*}\times o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}({\boldsymbol{q}(\vec{a})})
, i.e.
{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}({\boldsymbol{q}(\vec{a})})\supseteq\{m^{*}\times o\mid(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O}),o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}(\vec{a})})\}.

[“
\subseteq
”]
In the other direction, let
n\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}({\boldsymbol{q}(\vec{a})})
: There is a match
\pi
of
\boldsymbol{q}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\pi(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
and
n=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi(t)
.

•

Let
V_{an}
be the set of variables of
\boldsymbol{q}
that are mapped by
\pi
to anonymous individuals from
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}\setminus{\sf N_{I}}
. Since
\pi(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
and
c^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}=c
for every
c\in{\sf N_{I}}
, all variables in
V_{an}
are existentially quantified in
q
. Moreover, the anonymous part of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
is tree-shaped, so for every
x\in V_{an}
, there are no atoms of the form
R(x,x)
in
q
.

•

We define a total order
\succ
over variables from
V_{an}
as follows. Considering a fixed sequence of applications of the chase rule that constructs
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we define
y\succ y^{\prime}
iff
\pi(y)
has been introduced before
\pi(y^{\prime})
.

•

We build a rewriting sequence
(q_{0},m_{0})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{1},m_{1})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}\dots\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{k},m_{k})
together with a sequence
\pi_{0}=\pi,\pi_{1}\dots,\pi_{k}=\pi^{*}
such that:

1.

(q_{0},m_{0})=(q,1)
,
(q_{k},m_{k})=(q^{*},m^{*})
and
\mathsf{terms}(q^{*})\cap V_{an}=\emptyset
;

2.

each
\pi_{i}
is a match for
\boldsymbol{q_{i}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\pi_{i}
coincides with
\pi
on their common domain;

3.

\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi(t)=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi^{*}(t)\times m^{*}
.

The construction by induction ensures that (1)
\mathsf{terms}(q_{i+1})\cap V_{an}\subsetneq\mathsf{terms}(q_{i})\cap V_{an}
, (2)
\pi_{i+1}
is a match for
\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\pi_{i+1}
coincides with
\pi
on their common domain, and (3)
\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}}\pi_{i+1}(t)\times m_{i,i+1}=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\pi_{i}(t)
for some
m_{i,i+1}
such that
m_{i+1}=m_{i}\times m_{i,i+1}
.

For
i\geq 0
, assuming that
(q_{i},m_{i})
and
\pi_{i}
are constructed, we obtain
(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
and
\pi_{i+1}
as follows:

(S1)

Choose
x_{0}
such that
x_{0}
is the least element in
V_{an}\cap\mathsf{terms}(q_{i})
w.r.t.
\succ
(i.e.
\pi_{i}(x_{0})=\pi(x_{0})
has been introduced last in the construction of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
).

(S2)

Replace each role atom of the form
R(x_{0},y)
in
q_{i}
, where
y
and
R
are arbitrary, by the atom
\mathsf{inv}(R)(y,x_{0})
.

(S3)

Let
V_{p}=\{y\mid Q(y,x_{0})\in q\text{ for some }Q\}
.
By construction of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
\pi_{i}(x_{0})
has been introduced by applying the chase rule with some
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)\in\mathcal{O}
(recall that
\mathcal{O}
is in normal form, so
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
) and some
(e_{0},m_{A})\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
.

Let
At(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)=\{A\}
and
mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)=v
.

*

For every
Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{i}
,
Q(y,x_{0},t_{Q})\in\boldsymbol{q_{i}}
and since
\pi_{i}
is a match for
\boldsymbol{q_{i}}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
(\pi_{i}(y),\pi_{i}(x_{0}),\pi_{i}(t_{Q}))\in Q^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
.

Since
\pi_{i}(x_{0})
is least w.r.t.
\succ
in
V_{an}\cap\mathsf{terms}(q_{i})
, it has been introduced after
\pi_{i}(y)
. By construction of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
(since there is no loop in the anonymous part of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
), this implies that
\pi_{i}(y)=e_{0}
.

By Lemma
D.3
, there exists
(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
such that

\pi_{i}(t_{Q})=m_{A}\times v\times m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}.

Update
mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\leftarrow mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\times m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}
.

*

For every
C(x_{0})\in q_{i}
,
C(x_{0},t_{C})\in\boldsymbol{q_{i}}
and since
\pi_{i}
is a match for
\boldsymbol{q_{i}}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
(\pi_{i}(x_{0}),\pi_{i}(t_{C}))\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
.

By Lemma
D.6
,
here are
p^{C},p^{\prime C}\geq 0
such that

·

for every
1\leq i\leq p^{C}
, there exist
(\exists\mathsf{inv}(P^{C}_{i}).A^{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq B^{C}_{i},v^{C}_{i})\in\mathcal{O}
and
(P\sqsubseteq P^{C}_{i},m^{C}_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,

·

for every
1\leq i\leq p^{\prime C}
, there exists
(\top\sqsubseteq B^{\prime C}_{i},o^{C}_{i})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,

·

(B^{C}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{C}_{p^{C}}\sqcap B^{\prime C}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime C}_{p^{\prime C}}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
,

·

for every
1\leq i\leq p^{C}
there is some
(e_{0},m_{A^{C}_{i}})\in(A^{C}_{i})^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
,

and if we let
m_{C}=\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}m^{C}_{i}\times v^{C}_{i}\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{\prime C}}o^{C}_{i}
, then

\displaystyle\text{if $p^{C}\neq 0$: }\quad\quad\pi_{i}(t_{C})=

\displaystyle m_{A}\times v\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}(m^{C}_{i}\times v^{C}_{i}\times m_{A^{C}_{i}})\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{\prime C}}o^{C}_{i}\times n_{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{A}\times v\times m_{C}\times n_{C}\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}m_{A^{C}_{i}}

\displaystyle\text{and if $p^{C}=0$: }\quad\quad\pi_{i}(t_{C})=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{p^{\prime C}}o^{C}_{i}\times n_{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{C}\times n_{C}.

Update
At(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\leftarrow At(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\cup\{A^{C}_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq p^{C}\}
and
mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\leftarrow mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\times m_{C}\times n_{C}
.

(S4)

Drop from
q_{i}
every atom that contains
x_{0}
.

(S5)

Select a variable
y_{0}\in V_{p}
and replace every occurrence of
y^{\prime}\in V_{p}
in
q_{i}
by
y_{0}
(recall that
\pi_{i}
maps all these variable to
e_{0}
).

(S6)

Add the atoms
\{D(y_{0})\mid D\in At(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\}
to
q_{i}
and multiply
m_{i}
by

m_{i,i+1}=mon(q_{i},x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)=v\times\prod_{Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{i}}m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}\times\prod_{C(x_{0})\in q_{i}}m_{C}\times n_{C}.

Moreover, define
\pi_{i+1}
as follows:
\pi_{i+1}
coincides with
\pi_{i}
hence with
\pi
on their common domain, and for every
D\in At(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)
,
\pi_{i+1}(t_{D})=m_{D}
for
t_{D}
the provenance variable that occurs in
D(y_{0},t_{D})\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}
(recall that
(e_{0},m_{D})\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
with
e_{0}=\pi_{i}(y)
for every
y\in V_{p}
).

We have that (1)
\mathsf{terms}(q_{i+1})\cap V_{an}\subsetneq\mathsf{terms}(q_{i})\cap V_{an}
since
x_{0}
has been removed, (2)
\pi_{i+1}
is a match for
\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
that coincides with
\pi
on their common domain, and

\displaystyle(3)\ \prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}}

\displaystyle\pi_{i+1}(t)\times m_{i,i+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}\cap\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\pi_{i+1}(t)\times\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}\setminus\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\pi_{i+1}(t)\times m_{i,i+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}\cap\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\pi_{i}(t)\times\pi_{i+1}(t_{A})\times\prod_{D\in At(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\setminus\{A\}}\pi_{i+1}(t_{D})\times m_{i,i+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}\cap\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\pi_{i}(t)\times m_{A}\times\prod_{D\in At(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\setminus\{A\}}m_{D}\times m_{i,i+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}\cap\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\pi_{i}(t)\times m_{A}\times\prod_{D\in At(q,x_{0},A\sqsubseteq\exists P)\setminus\{A\}}m_{D}

\displaystyle\times v\times\prod_{Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{i}}m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}\times\prod_{C(x_{0})\in q_{i}}m_{C}\times n_{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}\cap\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\pi_{i}(t)\times\prod_{Q(y,x_{0})\in q_{i}}m_{A}\times v\times m_{P\sqsubseteq Q}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{C(x_{0})\in q_{i}}m_{A}\times v\times m_{C}\times n_{C}\times\prod_{i=1}^{p^{C}}m_{A^{C}_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i+1}}\cap\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\pi_{i}(t)\times\prod_{Q(y,x_{0},t_{Q})\in\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\ \pi_{i}(t_{Q})\times\prod_{C(x_{0},t_{C})\in\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\ \pi_{i}(t_{C})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{i}}}\pi_{i}(t).

The rewriting sequence ends when
q^{*}
does not contain any variable from
V_{an}
and we obtain a match
\pi^{*}
for
\boldsymbol{q^{*}}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
that coincides with
\pi
on their shared domain (in particular, on all variables of
q^{*}
) and such that
\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi^{*}(t)\times m^{*}=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi^{*}(t)\times m_{0}\times\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{k}}}\pi_{k}(t)\times\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}m_{i,i+1}=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q_{0}}}\pi(t)=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi(t)
.

•

By definition of
V_{an}
,
\pi^{*}
maps every variable in
q^{*}
to an individual from
{\sf N_{I}}
. Hence, for every
P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}
:

–

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(P(\pi^{*}(\vec{t})),\pi^{*}(t))
;

–

so
\mathcal{O}\models(P(\pi^{*}(\vec{t})),\pi^{*}(t))
by Theorem
4.5
;

–

we have two cases to consider:

*

if
\pi^{*}(\vec{t})\subseteq\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, then
(P(\pi^{*}(\vec{t})),\pi^{*}(t))\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
by Theorem
6.4
,

*

otherwise, it must be the case that (i)
(P(\pi^{*}(\vec{t})),\pi^{*}(t))
is of the form
(A(c),n)
with
c\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
(since by construction of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
, role interpretations in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
cannot contain any pair of individual names such that one of them is in
{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
), and (ii)
\vec{t}
is existentially quantified (since
q(\vec{a})
does not contain any individual name from
{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
by assumption on
q(\vec{x})
and
\vec{a}
), so by Theorem
6.4
,
(A(a_{\top}),n)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and we can redefine
\pi^{*}
by
\pi^{*}(\vec{t})=(a_{\top})
, so that
(P(\pi^{*}(\vec{t})),\pi^{*}(t))=(A(a_{\top}),n)
is in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
;

–

it follows that
(P(\pi^{*}(\vec{t})),\pi^{*}(t))\in\mathcal{D}
by definition of
\mathcal{D}
.

It follows that
\pi^{*}
is a match of
\boldsymbol{q^{*}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}
such that
\pi^{*}(\vec{x})=\vec{a}
.

•

Let
o=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi^{*}(t)
.
We obtain that
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}(\vec{a})})
and
n=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q}}\pi(t)=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi^{*}(t)\times m^{*}=o\times m^{*}
.

We have thus shown that for every
n\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}({\boldsymbol{q}(\vec{a})})
, there exists
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
and
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}(\vec{a})})
such that
n=o\times m^{*}
, i.e.
{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}({\boldsymbol{q}(\vec{a})})\subseteq\{m^{*}\times o\mid(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O}),o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}(\vec{a})})\}.

∎

Proof of Corollaries
6.18
and
6.19

Corollaries
6.18
and
6.19
are direct consequences of Theorem
6.17
and Lemma
D.15
below.

Lemma D.15
.

For every
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
, if
\mathcal{D}
is the set of annotated assertions in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
:

•

for every
o\in\mathsf{mon}({\sf X})
, one can decide whether
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}})

in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
,

•

computing
\Sigma_{o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}})}o

can be done in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.

Proof.

Let
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
. By Lemma
D.11
the size of
q^{*}
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
, and
by Theorem
6.4
,
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
can be computed in exponential time, so
|\mathcal{D}|
is exponential w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.
Deciding whether
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}})
amounts to deciding whether there is a match
\pi
for
q^{*}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}
such that
o=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi(t)
and
computing
\Sigma_{o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}})}o
amounts to find the matches for
q^{*}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}
.

•

The number of potential matches is exponential w.r.t.
|q^{*}|
and polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{D}|
, hence exponential w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.

•

Each match can be checked in polynomial time w.r.t.
|q^{*}|
and
\mathcal{D}
, hence in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and polynomial time w.r.t.
|q|
.

Thus deciding whether
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}})
and computing
\Sigma_{o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}})}o
can both be done in exponential time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.
∎

See
6.20

Proof.

To decide whether
\mathcal{O}\models(q,m)
, we use the following algorithm.

•

As in the proof of Corollary
6.12
, check that
\mathcal{O}
is satisfiable in polynomial time (if
\mathcal{O}
is not satisfiable, then
\mathcal{O}\models(q,m)
), then compute
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
(with the completion rules modified for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
, cf. Figure
2
) in polynomial time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and exponential time w.r.t.
|m|
.

•

Guess:

1.

a rewriting sequence
(q_{0},m_{0})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{1},m_{1})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}\dots\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{k},m_{k})
such that
(q,1)=(q_{0},m_{0})
,
(q^{*},m^{*})=(q_{k},m_{k})
,

2.

for every
0\leq i\leq k-1
, a certificate that
(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
is obtained from
(q_{i},m_{i})
by applying steps (S1) to (S6) of Definition
6.16
but using
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
instead of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and

3.

a match
\pi
of
q^{*}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}^{|m|}}
such that
m=m^{*}\times\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi(t)
, where
\mathcal{D}^{|m|}
is the set of annotated assertions in
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
.

•

Verify (1) to (3) in polynomial time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.

1.

Since each rewriting step removes a variable from the query,
k\leq|\mathsf{terms}(q)|
, and by Lemma
D.11
, for every
0\leq i\leq k
, the size of
(q_{i},m_{i})
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
.

2.

Since the sizes of
q_{i}
and
q_{i+1}
are polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
, a certificate that
(q_{i},m_{i})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
is also of polynomial size and can be checked in polynomial time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
. Indeed, it consists of a variable
x_{0}
of
q_{i}
, a GCI
(A\sqsubseteq\exists P,v)
of
\mathcal{O}
, and at most one RI
(P\sqsubseteq Q,m_{P\sqsubseteq Q})
of
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
per role atom of
q_{i}
and
5
GCIs and RIs of
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
per concept atom of
q_{i}
(since
p+p^{\prime}\leq 2
in the GCI of the form
(B_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B_{p}\sqcap B^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap\dots\sqcap B^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq C,n_{C})\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
).

3.

Since the size of
q^{*}
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and
|q|
, so is
\pi
, and since
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
is of polynomial size w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and exponential time w.r.t.
|m|
, we can check that
\pi
is indeed a match in polynomial time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
.

Since
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})\subseteq\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
, it is clear that this algorithm is sound. It remains to show that it is complete.
Recall that every annotated axiom that belongs to
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and has at most
|m|
variables belongs to
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
because every annotated axiom added by a rule application has at least as many variables as the premises of the rule.

Assume that
\mathcal{O}\models(q,m)
. Since
q
does not contain any individual name from
{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
, by Theorem
6.17
, there exist
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
and
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}})
such that
m=m^{*}\times o
, where
\mathcal{D}
is the set of annotated assertions in
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. Since
m=m^{*}\times o
,
|o|\leq|m|
and
|m^{*}|\leq|m|
.

Since
o\in{\sf prov}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}}({\boldsymbol{q^{*}}})
, there is a match
\pi
of
q^{*}
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}}
such that
o=\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi(t)
. Since
|o|\leq|m|
,
\pi
is actually a match of
q^{*}
to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{D}^{|m|}}
such that
m=m^{*}\times\prod_{P(\vec{t},t)\in\boldsymbol{q^{*}}}\pi(t)
.

Since
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q,\mathcal{O})
, there is a rewriting sequence
(q_{0},m_{0})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{1},m_{1})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}\dots\rightarrow_{\mathcal{O}}(q_{k},m_{k})
such that
(q,1)=(q_{0},m_{0})
,
(q^{*},m^{*})=(q_{k},m_{k})
and for every
0\leq i\leq k-1
, a certificate that
(q_{i+1},m_{i+1})
is obtained from
(q_{i},m_{i})
by applying steps (S1) to (S6) of Definition
6.16
.
Since
|m^{*}|\leq|m|
, every annotated axiom used in the rewriting sequence from
(q,1)
to
(q^{*},m^{*})
is in
\mathsf{saturate}^{|m|}(\mathcal{O})
.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs for Section
7

See
7.4

Proof.

Since
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
,
\alpha
and
\mathcal{O}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
C.1
, it follows that for every
\mathcal{J}\in{\sf Just_{\mathcal{O}}}(\alpha)
, it holds that
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,\bigwedge_{\beta\in\mathcal{J}}\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta))
. Moreover, since
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
is absorptive,
\bigvee_{\mathcal{J}\in{\sf Just_{\mathcal{O}}}(\alpha)}\ \bigwedge_{\beta\in\mathcal{J}}\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)=\bigvee_{\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}\models\alpha}\bigwedge_{\beta\in\mathcal{M}}\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)
. Indeed, every
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{M}\models\alpha
contains a subset
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
which is a justification. To obtain that
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O})=\bigvee_{\mathcal{J}\in{\sf Just_{\mathcal{O}}}(\alpha)}\ \bigwedge_{\beta\in\mathcal{J}}\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)
, it remains to show that for every subset
V\subseteq{\sf X}
, if
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,\bigwedge_{v\in V}v)
, then there exists
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
such that
\{\lambda_{X}(\beta)\mid\beta\in\mathcal{M}\}=V
and
\mathcal{M}\cup\{\beta\mid(\beta,1)\in\mathcal{O}\}\models\alpha
. Let
V\subseteq{\sf X}
such that
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,\bigwedge_{v\in V}v)
.

If
\alpha
is an assertion, then by Theorem
4.5
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(\alpha,\bigwedge_{v\in V}v)
where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
is the canonical model of
\mathcal{O}
. It follows from the construction of the canonical model of
\mathcal{O}
that if
\mathcal{N}=\{(\beta,x)\mid(\beta,x)\in\mathcal{O},x\in V\cup\{1\}\}
is the subset of
\mathcal{O}
annotated with variables from
V
or
1
, then
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N}}\models(\alpha,\bigwedge_{v\in V}v)
. Hence
\mathcal{N}\models(\alpha,\bigwedge_{v\in V}v)
and by Theorem
5.1
,
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\models\alpha
, where
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=\{\beta\mid(\beta,x)\in\mathcal{N}\}
. We obtain
\mathcal{M}
as required by setting
\mathcal{M}=\{\beta\mid\beta\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime},\lambda_{\sf X}(\beta)\neq 1\}
.

Consider now the case where
\alpha
is a GCI of the form
C\sqsubseteq D
with
C
and
D
basic concepts and
C
satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
, and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side. Since
\sf{PosBool}[{\sf X}]
is
\otimes
-idempotent, by Theorem
4.10
,
\mathcal{O}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,\bigwedge_{v\in V}v)
iff
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}\models(E(a_{0}),\bigwedge_{v\in V}v)
where
\mathcal{T}_{D}=\{(D\sqsubseteq E,1)\}
,

\mathcal{A}_{C}=\{(C(a_{0}),1)\}
if
C\in{\sf N_{C}}
,
and
\mathcal{A}_{C}=\{(P(a_{0},b_{0}),1)\}
if
C=\exists P
,
with
a_{0},b_{0}\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
E\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})
. We can then obtain as above
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
such that
\{\lambda_{X}(\beta)\mid\beta\in\mathcal{M}\}=V
and
\mathcal{M}\cup\{\beta\mid(\beta,1)\in\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}\}\models E(a_{0})
and one can check (using the fact that
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}
is satisfiable because
C
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
) that
\mathcal{M}\cup\{\beta\mid(\beta,1)\in\mathcal{O}\}\models C\sqsubseteq D
.

The case where
\alpha
is a positive RI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
can be handled in the same way, using Theorem
4.11
.
∎

Recall that the completion algorithm to compute provenance in the
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
semiring initializes

\mathcal{S}
as in Section
6.1

and extends it by exhaustively applying the rules in Table
3
, where rule applications are modified to
change
\mathcal{S}
into

\mathcal{S}\Cup\{(\alpha,m)\}:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{S}\cup\{(\alpha,m)\}\text{ if there is no $(\alpha,n)\in\mathcal{S}$}\\
\mathcal{S}\setminus\{(\alpha,n)\}\cup\{(\alpha,m\times n)\}\text{ if $(\alpha,n)\in\mathcal{S}$.}\end{cases}

See
7.7

Proof.

First note that by construction of
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
, there are no two annotated axioms
(\alpha,m)
and
(\alpha,m^{\prime})
(
m\not=m^{\prime}
) in
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
since
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
is initialized in a way that respects this condition and if
(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
, a rule application modifies the annotation of
\alpha
in
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
instead of adding
(\alpha,m^{\prime})
. Hence we need to show that

1.

if
\alpha
is an assertion of the form
A(a)
or
R(a,b)
with
a,b\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
such that
\mathcal{O}\models\alpha
, then

(\alpha,\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O}))\in\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
; and

2.

if
\alpha
is an assertion of the form
A(c)
with
c\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
such that
\mathcal{O}\models\alpha
, then

(A(a_{\top}),\mathcal{P}(A(a_{\top}),\mathcal{O}))\in\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
(since it is easy to see that
\mathcal{P}(A(c),\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{P}(A(a_{\top}),\mathcal{O})
, e.g. using the canonical model of
\mathcal{O}
).

By definition of the addition in
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
, for every assertion
\beta
,
\mathcal{P}(\beta,\mathcal{O})=\{v\mid\exists m,\mathcal{O}\models(\beta,m\times v)\}
.
Hence it is sufficient to check whether for every variable
v
, there exists
m
such that
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m\times v)
(resp.
\mathcal{O}\models(A(a_{\top}),m\times v)
) iff there exists
n
such that
(\alpha,n\times v)\in\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
(resp.
(A(a_{\top}),n\times v)\in\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
).

Let
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
be the set obtained by the completion algorithm of Section
6.1.1
.
By Proposition
7.1
(which states that
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]}
entail the same annotated assertions) and Theorem
6.4
,
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m\times v)
iff
(\alpha,m\times v)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
(resp.
\mathcal{O}\models(A(a_{\top}),m\times v)
iff
(A(a_{\top},m\times v)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
).
We thus only need to show that
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})=\{(\beta,\Pi_{(\beta,o)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})}o)\mid(\beta,o)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})\}
to get the theorem’s result.

•

The initial set
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is the same for the completion algorithm that computes
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and the one that computes
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
, and
\mathcal{S}_{0}=\{(\beta,\Pi_{(\beta,o)\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}o)\mid(\beta,o)\in\mathcal{S}_{0}\}
since there is no two annotated axioms
(\beta,o)
and
(\beta,o^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{S}_{0}
.

•

Moreover, we can apply the completion rules in parallel to obtain
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
and
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
while preserving
\mathcal{S}=\{(\beta,\Pi_{(\beta,o)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}o)\mid(\beta,o)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\}
where
\mathcal{S}
is an intermediate step in the computation of
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is an intermediate step in the computation of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. Let
\mathsf{CR}
be a rule applicable to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and assume that
\mathcal{S}=\{(\beta,\Pi_{(\beta,o)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}o)\mid(\beta,o)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\}
before the application of
\mathsf{CR}
.

First apply
\mathsf{CR}
to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
as follows:

–

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
be the axioms in the premises of
\mathsf{CR}
and
\beta
the axiom in its consequence.

–

For every
1\leq i\leq n
, let
mon(\alpha_{i})=\{o\mid(\alpha_{i},o)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\}
and apply
\mathsf{CR}
to every
\{(\alpha_{1},o_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{n},o_{n})\}
where
o_{i}\in mon(\alpha_{i})
for each
i
. After these applications,
\{(\beta,\Pi_{i=1}^{n}o_{i})\mid o_{i}\in mon(\alpha_{i}),1\leq i\leq n\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Then try to apply
\mathsf{CR}
to
\mathcal{S}
.

–

If
\mathsf{CR}
is not applicable to
\mathcal{S}
, since
\{(\alpha_{1},\Pi_{o_{1}\in mon(\alpha_{1})}o_{1})\dots,(\alpha_{n},\Pi_{o_{n}\in mon(\alpha_{n})}o_{n})\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
, this means that
\mathcal{S}
already contains
\beta
annotated with
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\Pi_{o_{i}\in mon(\alpha_{i})}o_{i}
.

–

Otherwise,
\mathsf{CR}
is applicable to
\mathcal{S}
and either adds to
\mathcal{S}
a new annotated axiom
(\beta,\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\Pi_{o_{i}\in mon(\alpha_{i})}o_{i})
, or updates
(\beta,n)\in\mathcal{S}
by adding to
n
the variables from
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\Pi_{o_{i}\in mon(\alpha_{i})}o_{i}
that do not already belong to it.

In both cases, after applying
\mathsf{CR}
in this way,
\mathcal{S}=\{(\beta,\Pi_{(\beta,o)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}o)\mid(\beta,o)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\}
.

Hence,

\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})=\{(\beta,\Pi_{(\beta,o)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})}o)\mid(\beta,o)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})\}.

∎

See
7.8

Proof.

We show that the saturated set
\mathsf{linsat}(\mathcal{O})
is computed in exponential time (resp. polynomial time if the ontology belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
). The theorem then follows from Theorem
7.7
when
\alpha
is an assertion. When
\alpha
is a GCI or an RI, one first need to check whether its left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
in exponential time (resp. polynomial time if the ontology belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
). If it is unsatisfiable,
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O})=1
. Otherwise, we use the polynomial reduction from annotated GCI or RI entailment to annotated assertion entailment in
\otimes
-idempotent semirings (Theorems
4.10
and
4.11
) to obtain
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
and an assertion
\beta
such that
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
iff
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\models(\beta,m)
, so that
\mathcal{P}(\alpha,\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{P}(\beta,\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
.

Each rule application either adds a new axiom or adds a variable to the axiom annotation in
\mathcal{S}
. As the number of variables is linear in
\mathcal{O}
, the total number of rule applications is linearly bounded by the number of (non-annotated) axioms that may be added.

It follows from the proofs of Theorems
6.4
and
6.11
that this number of axioms is exponential in the size of
\mathcal{O}
for the general
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
case, and polynomial in the size of
\mathcal{O}
in the case of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
.
∎

See
7.10

Proof.

We first consider the case where
\alpha
is an assertion. By Theorem
4.5
, for every
m\in\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
,
\mathcal{O}\models(\alpha,m)
iff
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(\alpha,m)
, where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
is the canonical model of
\mathcal{O}
.
It follows that
\gamma\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is relevant to entail
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
iff there exists a monomial
m
such that
\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)
occurs in
m
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}\models(\alpha,m)
.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
be the (classical) interpretation defined by
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
,
a^{\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}=a^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}
for every
a\in{\sf N_{I}}
, and for every
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
and
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
:

•

A^{\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}=\{e\mid(e,n)\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}\}
,

•

{A^{\gamma}}^{\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}=\{e\mid(e,n)\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}},\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)\text{ occurs in }n\}
,

•

R^{\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}=\{(d,e)\mid(d,e,n)\in R^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}}\}
, and

•

{R^{\gamma}}^{\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}=\{(d,e)\mid(d,e,n)\in R^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}},\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)\text{ occurs in }n\}
.

By construction of
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
,
\gamma
is relevant to entail
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
iff
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\models\alpha^{\gamma}
.
We show that
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
is the canonical model of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\cup{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}\cup\{\gamma^{\gamma}\}
, so that
\gamma
is relevant to entail
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}

iff
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\cup{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}\cup\{\gamma^{\gamma}\}\models\alpha^{\gamma}
, i.e.
\gamma
is usable to derive
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{I}_{n}
and let
\mathcal{J}_{n}
be defined from
\mathcal{I}_{n}
as
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
is defined from
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
First, one can easily show (using structural induction in the case of GCI and the fact that GCIs and RIs in
{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}
are such that their left-hand sides all contain a concept or role name of the form
A^{\gamma}
or
R^{\gamma}
) that:

•

for every GCI
C\sqsubseteq D\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
,
{C}^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}=\{d\mid(d,n)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\}
,

•

for every GCI
C^{\prime}\sqsubseteq D^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}
,
{C^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}=\{d\mid(d,n)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}},\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)\text{ occurs in }n\}
,

•

for every RI
P\sqsubseteq Q\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
,
{P}^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}=\{(d,e)\mid(d,e,n)\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\}
,

•

for every RI
P^{\prime}\sqsubseteq Q^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}
,
{P^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}=\{(d,e)\mid(d,e,n)\in P^{\mathcal{I}_{n}},\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)\text{ occurs in }n\}
.

It is easy to see that
\mathcal{J}_{0}
is the interpretation that satisfies exactly the assertions in
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\cup{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}\cup\{\gamma^{\gamma}\}
(note that they are exactly the assertions in
\mathcal{O}
, plus
\gamma^{\gamma}
in the case where
\gamma
is an assertion, and that in this case,
\mathcal{J}_{0}\models\gamma^{\gamma}
since
\mathcal{I}_{0}\models(\gamma,\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma))
). Then each application of the chase rule from
\mathcal{I}_{n}
to
\mathcal{I}_{n+1}
that uses some
(e,n)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}
and
(C\sqsubseteq D,v)\in\mathcal{O}
and yields
(e,n\times v)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}
corresponds to the following (possibly multiple) applications of the chase rule that build
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}
from
\mathcal{J}_{n}
:

•

using
C\sqsubseteq D\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
and
e\in C^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}=\{d\mid(d,o)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\}
, since it is indeed the case that
e\in D^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}=\{d\mid(d,o)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}\}
since
(e,n\times v)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}
,

•

in the case where
\gamma=C\sqsubseteq D
(hence
\lambda_{\sf X}=v
), using
\gamma^{\gamma}=C\sqsubseteq D^{\prime}
(where
D^{\prime}
is obtained by replacing the unique predicate in
D
by its adornment by
\gamma
) and
e\in C^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}=\{d\mid(d,o)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}\}
, since it is indeed the case that
e\in{D^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}=\{d\mid(d,o)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}},\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)\text{ occurs in }o\}
since
(e,n\times v)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}
and
\lambda_{\sf X}=v
,

•

in the case where
\lambda_{\sf X}
occurs in
n
, using every
C^{\prime}\sqsubseteq D^{\prime}\in f^{\gamma}(C\sqsubseteq D)
and
e\in{C^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{J}_{n}}=\{d\mid(d,o)\in C^{\mathcal{I}_{n}},\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)\text{ occurs in }o\}
, since it is indeed the case that
e\in{D^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{J}_{n+1}}=\{d\mid(d,o)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}},\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)\text{ occurs in }o\}
since
(e,n\times v)\in D^{\mathcal{I}_{n+1}}
and
\lambda_{\sf X}
occurs in
n
,

and similarly for application of the chase rule using some RI. Hence
\mathcal{J}_{n+1}
is obtained from
\mathcal{J}_{n}
by applying one or several chase rules using axioms of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\cup{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}\cup\{\gamma^{\gamma}\}
. Moreover, no rule is applied twice with the same axiom and tuple, and the rule application is fair since every rule applicable in
\mathcal{J}_{n}
corresponds to a rule applicable in
\mathcal{I}_{n}
and will thus eventually be applied. We obtain that
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
is indeed the canonical model of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\cup{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}^{\gamma}\cup\{\gamma^{\gamma}\}
.

We now consider the case where
\alpha
is a GCI of the form
C\sqsubseteq D
with
C
and
D
basic concepts such that
C
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}
does not contain any GCI with
\top
as left-hand side. Since
\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
is
\otimes
-idempotent, by Theorem
4.10
, for every
m\in\sf{Lin}[{\sf X}]
,
\mathcal{O}\models(C\sqsubseteq D,m)
iff
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}\models(E(a_{0}),m)
where
\mathcal{T}_{D}=\{(D\sqsubseteq E,1)\}
,

\mathcal{A}_{C}=\{(C(a_{0}),1)\}
if
C\in{\sf N_{C}}
,
and
\mathcal{A}_{C}=\{(P(a_{0},b_{0}),1)\}
if
C=\exists P
,
with
a_{0},b_{0}\in{\sf N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{O})
and
E\in{\sf N_{C}}\setminus\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})
. Hence,
\gamma\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is relevant to entail
\alpha
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
iff there exists a monomial
m
such that
\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)
occurs in
m
and
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}\models(E(a_{0}),m)
, i.e., iff
\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)
is relevant to entail
E(a_{0})
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}
. It is easy to check that replacing the annotation
1
by some fresh variables in
\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}
, so that
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}
fulfills the conditions of the proposition (note that since
C
is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}
is satisfiable), has no impact on whether
\lambda_{\sf X}(\gamma)
is relevant to entail
E(a_{0})
. Hence, using the proposition in the assertion case,
\gamma
is relevant to entail
E(a_{0})
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}
iff it is usable to derive
E(a_{0})
w.r.t. the non-annotated version
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{C}
. Finally, one can check that
\gamma
is usable to derive
E(a_{0})
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{C}
iff it is usable to derive
C\sqsubseteq D
w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

The case where
\alpha
is a positive RI whose left-hand side is satisfiable w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}
can be handled in the same way, using Theorem
4.11
.
∎

Appendix F
Proof of Theorem
2.2
(Complexity of
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
)

The proof of Theorem
2.2
relies on the algorithms we develop for computing the
\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]
-
provenance of assertions and queries w.r.t.
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
ontologies in Section
6.1.2
.

See
2.2

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a (non-annotated) ontology that belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
,
and let
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
be the ontology obtained by applying exhaustively the saturation rules for
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
defined in Section
6.1.2
, while ignoring the monomials part of the rules.

•

We have shown in the proof of Corollary
6.12
that
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
can be built in polynomial time w.r.t.
|\mathcal{O}|
and contains some
\bot(a)
iff
\mathcal{O}
is unsatisfiable, so that satisfiability in
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
is in
PTime
.

•

Assertion entailment can be reduced to satisfiability in polynomial time as follows:
\mathcal{O}\models A(a)
iff
\mathcal{O}\cup\{A\sqcap B\sqsubseteq\bot,B(a)\}
is unsatisfiable where
B\notin\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})
, and
\mathcal{O}\models R(a,b)
iff
\mathcal{O}\cup\{R\sqcap S\sqsubseteq\bot,S(a,b)\}
is unsatisfiable where
S\notin\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})
. Note that in both cases the modified ontologies belong to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
.
Hence assertion entailment is in
PTime
.

•

RI entailment can be reduced to role assertion entailment in polynomial time as follows:
\mathcal{O}\models P_{1}\sqsubseteq P_{2}
iff
\mathcal{O}\cup\{P_{1}(a_{0},b_{0})\}\models P_{2}(a_{0},b_{0})
where
a_{0},b_{0}
are fresh individual names. Hence RI entailment is in
PTime
.

•

GCI entailment can be reduced to concept assertion entailment in polynomial time.
Given a GCI
C\sqsubseteq D
,
let
\mathcal{T}_{D}=\emptyset
if
D=\bot
and
\mathcal{T}_{D}
be the set of GCIs obtained from the normalization of
D\sqsubseteq E
otherwise, where
E\notin\mathsf{sig}(\mathcal{O})
. Note that
\mathcal{T}_{D}
belongs to
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n,-}_{\bot}
since for any
{\cal E\!LHI}^{n}_{\bot}
concept
D
, the normalization of
D\sqsubseteq E
will only produce GCIs with concept names in the right-hand side.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{C}=f(C,a_{0})
where
a_{0}
is an individual name that does not occur in
\mathcal{O}
and
f
is the function inductively defined as
follows, where all constants introduced are fresh:

–

f(\top,a)=\emptyset
,

–

f(A,a)=\{A(a)\}
if
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
,

–

f(\exists R.B,a)=\{R(a,b)\}\cup f(B,b)
,

–

f(\exists R^{-}.B,a)=\{R(b,a)\}\cup f(B,b)
,

–

f(B\sqcap B^{\prime},a)=f(B,a)\cup f(B^{\prime},a)
.

We can show that

\mathcal{O}\models C\sqsubseteq D
iff
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{T}_{D}\cup\mathcal{A}_{C}\models E(a_{0})
.
Hence GCI entailment is in
PTime
.

•

For BCQ entailment, we adapt the rewriting algorithm (Definition
6.16
) so that it does not take into account monomials and uses
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
instead of
\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})
. The proof is then similar to the one for Theorem
6.20
: We obtain a
NP
-upper bound by guessing a rewriting
q^{*}
together with a rewriting sequence and a match for
q^{*}
in
\mathcal{D}
. Since
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\{\alpha\mid(\alpha,m)\in\mathsf{saturate}(\mathcal{O})\}
, it is easy to check that
q^{*}
can be obtained by the adapted rewriting algorithm iff there exists
(q^{*},m^{*})\in\mathsf{Rew}(q^{*},\mathcal{O}^{\sf{Why}[{\sf X}]})
, and that the BCQ entailment algorithm is correct.

NP
-hardness of BCQ entailment already holds for, e.g., DL-Lite (?, Theorem 44), and
PTime
-hardness of axiom entailment already holds for the language of GCIs of the form
C\sqsubseteq D
with
C:=A\mid A_{1}\sqcap A_{2}
and
D:=A\mid\bot
(?)
(see also (?, Theorem 4.3, case 1), for PTime-hardness of instance checking in the language of GCIs of the form
C\sqsubseteq D
, with
C:=A\mid A_{1}\sqcap A_{2}\mid\exists R.A
and
D:=A
, where
A,A_{1},A_{2}\in{\sf N_{C}}
and
R\in{\sf N_{R}}
).
∎
